# THIRD
### a novel of Anka
**Christopher Aruwa**

---

*Dedicated to my best friend and wife, Liza. Without whose patience this would never have been completed. Also to my best man Eric, for the patience and inspiration for Wally. Lastly, to Zanaimo and her almost sibling ‘Liwon’, who i discovered during this writing as a way to process hir loss. My first completed book since being diagnosed as AuDHD. Thank you to all who put up with pre-diagnosed me who just couldn't understand. Thank you Lord for the blessings herein, and may all those who read this enjoy it as much as I did writing it.*

---

# One

The last customer left when the Scar had wheeled to a hand's-width from full dark, which gave Klar and me just long enough to close before the light went and we'd be finishing by firelight.

"You don't have to stay," I told him — which was true, and also the thing you say when you'd rather not lock up alone.

"I'm not in a hurry." He kept stacking bowls, and didn't make anything of it, which was a kindness. Klar was five thousand spins old — barely grown, by the reckoning we keep, where the Scar turns over you once a day and even a long life runs out somewhere short of twenty thousand. He cooked like a man three lifetimes into it. On the nights I closed alone he always found a reason to still be there, and never said what it was, and I never made him. I wiped the tables. He dealt with the kitchen. The fire burned to coals.

Through the shutters came the militia's boots on the packed earth, the patrol heading out. The Scar was almost across. Thirty minutes, maybe, before I'd want a locked door between me and the dark.

The greybeards call them Titanspawn. The militia call them problems. The rest of us don't call them anything — you don't name the thing that took the Pell family, you just get your ladder up before the Scar finishes crossing and you don't listen too hard to the sounds that come, some nights, off the far side of the wall. After long enough the not-thinking is a skill of its own. I'd had the practice.

Ours was not a remarkable town, and everyone who passed through said as much, kindly enough, and enough of them passed through to make it a fair verdict. The trail linked two larger places, so we got travellers, and travellers meant trade, and trade was the whole reason there was an inn for me to close. Tondi had taken me on as a girl — old enough to carry a stack of bowls without wearing them — back when my father had stopped being someone you could count on to be anywhere in particular. He was alive, last anyone knew. Just elsewhere. I was good at not thinking about that.

"That's me." Klar hung his apron on its hook and squared it so it wouldn't crease, and at the door he stopped. "Bar it after me."

"Go home, Klar."

His feet crossed the yard, the tall gate clattered, and the last living sound went with him, and left me the fire and the dark and the patrol thinning out around the mill.

The table under the window was Kern's. He was the Kro who'd been coming to the inn longer than I'd been closing it — a fixture, like the memorial pillar — in for a single cup most afternoons, which he made last until the light moved. He brought his bow, the way he always did: shorter than you'd expect of something so old, recurved, horn-tipped, no taller than his own chest, carved its whole length too fine to follow, worn pale where his shoulder rode it, old red worked deep in the bindings. A family piece, he'd told me once, when I was small enough to ask rude questions. Virek-made — *may they never be forgotten*; he said the charm the way everyone does, the way you respond to someone who sneezed. Older than the inn. Older than him. A Kro doesn't call a thing old just to be saying something. Roan had asked him once, early on, why he didn't carry a Boltcaster like every other man who'd soldiered — Boltcasters being common as boots, and a good deal less trouble to restring. Kern had looked at him the way you look at someone who has asked a perfectly reasonable question in entirely the wrong room. "The Charter sells the bolt," he'd said. "Not the bow." He hadn't explained further, and Roan hadn't asked twice. I understood it a little better later, when I'd seen more of the world and what the Powder Charter's hand was in all of it — that a man carrying a weapon two hundred thousand spins out of fashion, that answered to no license and no toll and no supplier, was saying something with his shoulder that he'd never once said with his mouth. I'd wiped around it a hundred times and thought it handsome and in my way, that was the whole of what it was to me, then.

I made my round. Six tables, and I knew the wobble in each. The lockbox in the floor — lever down, lid true, the way Tondi had shown me the first night she trusted me with it, kneeling on the boards beside me as if she were teaching me something holy. I straightened chairs that were already straight, which was either professional habit or a way of not going home yet. I knew which.

Through the gap in the shutters, if you stood right, you could see the memorial pillar — knee-high, three-sided, every name the town had buried since its founding. Not a long list. We were young, as towns go. I stood there too long and looked at it in the last of the Scar's light, and felt the quiet come down the way it did some nights, and not as a comfort. I'd been as far as Dunarth once, spireward, on a caravan, and back, and that was the whole of everywhere I'd been that wasn't here. I wasn't unhappy. I was unhappy the way you aren't hungry until the food is carried past you — which holds up fine, right until something changes.

Nothing was going to change. That was the thing about quiet towns.

I banked the fire, took my coat from the peg, spun the lock, and stepped out as the last Scar-light went out of the sky and the true dark of Anka came down — flat and total, the wound turning slow overhead and shedding its black straight down. Far off, the Spire still held its light. I stood at the gate a moment, taking the temperature of out there before I trusted myself to it. Sem's lamp burned over the carpenter's: working late, or just not going to bed, the same thing at his age. The boundary spikes at the town's edge had gone invisible in the dark, which had always struck me as a flaw in the design.

The ordinary kind of dangerous, which in our town meant probably fine.

I was three paces toward home when the sound came from the town entrance — not the patrol. Two of the militia called out, voices pitched a notch high, the way they go when the body decides something before the mind catches up. Then quiet. Then the careful talk of people working out what to do about a thing rather than running from it. In hand, then. I kept walking. Almost believed it.

Halfway home, past the dark bulk of the mill, boots came up behind me — quick, uneven, heavier on the left. Roan's gait; he'd favoured that leg since before either of us was old enough to know why.

"Tasha! Hold up—" A wobble in his voice I didn't like. "Tasha, I need—"

I turned. Roan was coming up the path at the pace that isn't quite running, because running would mean admitting something. And he wasn't alone. Beside him, keeping easy time with a stride that had nothing to do with Roan's hurry, came a shape I had no ready name for: thin, hooded, walking on something that didn't sound like feet on the packed dirt.

I was bad at leaving things alone. It was either a failing or the one interesting thing about me — and with my hand drifting toward my blade, I had the brief, unwelcome feeling I was about to learn which.

*  *  *


# Two

They stopped a few paces short of me — far enough to let me look at the thing without it looming, and looming was clearly something it could do without trying.

Wooden limbs, with just disturbingly too-long forearms, wrapped in something like leather, in the way of something built to suggest muscle without quite managing it. A cloak doing an earnest, unsuccessful job of concealing the wearer, mostly because the wearer had cloven hooves where feet should be, and the sole-less boots someone had pulled over them had long since worn through. And in the shadow of the hood, slightly off-centre, one eye: a hole in smooth wood, lit from somewhere inside, a steady blue. Not facing me — and then it was, the glow finding me, settling, brightening a fraction as it fixed in place.

 Strapped across the back of its waist, only the leather-wrapped handles showing, were a pair of blades i'd later learn were Falkas. Each one as long as a forearm, delicately carved their entire length, leaf-shaped but angled slightly down, and weighted towards the tip. "Better for chopping" I was later told. The cloak hid the hooves badly, the hood didn't hide the eye at all, and the whole of it gave the impression of something that had learned how to be unalarming from a single description, once, and gotten every detail a little, and terribly, wrong. The way a large animal sitting still ans watching you, is worse than one already moving. 

My hand was on my own blade. I left it there.

"They wouldn't let it through the gate on its own," Roan said, in a rush, like he'd been holding the words in his mouth the whole way over. "Course they wouldn't. But it asked — it talks, Tasha — it asked for Tondi by name, and Pek said bring it to whoever's at the inn and let them sort it, so I—" He gestured at the entire situation. "So I brought it. To whoever's at the inn. Which I was hoping would be Tondi."

"Tondi's gone to bed."

"Yeah." His face fell about as far as a face can fall. "I was afraid you'd say that."

The thing's eye stayed on me. The body hadn't moved to follow it — the whole of it as still as the mill behind us, which should have been reassuring and wasn't.

"It wants to come in," Roan added, when the silence had gone on long enough to need something in it. "Into the inn, I mean. Not just the town. That's the part I didn't want to decide on my own."

"I did ask," the thing said.

The voice arrived like the first stones of a rockslide — not loud, not sharp, felt as much as heard, happening in the chest before it reached the ears. I hadn't expected it to speak. I took my hand off my blade — not because I'd decided it was safe, but because I'd decided that if it wasn't, my blade wouldn't be the relevant variable.

"All right," I said, mostly to buy a breath. Then, to Roan, because it was easier than talking to the thing: "What did Tondi tell you to do, exactly? Her words."

He worked at it. "She said — if it's reasonable, there's a condition. For letting it in. She said tell it to bring back the head of the Subsurface Shredder, the one that's been working the trail anti-spinward. Proof it's what it says it is." He glanced at it. "And bring the head to her. Not take its word."

"You heard her," I said to the thing. "Proof. Do you have the Shredder's head?"

"I do."

Then it reached an arm around to its own back — not turning, not rotating at the shoulder the way a person would, the arm just going where arms don't go, at angles that put a sympathetic twinge in my own shoulder — and produced, from somewhere behind itself, the head. Held it out at arm's length, in a manner that suggested it found the object neither interesting nor troubling.

I looked at the head. Squid-like, but with six eyes, each pair a different size, all of them clouded now. The skin pale past the pallor of death — a thing that had lived mostly without light. By reputation it ambushed the unwary and was the last thing they ever met. By appearance, now, it was an oversized dead fish. Funny, how much death takes out of a thing.

Roan had the look of a man who's found a puppy and is hoping no one will tell him to put it down.

"Put that away," I said. "Please. And — fine. Come back to the inn. I'm not deciding anything out here in the dark."

It returned the head to wherever it kept such things, with that same wrong, unhurried motion, and said nothing, which I was starting to understand was how it agreed.

So the three of us went to the inn — me in front, because it felt important to be in front; the thing behind me, its tread landing wrong on the packed dirt; Roan at the rear with the air of a man escorting a prisoner he isn't sure is actually his. The town stayed dark and shuttered. If anyone watched, they kept it to themselves.

At the inn I unlocked the gate but didn't open it, and turned to face the thing properly, with the building at my back as though that would help.

"Tondi set the condition," I said, "and Tondi's asleep, and I'm not waking her for this. So. You can't come in until she's seen that head and said you're what you say you are. Her rule, not mine, and I'm not clever enough to overrule it tonight. But you can wait inside the gate. That much is my call."

The eye stayed on me, and the quality of its stillness shifted in a way I couldn't have named — not impatience; nothing so humin as that. More like a sum coming out even.

"Then I will wait," it said, and stepped through the gate, and stood beside the door, and went, as far as I could tell, completely still.

I looked at Roan. Roan looked at the thing.

"You're the one who told it to come here," I said.

"Tondi told me to bring it. If it—"

"Tondi told you to bring the head. She didn't say anything about the rest of it."

He opened his mouth. Closed it.

I checked the lock — combination set, the hexwood bolt sliding home with its reassuring click — and gave Roan the look I hoped said *not my problem*, and left.

Behind me, rounding the mill, I heard him say, at about a third of his usual volume, "Do you need anything? Water, or—" and stop, presumably having remembered what he was talking to.

I didn't hear the answer, if there was one.

*  *  *

At home I sat on the edge of the bed a while without taking my coat off.

The thing had spoken. It had been polite. It had the Shredder's head, which meant the test was passed, or would be once Tondi was awake to call it passed. It had walked to a wall and stood against it like it could do that until the world ended and not mind.

None of which was, technically, alarming.

I took my coat off. Told myself I'd sleep, and was surprised, after a while, to find that I had.

*  *  *


# Three

I found out later what he did while I was asleep. I'm not exactly sure when it stopped being *it* in my head, I only noticed I'd already done it - the way you notice, well after the fact, that you've started calling someone by their first name. 

When I came around the corner of the mill with my coat still half-buttoned and my eyes not fully decided about being open, he was there. Something about the way he was standing — not the stillness, which was always there, but the quality of it — made me stop walking about a dozen paces out.

I stood there for a moment. He stood there for a moment.

His eye found me — the glow settling, one lid dropping fractionally as it fixed on me.

"Did you sleep?" I asked, because I was not yet awake enough for a more sophisticated opening.

There was a bellows-wheeze, slow and considered, from somewhere inside his chest before the avalanche spoke. "I do not need sleep. However, during the dark I did advise someone that attempting a non-front-door entry during non-open hours would not be in their best interest. They then left."

I thought about that for a moment. My heart was doing what it had been doing since I'd turned the corner and seen him — the specific rhythm of a body that hasn't yet caught up to the decision the mind already made.

"If that's the truth," I said, "I think we can offer you something on the house. Or whatever you need for—" I looked at his face, which had no mouth. "—nourishment."

"I require only to be indoors where I do not need to consider night attacks from directions I am not watching." The eye didn't move from me. "Tondi was to arrange this. It appears I have circumvented that requirement by standing watch outside your inn."

"You are," I said, "genuinely resourceful."

I walked the remaining dozen paces and stopped at the door. He came aside in the way he had — the whole body moving as a single considered unit, not the unconscious shuffle of someone making room. He chose to move. That was the thing about Third, I was already beginning to understand. Everything was chosen.

At the lock I asked what else he'd done with the night. He told me in pieces.

He'd spent most of it considering the memorial pillar. Not looking at it — he'd read it before I'd arrived, all the names, the founding plaque worn smooth at the edges. He was considering it from memory, which for Third was not a figure of speech. He had the names of everyone who had ever died in this town stored somewhere in whatever served him for a mind, and had been cross-referencing them against what he knew of the region, the trail, the kinds of things that tended to happen to small settlements within a certain range of certain other things.

I asked him what he'd concluded. He said the town was not in immediate danger. That the kind of threat he was currently occupied with tended toward specific targets rather than casual ones, and this town was not specific enough to be a target yet. That yet was doing a lot of work in that sentence, and he knew it, and I knew he knew it.

I asked him why he hadn't said any of this the night before. He said I hadn't asked. I said that was technically true and also a very annoying answer. He said he knew that too.

He'd stayed at the window the rest of the night, he said — for no reason he could name precisely. The night was the same. The patrol was the same. The town was the same. It had simply seemed the right place to be when morning came, if morning was going to bring what he thought it might.

Once inside, i put away my coat and got him to a seat. "What did you think morning was going to bring?"

He said: someone willing to ask how soon.

"Looks like it might get stony today," I said — the sky had that particular flat quality that usually meant falling rocks by mid-morning.

I started the morning routine — shutters, stove, stew selection — and did not think about the fact that there was a Titanspawn Forged sitting in the corner of my inn wearing its worn cloak, and watching me with one blue eye.

I was not thinking about it very hard.

*  *  *


# Four

I'd got as far as selecting the vegetables for the day's stew when Tondi came down, and that was when the morning stopped going the way mornings usually went.

I should explain about Tondi. She was the town's mender, which meant she stitched clothes and stitched people and was, by a margin nobody disputed, the most capable person in the settlement at both. She had grey hair that she wore loose and falling across her face, which made eye contact difficult, which I had eventually understood was the entire point. She did not touch money — she had a bucket, and you put your Black or your Teeth in the bucket, and that was the transaction. Nobody knew why. Nobody asked. You did not ask Tondi things. Tondi was a curmudgeon of such reliable and total commitment to the craft that the town had simply organised itself around the assumption that she would be unpleasant and correct, in roughly that order, about everything.

She had raised me, more or less, since I was old enough to carry things. I would not have said she was warm. I would have said she was the most dependable fixed point in my life, which is a different thing, and arguably better.

She came down the stairs from her room with the particular shuffle of someone who is awake but has not yet decided to be, took three steps into the main room, and saw Third.

The rocks were just starting to bounce off the roof, a loud plinking off the wood shingles. I have heard Tondi raise her voice perhaps four times in my life. This was one of them.

"Gah! What in the name of the Titans—"

And then she stopped.

The plinking continued, but abated slightly, as if waiting for our mender to gather herself. That was the thing I would think about afterward, when I had time to think about things. The scream was real — she was genuinely, fully startled, a grey-haired woman finding a Titanspawn sitting in the corner of her inn at first light. But the stopping was just as real, and faster than it had any right to be. One breath of pure shock, and then something behind her eyes simply went to work, sorting the impossible thing in the corner into categories, weighing it, and arriving — visibly, in the space of about two heartbeats — at a conclusion.

The conclusion was: this requires information, not panic.

"Tasha," she said, in a voice that had already climbed all the way back down to its normal register. "Did you let that in here."

It was not really a question. I answered it anyway, from the kitchen doorway, with a vegetable still in my hand.

"Morning, Tondi. Yes. It scared off a burglar last night, and it—" I gestured with the vegetable, which I realised partway through was not helping. "It's got the Shredder's head. The one Roan was supposed to ask about. So I figured I'd trust it with a chair."

Tondi looked at me. Then she looked at Third. Then she looked back at me with an expression that communicated, with great economy, that we would be discussing the limits of my judgement at a later date.

Third, to his credit, did the thing he always did when he became the subject of a room's attention, which was nothing. He simply turned his head toward Tondi, and waited.

Then he produced the Shredder's head.

I had seen him do this the night before and it had unsettled me then. In the morning light, with Tondi watching, it was somehow worse — the arm going around to the back without the body turning, the angles wrong, the whole motion carrying the casual competence of something that had been retrieving objects from impossible places for longer than the town had existed. The head came out and was held forth at arm's length, a gruesome offering between two intelligent beings, neither of whom was entirely sure what the correct etiquette was.

"This was what you sought," Third said. The voice did the thing it did — arrived in the chest before the ears. "As I understood it. If it is not sufficient, I will leave."

There was a silence then that I would not wish on anyone. Tondi standing very still. Third holding a decapitated Shredder head at arm's length. Me in the doorway with a vegetable. The fire not yet lit, the room cold, the light coming grey through the just-opened shutters.

The stony had passed. Tondi closed her eyes for a breath. I watched her hands, which had balled into fists at her sides, slowly uncurl. When she opened her eyes again, she had decided something, and Tondi deciding something was a force roughly equivalent to weather.

"Thank you," she said, "for ridding us of that beast." A pause, during which she was clearly choosing her next words with the care of someone disarming a trap. "I can see you're a reasonable... being."

The word being cost her something. I could hear it. But she said it, which was Tondi all over — she would give you the truth even when the truth was inconvenient to her own prejudices, because the alternative was being wrong, and Tondi would rather do almost anything than be wrong.

"That said," she went on, "some of the townsfolk—" She stopped, reconsidered, and corrected herself with the flat honesty that was the closest thing she had to a personality trait. "Most of the townsfolk will not understand. Would you mind setting yourself somewhere more out of the centre of the room. And—" she looked at the cloak, which was doing its usual poor job. "—covering yourself a bit more thoroughly. Scar's sake."

She did not wait for an answer. She walked to the corner she always sat in, the one with the best light for mending, and she picked up the cup of water I put out for her every morning, and she drank from it, and she almost visibly relaxed. Third looked down at its cloak, considering the problem of how to cover himself better. This required the use of an arm, so it set the Shredder's head down on the nearest table — without any apparent concern for the fact that it was a decapitated head on a table where people would later eat — and began trying to arrange the inadequate cloak into something more adequate.

I put the vegetable down and went over to Tondi, not for conversation, which one did not seek with Tondi, but because there was a tablecloth on the pile of her current projects that was past saving, stained and fraying, not worth the Black it would take to mend.

"That old cloth," I said. "It's done, isn't it. Not worth fixing."

She didn't look up. She made a sound that I had learned to interpret, over many spins, as assent delivered grudgingly enough to preserve her dignity.

I took the cloth.

"Here," I said to Third, and draped it over his back and shoulders, on top of the failing cloak. With the tablecloth added, and Third positioned away from the door and out of the direct sightline of anyone walking in, you would have to get fairly close to notice the hooves, or the wrongness of the arms, or the single blue glow where a face should have been.

Klar arrived for his shift just then, which was its own small mercy, because Klar arriving meant the morning had to resume being a morning. I took the Shredder's head off the table before he could see where it had been sitting and handed it to him.

"Customer brought us a trophy," I said. "Think you can do something with it?"

Klar's whole face changed. He had not yet looked up enough to register the Forged Titanspawn sitting in the corner under a tablecloth, because he was only 5,100 spins and a Shredder head was the most interesting thing that had happened to him in a tenday.

"A Shredder head? You don't even—" He was already turning toward the kitchen, already planning. "Let me get a skewer."

And he was gone.

I looked at Third, in his corner, under his tablecloth, with his one blue eye, watching the room with the patience of something that had decided this was an acceptable place to be and had no particular feelings about how long it took. I looked at Tondi, mending, her water at her elbow, behaving as though there were not a Titanspawn in her inn.

Then I went and lit the stove, because the stew was not going to make itself, and because I had apparently decided, somewhere in the last few hours, that this was simply how the morning was going to go.

I did not examine that decision very closely either.

*  *  *


# Five

The day did what days do. Customers came for the stew and left having eaten it; the Scar crawled its slow arc overhead; Tondi mended and grumbled and eventually went up to rest; and through all of it the thing in the corner sat under its tablecloth and did not move, except to adjust the Falkas on its back rarely, until the light began to fail and the inn emptied to nothing and I found that I had run out of reasons not to go and sit with it.

So I brought my stew, just in case. The tablecloth had done a passable job of hiding the worst of it, but up close, where I was sitting, there was no pretending the thing across the corner table from me was anything other than what it was.  The wooden hand with its too-many joints resting on the table's edge. The faint sound, every so often, of something mechanical inside a chest that didn't move. And the eye — one blue eye, set slightly off-centre in a smooth featureless face, which had not so much looked at me as found me, the glow of it fixing in place with a weight I could almost feel.

I'd served stranger, maybe. Tondi, for one.

What I kept coming back to was the weapon. Slung across its back, under the failing cloak, was a Boltcaster — a real one, a lightning-gun, the kind the militia talked about the way some men talk about women they'll never meet. Ours had exactly none. And here was one riding the back of a Titanspawn that fought with curved wooden blades it kept in its hands, while the Boltcaster stayed where it was, unslung, unfired, gathering the same dust as the rest of it.

"You don't use that," I said, before I'd decided to. I nodded at the weapon. "The Boltcaster. You've had a blade in your hand twice since you got here and that thing hasn't come off your back once."

For a moment I thought I'd asked the wrong thing, in the way you can feel a wrong question land before anyone tells you it was wrong.

"No," it said. "I do not use it."

"Is it broken?"

"It functions." A pause — not the kind where someone decides what to say, the kind where someone does arithmetic on a sum they have done many times and still do not like the answer to. "It belonged to Dorn. I carry it because putting it down would mean something I am not prepared to mean yet."

I didn't say anything. There are sentences you don't step on.

"Dorn travelled with me," it went on, when it had decided I'd earned the rest. "He told jokes. There was one he was fond of, concerning a Mender, a Crafter, and a Grask. I will not attempt to reproduce it. The delivery was the entire substance of the thing, and the delivery was his." The glow dimmed a fraction. "He was killed by a Subsurface Shredder. It came up through the ground where we had made camp, and it was very fast, and I was not fast enough, which is a thing I have had occasion to be more than once and have never learned to care for."

"I'm sorry," I said.

"Thank you." Stated simply, the way it said most things — not performed, just received and set down, like a toll paid. "There was a third of us, also. Adura. She was not taken by any creature. She had been unwell for a long while, and one day the being unwell finished what it had been doing slowly." The eye held on me. "I mention them because you should understand what travelling with me has tended to cost the people who have done it. It would be dishonest to ask without saying so."

I turned my cup in my hands. The stew had gone cold while we'd been talking and I hadn't noticed. "So you're looking for new ones. Companions."

"I am."

"Here." I gestured vaguely at the inn, the town, the general smallness of everything.

"It seemed a reasonable location to begin. Frequently travelled. A mender in residence suggests a degree of civilised infrastructure. And the memorial pillar outside is—" The eye moved to the shuttered window facing the square — the glow sliding across the dark, unhurried, the head not following — then back to me. "—modest. Which suggests the town has not yet suffered the kind of loss that makes people reluctant to risk further loss."

I stared at him. "You read the pillar."

"Before you arrived this morning. Yes."

I thought about that — Third standing in the pre-dawn, reading a knee-high plinth with one blue eye while I'd been asleep, unbothered by the dark and apparently making strategic assessments about grief thresholds. "That's very—" I tried to find the word. "Methodical."

"I have found it useful to understand a place before asking something of it."

Before asking something of it. I looked at the tablecloth I'd draped over him — my idea, technically, though Tondi had grunted permission — and tried to remember if I'd ever thought of this town as something that could be asked things. I'd mostly thought of it as somewhere I'd been born and hadn't left.

"What are you asking of it?" I said. "Specifically."

"For someone — or several someones — willing to travel with a Titanspawn toward a problem that will likely become violent, with no guarantee of return, in service of preventing something most people do not yet know is happening."

The stew was definitely cold. I pushed it aside. "And what is happening? The thing most people don't know about."

The eye did not move from me. Whatever considering was happening, it happened behind that fixed blue point. Something settled in the set of the wooden shoulders — a decision being made about how much to say.

"Someone is attempting to resurrect a Titan," Third said. "I use the word attempting deliberately. I do not know how far along this attempt is. I do not know, yet, what they are collecting to achieve it. But I know they are collecting something, and I know that whatever it is, it has already cost one town everything it had."

The inn was very quiet. Klar had stopped scraping.

"The Titan," I said carefully. "As in — one of the ones from the war."

"As in the ones from the war. Yes."

"The ones that—" I gestured at the general state of everything. The world with a wound in its sky. The buildings with no ground-floor doors. The militia that drank before going out at night.

"Yes," Third said again. "Those ones."

I sat with that for a moment. Then: "And you're looking for companions. To stop this."

"I am already committed to stopping this. I am looking for companions because—" A pause. Longer than the arithmetic one, shorter than the one before he had said Adura's name. "Because I have found, over a considerable period of time, that I am better at most things with company. And because the last time something like this threatened everything living on Anka, it required a coordinated effort by an entire people to address it." The eye was steady on me. "That people no longer exists."

May they never be forgotten.

I didn't say it aloud. I didn't know why. It didn't feel like mine to say — and then I understood it was stranger than that. I had never in my life heard anyone name that lost people without the words trailing after, the charm tucked in quick and unthinking: Kern over his bow, the militia, Tondi when she wasn't even listening to herself. Everyone said the rest of it. Third had set an entire people down — gone, all of them, that people no longer exists — and reached for none of it. As though they were not a thing you guarded with a charm. As though they were just his.

"How many people are you looking for?" I asked instead.

"I am not certain. Enough. People who can think as well as fight. People who are not so committed to surviving that they become useless when survival seems unlikely."

"People like Roan," I said, before I'd decided to say it.

A long beat. "The militia member who found me at the gate."

"He's braver than he looks. And he doesn't drink for courage, which around here means something."

"I had noted that," Third said. "Yes. People like Roan." The bellows-sound again — slower this time, something gathering itself. "People like you, if you are willing."

I looked at my cold stew. I looked at the shuttered window. I thought about the pillar outside, knee-high, the town's whole history in a dozen names, and I thought about the fact that Third had stood in the dark reading it before the sun came up, before I'd even opened the inn, already deciding whether we were worth asking.

"You said whoever's doing this already cost one town everything," I said.

"Yes."

"And you don't know how many more before you can stop them."

"No. I do not."

I nodded slowly. Outside, the patrol's footsteps had faded. The Scar was almost fully across now — in another few minutes the light would go and the dark would come down. "How soon?" I asked.

*  *  *


# Six

"Soon" he'd said, which I'd took for a word to avoid choosing a number. it turned out to mean the next day, and it wasnt Third who chose the date. 

The traveller came in a little after midday, which was the wrong time for travellers. They arrived in the evening, mostly, when the dark was coming and a roof seemed worth paying for. A midday arrival meant someone who had been walking through the night, which meant someone who had not felt safe stopping, which meant news, and news that came in on foot at the wrong time of day was never the good kind.

He was Humin, middle-aged, with the particular grey-faced look of a man who has used up everything he had and is running on the memory of it. There was a wound on his forearm, bound with a strip of his own shirt, and the binding had gone stiff and dark. He came through the door and stopped just inside it, the way people do when they have been moving toward a destination for so long that arriving leaves them with nothing to do.

I got him into a chair before he fell into one. Klar brought water without being asked, which was Klar. Tondi, in her corner, set down her mending and watched, which was Tondi. And in the other corner, under his tablecloth, Third did not move at all, which by now I had stopped finding remarkable.

The traveller drank. His hands shook. When he could speak, the first thing he said was a name I half-recognised — Chipwood — a settlement maybe two days spinward, on the same trail as ours, the kind of place that was to us what we were to Dunarth. Smaller. Further out. The next notch down the line.

"Gone," he said. "It's gone."

"Gone how," Tondi said, from her corner. Not unkind. Just direct, the way you are direct with someone in shock, because vagueness is a thing they cannot afford and neither can you.

"Ravagers." He said the word and then had to stop and breathe around it. "A whole — there were so many of them. They don't come in numbers like that. They don't. They come in twos and threes, you lose a goat, maybe a man if he's careless. This was—" He looked at his bound arm as though it belonged to someone else. "They came all at once. Like they'd been told to."

I felt the room go quiet in the specific way a room goes quiet when several people have all had the same thought and none of them want to be the one to say it.

"Like they'd been told to," Tondi repeated.

"I got out because I was anti-spinward at the mill when it started. I ran. I didn't—" His voice did something then, and he didn't finish, and nobody made him. There are sentences you let a man leave on the floor.

I refilled his water. It seemed like the only useful thing available to me.

"There's more," he said, after a while. "I didn't see this part. I heard it from a woman on the trail, going the same way, faster than me." He wiped his mouth. "There was another town. Spinward of the one that — of mine. The Ravagers were going for it too. And a skybarge came down. The Porter."

I knew the Porter. Everyone on the trade did, even those of us who'd never once set foot up its ramp — it was the only inn in the world that came to you. A whole tavern slung under a skybarge's fans: Waj Hool's travelling house, food and drink and a dry bed for sale a hundred paces off the ground, here twice in three hundred spins if you were lucky and gone again on the wind. The whole town turned out to watch it settle, the time it came; I'd spent more than one shift at Tondi's after that wishing I worked a floor that moved. It was the last barge in the world I'd have looked to come down into trouble instead of sailing wide of it.

Tondi's mending had not moved in some time.

"Came down right over the square," the traveller said. "In the middle of it. Fans going, the whole — you could hear it to the next town, she said. And it just hung there. And the people were running for it, getting underneath it, and it wouldn't open. The barge wouldn't drop its ramp. Just hung there with the whole town screaming under it."

"Why wouldn't it open," Klar asked. He had not meant to ask. It had come out of him the way questions come out of the young, before they have learned that some answers cost more than the question is worth.

"She said there was someone arguing with Waj — with the master, down on the ground. Someone the master didn't want to listen to. She couldn't hear what was said. But the ramp came down in the end." He turned his cup in his hands, an echo of a gesture I recognised because it was mine. "Most of them got up and in before the Ravagers reached the square. Most of them. The woman said the thing that argued the ramp open was the last one up, and it walked up that ramp backward, facing the Ravagers, the whole way."

I did not look at the corner where Third sat. I want that on the record. I had every reason to and I did not, because some part of me had already done the arithmetic and did not want to confirm the sum.

But I heard it. Across the room, from under the tablecloth, the bellows in Third's chest drew breath — slow, the sound it made when something had landed with weight — and then nothing. No comment. No movement. Just the one long mechanical inhalation and the stillness after it, which was a different quality of stillness than the stillness before.

The traveller hadn't noticed. He was looking into his cup.

"There's one more thing," he said. "The part I keep — the part I can't—" He stopped. Started again. "When I went back. To my town. After. To see if anyone — I went back."

"You shouldn't have," Tondi said, gently, which was a register I had heard from her perhaps twice in my life.

"I know. I did anyway." He looked up, and his eyes found mine, and I wished they hadn't, because there was something in them that I did not have anywhere to put. "There was something there. Walking through it. No wait, more like floated through it. Through what was left. Not a Ravager — the Ravagers were gone, they'd moved on, they don't linger. This was after. Something floating through the dead, slow, like it was — looking for something. Going building to building. And the survivors, there were a few of us hiding, and it went right past us. Right past. Didn't look. Didn't care. Like we weren't worth the turning of its head."

The bellows again. Slow. Across the room. I kept my eyes on the traveller.

"What did it look like," I asked, and my voice came out steadier than I felt.

"I don't know. Cloaked. Hunched. There was a — a light on its back, blue-white, like a bottled storm, like a whole rack of them, glowing through the cloak." He shuddered, and it was not from the wound. "It found whatever it was looking for in the headman's house. I heard it — there was a sound like a building being unmade. And then it left. Anti-spinward. Away from everything. And it never once looked at any of us."

He went quiet then, all the way quiet, the quiet of a man who has finally set down the thing he had been carrying and discovered that setting it down does not make it lighter, only shared.

I gave him the room. I gave everyone the room. The fire ticked. Klar stood in the kitchen doorway with a cloth in his hands, not using it. Tondi's mending lay in her lap.

And in the corner, Third stood up.

He did it slowly, the tablecloth sliding off his shoulders, and he did not bother to catch it, which was the first time I had seen him fail to account for the cloak in any way. He stood in the corner of the inn at midday with his one blue eye gone bright — brighter than I had seen it, the glow of it hard and steady — and he looked at the traveller, and the traveller looked up at the thing that had been sitting two paces away the whole time, and made a sound.

He was not the only one to make it. I had forgotten them the way you forget furniture — but the news had drawn a few of the regulars in off the lane and then kept them, the way bad news keeps people, and they had spent the last hour with their backs to the corner, listening to a town die, while the corner sat two paces behind their spines and did not breathe. Old Sem was there, and a carter I half-knew, and a woman who'd come for stew and stayed for the worse thing. And now the corner had stood up, and shrugged its cloth onto the floor, and become a wooden monster with a burning eye, and it had been there, that close, the entire time. The room came apart the way a startled flock does, all at once and in every direction. A bench went over backward. The carter was on his feet with a paring knife he'd lifted off the bar without deciding to, holding it the way you hold a thing you already understand will not help. Sem had the door half open. The traveller had shoved himself clear off his chair and fetched up against the wall, and the noise coming out of him had stopped being a noise a man chooses to make.

"Sit." Tondi did not stand. She didn't need to. She had a voice she kept for about once in three hundred spins, and she spent it now, and it landed on the room like a dropped lid. The knife stopped. The door stopped.

"That," she said, into the ringing after, and she did not so much as glance at the corner, "is the reason there's meat in your bowls and not a Shredder on your road. It came in off the dark and laid the head of the thing on my table — the Shredder that's been taking travellers off the anti-spinward trail, the trail half of you walk to market. I examined it myself. Klar's been feeding it to you since the pot got hot." She licked her thread and went looking for her needle.

"It has sat in that corner behind a cloth, troubling no one, doing you the courtesy of not frightening the very people who are now frightening themselves over a tipped bench. So. Sit down and finish your bowls like grown folk, or take your fright out the door and leave the rest of us to hear the man — but either way you'll do it without the knife, Bevin, before you cut yourself and I'm obliged to stitch you for nothing." It was not kindness. Tondi had no use for kindness; she found it sentimental. It was the thing she kept instead, which was worth more — the flat, bottomless certainty of a woman who has decided a thing is true and will not be argued off it by anybody's heartbeat. Bevin looked at the knife as though it had got into his hand by witchcraft, and set it on the bar. Sem let the door swing to. The woman sat, because Tondi had said sit and there are people in every town who have been waiting their whole lives for someone to tell them what to do in exactly that voice. Nobody else quite managed it. But nobody ran, either, and I was starting to learn that nobody-running was the whole of what you got to ask of people the first time they met him. Through all of it Third had not moved. He had not turned his head, had not marked the knife or the door or the going-over of the bench; he had stood with his one bright eye fixed on the traveller and the traveller alone, as untroubled by a room deciding whether to kill him as the Scar is by the things it passes over. He had been waiting, I understood, for the noise to finish — not out of patience, but because the noise had nothing to do with the single thing he had stood up to learn. "Anti-spinward," Third said. The voice filled the room the way it always did, but there was something underneath it now, something I had no name for and was not sure Third did either. "You are certain. Anti-spinward."

The traveller could only nod his frightened head. Third turned his head — the head, the whole head — toward me. The eye was very bright.

"You asked how soon," he said.

I had. The evening before, at the corner table, I had asked exactly that, and he had not answered, and I had gone home thinking I'd have days to decide whether I'd meant it.

"Yes," I said. "I did."

"Now," Third said. "It is now."

*  *  *


# Seven

We got the traveller into one of the rooms upstairs, and he was asleep before Klar had finished setting down the second cup of water, the deep instant sleep of a body that has been refusing itself for too long. Tondi had looked at the wound on his arm and made the face she made when something was going to need more attention than it was going to get, and then she'd dressed it properly anyway, because that was the difference between Tondi and her reputation.

When she came back down, the inn had emptied of its few midday customers. It was just the four of us. Me. Tondi in her corner. Roan, who had come in off his rest and who had been standing by the door for the better part of an hour with the expression of a man who has not been invited to sit but does not want to leave. And Third, who had put the tablecloth back on without being asked, and who now sat at the corner table with his one blue eye on the middle distance, waiting, the way he waited for everything.

"All right," I said, and sat down across from him. "You said now. So tell us. All of it."

The bellows drew. Once. Then he began.

"I have been following this for some time," Third said. "Long enough to be certain it is not chance. Towns along the trails — small ones, mostly, the kind that do not appear on a trader's map and are not missed when they cease to appear anywhere at all. Each one struck. Each one in the same manner. Ravagers in numbers they do not naturally gather in, directed against the settlement all at once. And then, after, something walking through the wreckage. Searching. Taking one thing and leaving everything else, including the survivors, untouched."

"The thing the traveller saw," Roan said, from the door.

"Yes."

"With the lights on its back."

"Yes." Third's eye moved to Roan — the glow sliding across the room, unhurried, the head following. "magefire. A great many bottles of it, more than I have ever seen one body carry. I do not know why it needs so much. I know that it does."

I thought about the traveller's words — a bottled storm, a whole rack of them — and I thought about how Third had gone still when he heard it, and I made myself ask the question I had been not-asking since midday.

"You were there," I said. "At the town with the Skybarge. You're the one who argued the ramp open."

The inn was very quiet. Roan looked at me, then at Third.

"Yes," Third said. No weight on it. No reluctance. He stated it the way he stated the hour. "The master of the barge — a Hin called Waj Hool — did not wish to descend into a Ravager assault. This was a reasonable position. I persuaded him that it was less reasonable than the alternative." A pause. "I was the last aboard. I prefer to keep a Ravager pack in front of me where I can account for it."

"You walked up backward," Roan said, and there was something in his voice I hadn't heard from him before. Not quite awe. Closer to recognition.

"It is simply sound practice," Third said, which I was learning was the thing he said when the truer answer was something he did not have words for.

Tondi, in her corner, had not picked her mending back up. "You saved a town," she said. "And you did not stay. You came here instead."

"I saved the people of a town. The town itself is gone." The eye moved to her. "And I came here because the thing doing this moved anti-spinward, and anti-spinward of that town, on the trail it is following, is here. And then beyond here. I did not come to your town by accident, mender. I came because I believe it is in the path."

I felt that land in my stomach, cold and specific. "You think it's coming here."

"I think it is going somewhere, and that the line it is travelling passes near enough to here that I would not wish to be elsewhere when it does." He let that sit a moment. "But your town is not the destination. Of that I am reasonably certain. Your town has nothing it wants."

"And what does it want?" Tondi asked. "You keep saying a thing. The thing takes one object and leaves the rest. What is the object?"

This was the question. I could feel it was the question, the way you can feel the weight of a room turn toward a single point. Third was quiet for long enough that I thought, again, he might not answer. Then the bellows drew a second time — the second, I noted, the last one I would get from him tonight — and he set both his bifurcated hands flat on the table, which I had never seen him do, and which had the effect of making the wooden parody of a humin look, for a moment, like a man gathering himself to say a hard thing plainly.

"You know that the Titans made the world," he said. "This is told to children. It is also true, which is rarer than you would think for a thing told to children." The eye dimmed slightly — consideration, not grief. "What the children's telling leaves out is how. The Titans did not make the world by wishing it. They made it the way anything is made. With tools. With instruments. Things to cut with, and shape with, and bind one thing to another. Vast things, suited to vast hands."

"Tools," Roan repeated.

"When the Titans fell, the tools did not fall with them. They remained. They are scattered across all of Anka, and most of them are not recognised for what they are. A piece of titanic forge tongs, used as a shield; a titanic bowstring used as support for a bridge across a chasm; three gears that placed in proximity to each other begin rotating without external power. People walk past the instruments of creation every day and call them landmarks, or curiosities, or hauntings, and they are not wrong to, because the truth is not available to them and the landmark serves them better." The eye came back to me, bright again. "But someone has learned to recognise them. And someone is gathering them."

I had grown up in a world with a wound in its sky and doors on the second floor and a spire on the horizon that everyone navigated by and no one questioned. I had never once thought of any of it as a thing somebody made. It had simply always been the shape of things. To have Third say it plainly — that the Spire was a tool, that the world was a workshop someone had walked away from — did something to the floor of my understanding that I did not enjoy.

"Why," I said. "Why gather them. What do you do with a — with the tools that made the world?"

"That," Third said, "is what I do not know. Not with certainty." And I believed him, because Third did not pretend to certainties he lacked, which was rarer and more valuable than I had words for at the time. "But I have a fear. The tools that made a thing are the tools that can make it whole again — not mend it, the way you'd mend a chair, but make it, entire, a second time, out of whatever is left of it. And the largest thing that was ever unmade—" "Was a Titan," Tondi said. Quiet. She had got there a breath before I did.

"They made the world," Third said. "They made me, too, in their way — I am one of their tools. I watched it come apart: not into a body you could bury, but into red dust, and into something else — something that went sideways out of the world, and did not entirely leave it... It scatters. And if someone is gathering the tools of creation, then the question I cannot put down is whether they mean to gather a fallen Titan back — to make it whole out of its dust and its absence, and call it down off the far side of the world into this one. To undo the one thing the Virek spent an entire people to do." Nobody said anything.

The fire ticked. Upstairs, faintly, the traveller turned over in his sleep and was quiet again.

"You don't know that's what it's doing," I said, finally. I don't know why I said it. Maybe because someone had to put a hand against the size of the thing. "You said yourself. You don't know."

"I do not," Third agreed. "I may be wrong. I hope to be wrong. I have been wrong before, about smaller things, and the relief was considerable." The eye found me. "But I have learned that when I cannot rule out the worst version of a thing, it is unwise to plan for the comfortable one. So I am going to find whoever is doing this. And I am going to learn what they want. And if it is what I fear, I am going to stop them, because the last time this was necessary it required an entire people, and that people is gone, and so it appears the task has fallen to whoever is still standing near it." A pause. "Which, at present, is me."

"And us," Roan said.

He said it from the door, and then looked faintly astonished that he had said it, the way you look when a thing you had been deciding turns out to have already been decided somewhere below the place where you do your deciding.

Third turned his head toward him. The whole head.

"That is not a small thing to offer," Third said. "I would have you understand what you are offering before you offer it."

"I heard you," Roan said. "Towns that don't make the maps. People walked past like they weren't worth turning a head for. I'm militia in a town on a trail. If that thing's path runs through here, then standing at the gate with a spear isn't going to be the brave choice. It's just going to be the choice where I die at the gate." He swallowed. "I'd rather go meet it as someone who chose to. With someone who knows what it is."

I looked at Roan, who did not drink for courage, and who had just talked himself into the end of the world with his back against the inn's door, and I understood that whatever happened next, I was not going to be able to watch it from here. That had stopped being available to me somewhere around the word now.

"How soon," I said, for the second time in two days. But this time it wasn't really a question about Third's schedule.

Third's eye came to me, and the glow did the thing I would come to know as close as he came to gentleness.

"That," he said, "is up to you. But I would leave at first light."

*  *  *


# Eight

I closed the inn that night the way I'd closed it a thousand times, except that I was aware of doing it, which was the difference between a thing you do and a thing you are doing for the last time.

I wiped the tables that did not need wiping. I straightened the chairs. I checked the lockbox in the floor, the day's Black inside it — combination set, lever down, lid secured — and I thought about the traveller upstairs, and the town two days spinward that had a lockbox and a memorial pillar and a militia and a closing routine just like this one, right up until it didn't have anything at all. And then I made myself stop thinking about it, because that was a skill I had and there was no sense owning a tool you refused to use.

Tondi was in her corner. She had not gone up to bed, which she always did before I finished, which meant she was staying down on purpose, which meant she had something to say and was working her way around to the angle she wanted to say it from. I let her work. You did not hurry Tondi. Hurrying Tondi was how you got the short version, and the short version was always worse.

Klar had gone home an hour ago. Kanot had come by earlier — Kanot, who had wanted my job since before I'd realised it was a job a person could want, and who had said yes before Tondi finished asking, and who had looked at me with an expression caught between sympathy and the poorly hidden delight of someone inheriting a thing they'd coveted. I hadn't minded. It is easier to leave a place when you know the place will be fine. That is, I think, why Tondi had arranged it so fast. Not to replace me. To make it easy.

"You've packed," she said. Not a question.

"Roan helped me work out what to bring. Third corrected most of what Roan suggested." I hung the last cloth on its hook. "Apparently I was going to bring far too much of the wrong things and not nearly enough of the right ones, which Third says is the most common way the inexperienced die. Not the monsters. The packing."

"He's not wrong." Tondi's needle went in and out of whatever she was mending, the rhythm of it unbroken, the way it had been unbroken through every important conversation of my life. "Cheerful, that one."

"He grows on you."

"Most things that grow on you should be scraped off." But there was no heat in it. She'd made up her mind about Third somewhere around the second time he'd produced a severed head in her inn without disturbing the room, and Tondi's made-up mind was the most fixed object in the settlement, the Spire included.

I finished the room. There was nothing left to do, which meant there was nothing left to do but the thing we were both not doing.

I sat down across from her. She did not look up. The hair across her face did its work, the way it always had — Tondi conducted the important conversations of her life from behind a grey curtain, so that you could never be entirely sure she was having them.

"I could ask you to come," I said. "You'd be useful. You're more use than Roan and me put together."

"You could ask." The needle did not pause. "I'd say no. So don't, and we'll both save the breath."

"Why?"

It was, I realised as I said it, the first time in my life I had asked Tondi to explain herself. The needle stopped. Just for a moment. Then it went on.

"Because somebody who knows how to fix things has to be where the people are," she said. "Your wooden friend is walking toward the trouble. Good. Somebody should. But there'll be people here when you're gone, the same as there were before you came, and they tear their clothes and split their hands and take fevers, and they need somebody who stays." She pulled the thread taut. "Brave is going. I've nothing against brave. But somebody has to be the thing you come back to. And I have always been better at staying than at almost anything else."

I didn't say anything. There wasn't a safe thing to say.

She set the mending down — which she did not do, which I had perhaps seen her do twice — and reached into the basket at her side, and came up with a small roll of waxed cloth. She put it on the table between us and pushed it across with two fingers, the way she pushed everything she didn't want to be thanked for.

"Needles," she said. "Three. hexwood, anointed proper — not the bone kind that snaps when you breathe on it. And thread, the waxed gut, it'll hold through wet. You'll tear something out there — a pack strap, a coat, your own skin, doesn't matter — and there won't be a mender, because the mender will be here, being the thing you come back to." She still hadn't looked up. "I'd rather you didn't bleed out because you lacked the tools to close a seam. It would reflect poorly on me."

I picked up the roll. It weighed almost nothing. hexwood needles were not nothing — anointing wood that fine took a Crafter's whole patience and a measure of titan-blood besides, and a mender's needles were the kind of thing that got named in wills, handed down, guarded like teeth. She would have had these longer than she'd had me.

"Tondi—"

"Don't." The needle was back in her hand; the curtain of hair had closed again. "I mean it. Whatever you're winding up to say. Don't. You'll do it badly and then we'll both have to remember it."

So I didn't.

I put the needles in my coat, the inside pocket, the one over the chest, where you keep the thing you cannot afford to lose. And I stood up, because Roan was going to be at the door before first light and I still had not slept, and standing up was the only way the night was going to agree to end.

At the door I stopped. I couldn't help it. I turned around.

"Tondi."

She was mending. She did not look up. But the needle had stopped, and stayed stopped, which for Tondi was the same as another woman crossing a room with her arms open.

"Don't come looking for work here when you get back," she said, to the shirt in her lap. "Kanot's got your job, and I won't put a good worker out to take back one who ran off chasing Titanspawn across the world. You hear me? There's no job here for you."

When you get back. Not if. She had built the whole gruff little speech around a word she would have died before saying outright, and she had handed it to me anyway, wrapped in an insult so I could carry it without either of us having to look at it directly.

"I hear you," I said.

"Good." The needle started again. "Go on, then. Mind the seam on that coat, the left shoulder's going. I'd have got to it if you'd given me another day."

"I know."

"Go."

I went.

Roan was at the door, as it turned out, just lifting his hand to knock, with the particular look of a man who had also not slept and had decided that arriving early was a way of being brave about it. Behind him the sky had that first grey thinning at the edge of the Scar that meant the dark was an hour from breaking.

I did not look back at the inn as we walked toward the gate. I had looked back once already, at the only thing in there worth looking back at, and she had told me to go, and I was not going to spoil the single clean thing either of us had managed by doing it twice.

But I put my hand flat against my chest, over the inside pocket, where the needles were.

Just to be sure they were still there.

They were.

*  *  *

We left at first light — the Scar's edge going from black to charcoal to the flat grey that passes for morning, the change creeping across the sky the way water creeps across a floor, without any particular moment you could point to and call the start of the day.

We hadn't gone half a mile before the reeds started up. I'd heard them my whole life without once being out in them at the right moment to hear them properly — the bellow reeds that grew in stands along the low ground beside the road, tall and hollow and brown, and when the small wind that comes with the Scar's turning moved through them they let out a sound I can only call mourning: a low, tuneless moan, dozens of hollow throats at once, rising as the grey came up and falling as it settled. The morning bellow. Tondi had set the inn's whole day by it for as long as I'd known her — bread on at the first moan, shutters down at the evening one — and I'd thought of it, when I thought of it at all, as a thing the reeds did, the same as the Scar was a thing the sky did. Out here, with no walls to flatten it, it was enormous, and I understood for the first time that it was the nearest thing my whole country had to a clock. Roan walked into it without a flicker; he'd soldiered these roads and the sound was wallpaper to him. Third listened — the eye tracking the nearest stand as we passed, the small tilt that meant he was filing a thing away. "A loud stand, in a strong wind, will cover the sound of a man's feet entirely," he said, to no one in particular. "It is worth knowing which sounds in a country are the ones a person can hide inside." And he walked on, and left me to decide whether he'd handed me a piece of lore or a piece of warning — a distinction, I was learning, he didn't trouble to make.


# Nine

The gate was a different thing in the light. At night it had been a threat held off by spikes; now it was just a gap in a wall, with Pek leaning on the frame the way the militia always leaned on things, and the packed earth of the trail running out through it toward a horizon I had looked at my whole life and never once walked into.

There were not many to see us off. That suited everyone. A Forged leaving with two of the town's own was not the kind of thing you gathered to celebrate; it was the kind of thing you watched from a window with the shutter open just enough. I could feel the town doing exactly that around us, the way you can feel a room you've turned your back on.

Third stood a little apart, as he always did, his cloak doing its inadequate work and his one eye already turned toward the trail, toward the part of the morning that had not arrived yet. He had done this before. I understood that without being told — that for Third a gate was a gate, and a leaving was a leaving, and he had crossed more of both than there were names on every memorial pillar in the world. He was waiting for us to be finished being people about it.

Klar came running up just as Pek pushed the gate the rest of the way open.

"You almost left," he said, accusingly, out of breath, shoving a wrapped bundle into my arms. It was heavier than it looked and warm at the bottom. "Travel bread, the dense kind, it keeps. And the rest of the Shredder, smoked — don't make that face, it's good, I brined it. And there's a twist of the blue salt in the middle, don't lose it, that's the expensive one." He stopped, having run out of inventory, and then out of the thing under the inventory, which was the part he didn't have words for. "Just. Eat properly. He won't remind you." He jerked his head at Third. "He doesn't eat."

"I'll eat properly."

"You won't, but I tried." He stepped back, suddenly awkward, five thousand spins old again instead of the competent thing he became around a stove. "Bring the salt twist back if you don't use it. It's really expensive."

"I'll bring it back."

It was the second time in two mornings someone had handed me a thing I was meant to bring back, and I was beginning to understand that this was how the people who couldn't come with you said the thing they couldn't say: they gave you an errand. A reason shaped like an object. Come back, because the needles are mine. Come back, because the salt is expensive. Nobody said come back. Everybody said it.

Then we went through the gate.

I had expected it to feel like something. A line crossed, a door shut, some interior click of before and after. It didn't. It felt like walking, which is all it was — the spikes sliding past on either side, sharpened logs I'd seen ten thousand times and never from this angle, and then the open trail, and then the town was behind us and the morning was in front of us and my legs were just doing the ordinary thing legs do.

The Spire was on the horizon where it always was. From the town you mostly saw it over the rooftops, a thin dark line you navigated by without thinking, the way you navigate by a thing you've decided is permanent. Out here, with nothing between us and it but a great deal of distance, I could see the gold catch at its tip where the Scar's grey light found it. Spireward was that way. Everything was measured from it. I had known that since before I could walk and had never once had a reason to care, and now I was walking across the actual world it stood in the middle of, and the knowing turned over in my chest into something that felt strange and large and not entirely comfortable.

We walked. Roan beside me, Third a few paces ahead at a pace he'd clearly chosen to be followable. The trail rose toward a low hill — the first hill, the one that from town was just the edge of the world you could see, the place the trail went to disappear.

I knew that when we crested it, the town would go behind us. The pillar, the inn, the gatehouse, all of it, dropping below the line of the land like a coin into a pocket.

I did not look back.

So I kept my eyes on Third's back and the gold on the horizon and the dense warm weight of Klar's bundle in my arms, and I walked over the hill and did not turn around, and I was, I think, a little proud of myself for it.

Roan looked back.

I saw him do it — at the crest, just before the town would have gone — a quick turn of the head, the way you check whether someone is watching you leave. I knew the look. I'd worn it. It was the look of a person who needs the leaving to have been witnessed, because a brave thing done where no one sees it doesn't yet feel, to a certain kind of young man, entirely real.

I didn't know yet whether anyone had been at the gate to see him. I didn't ask. It seemed like the kind of thing a person should be allowed to keep to himself, at least at the start, before the road taught him what I suspected it was going to teach him — that the looking back stops mattering somewhere out there, and that the not-needing-to is a thing you earn rather than decide.

Then the hill was behind us too, and the town with it, and there was only the trail and the grey morning and the gold pin of the Spire holding the whole flat world in place.

Some part of me had spent my whole life believing nothing would ever change in a quiet town. That part had been right, in its way. The town hadn't changed. The town was exactly where I'd left it, doing exactly what it had always done, and would go on doing it without me, with Kanot wiping my tables and Tondi mending its torn shoulders and Klar feeding whoever was hungry.

It was just that I was no longer in it.

"How far to the first stop?" I asked, because the silence had gone on long enough and because I had discovered that I did not want to walk inside my own head any further just then.

"Four days to the causeway," Third said, without turning. "If the weather holds and you keep up." A pause. The glow of his eye angled back at me, just slightly, the head not following. "You will keep up. You are already doing better than most."

"That's almost encouraging."

"It was meant to be entirely encouraging. I am told I lack the range."

Roan laughed — surprised out of him, the first real laugh since the gate — and Third's eye held on us a moment longer, bright in the grey light, before turning back to the trail.

We walked on toward the causeway, and the Spire, and the thing at the end of it that none of us yet understood, and behind us the town did not change at all.

*  *  *


# Ten

We saw it on the third day, which was a day earlier than Third had said, and I learned something about distance on Anka that the trail between two larger settlements had never taught me: that a thing can be visible long before it is near, and that the gap between the two is where your legs find out what they are made of.

The country we crossed to reach it had already begun the work of unteaching me. I had spent my life in a day's walk of the inn, in soft managed land — fields, the near woods, the road — and I had thought, without ever putting it to myself, that the world was mostly that, with the strange parts kept out beyond it like weather. I had it backwards. The soft land was the exception. The world the Titan left was the rule, and the rule was wild.

On the second day the trail brought us up against something I will not forget if I live to be as old as Third. The ground had been broken — not cut, not trenched, but *broken,* the way a frozen puddle breaks when you put your heel through it, or the way dried mud lifts and curls back from its own cracks on a hot day, except that here the puddle was the bedrock of the world. Great slabs of grey stone stood heaved up out of the earth at every angle, some tilted, some standing near on end, twice and three times Third's height, their broken edges raw and jagged against the sky, leaning on one another and fallen apart with gaps between where the trail had to thread. It was as if a whole sheet of stone had been stepped through from underneath and left frozen mid-shatter, every shard thrust up and pointing nowhere. Folk call them titan footprints. The country is stitched with them — you can walk a tenday and cross a dozen — and some sit heaped in a rough oval you could read as a heel and a sole if you wanted to frighten yourself, and some only wander, broken slab after broken slab, the way a thing might stagger. Third laid his wooden hand flat against one of the tilted faces as we passed, the way you'd lay a hand on the flank of a big animal to feel whether it was breathing.

"Walking, or dragged, or thrown down," he said.

"I was not told which. The stone will not say." It should have felt like ruin — dead, and long done. It felt like picking a path through a mouth full of broken teeth.

The teeth had one bite left in them. Roan, hurrying for the open light at the far edge, put his boot down among the loose rubble at the base of the last slab — and the rubble bit him. He went down with a yelp, hauling at his leg, and clamped to the side of his boot was what I'd have sworn a breath earlier was only another fist-sized stone: a land oyster, shut tight as a miser's purse, gripping with a strength its size had no business owning. It had taken the boot and not the foot, which was luck and nothing else. Third knelt, worked the point of a Falka into the seam of the shell, and the thing let go all at once with a wet click. Roan called it several names. Third turned it over, considered it without expression, and said, "Supper." It was the nearest thing to a joke I'd yet had out of him — and the meat of it, that night, was good, whatever Roan muttered about being ambushed by lunch. Most things out here were hungry, one way or another; at least this one paid us back for it.

And that evening we came through the shards.

I had met a shard tree before — there's a lone one behind the mender's, and Tondi used to send me out to sweep the fallen leaves before some fool stepped on them barefoot. So I thought I knew the word. I did not know the word. What we walked into that evening was a forest of them grown the way they grow when nobody cuts them, which is to say *up,* and up, and up — slender pale trunks like a thousand bare masts, bone-coloured, naked the whole of their ridiculous height, with nothing on them at all until, very far overhead, each one burst into a single crown of the amber glass-leaves, so that the whole stand had a floor of pale pillars and a roof of glittering amber you could not have reached with a thrown stone. The light came down through it broken into coins. Underfoot the ground was black and soft and rich as a turned kitchen garden, thick with the amber that had already come down — though little of it stayed amber long: the fallen leaves went brittle, then to flakes, then to dark crumb, quicker than any leaf I knew, until the grove seemed to be quietly feeding on its own shed glass. It was, I think, the most beautiful thing I had seen in my life, and I said so, and Third said "wait," and the wind came up.

The sound started overhead — a tinkling, a million tiny glass bells, lovely, and then less lovely, then a hiss running through the crowns far above as the harder gusts took hold, and Third said "down, and still," and we crouched, and the beautiful roof began to come apart and rain itself down on us. Amber leaves, thin as a fingernail and edged like one of Tondi's good shears, turning and flashing as they fell the whole long way from the crowns, sticking point-first into the leaf litter around us with a sound like soft sleet. One opened a line across the back of my hand I never felt arrive. Roan took one through the meat of his shoulder and swore. Third simply knelt over the two of us with his cloak spread and his back to the sky and let the glass break against him, patient as the rock walls, while the trees sang their gorgeous lethal song twenty men's height above and shed knives until the wind got bored and moved on.

When it stopped, the floor around us glittered with fallen amber, and the crowns overhead were thinner, and just as beautiful, and I understood the shape of the lesson the country was teaching: that here the beautiful thing and the thing that would kill you were not two different things you could learn to tell apart. They were the same thing, and it depended entirely on the wind, and the wind did as it pleased.

The thinned crowns did not trouble Third. "They grow as fast as they shed," he said, already moving us on. "A thumb's length in a day. More, where the ground is good — and the ground is always good here." He did not say why, but I had stood ankle-deep in that black rich earth, and I had seen what the storm cut down, and a few paces on I worked out the why for myself and wished I hadn't. A grove like that never starved, because the wind saw to its feeding, and not with leaves alone. Whatever the glass brought down and the night caught out — a slow bird, a slower beast, a traveller too proud to crouch — went into the same dark crumb in the end and came back up the pale trunks as crown. The shards grew back so fast because nothing they killed was ever wasted.

So when, on the third day, I thought I saw a wall of cloud sitting low on the horizon, the flat grey kind that means weather, I had already half-learned not to trust my first read of anything.

Then, over the next half-day, it refused to behave like weather — it did not move, did not thin, did not climb. It only resolved, slowly, into a line. A straight line, laid across the world from one edge of sight to the other, too level to be a ridge and too long to be anything a person would build.

"The causeway," Roan said, when it had become undeniable. He said it the way you say a word you've only ever heard, trying it in your mouth to see if it fits the thing in front of you. It didn't, quite. No word would have.
