"The Emperor's Causeway," Third said. "Though there was no emperor. The name attached itself later, the way names do, looking for an owner that suits the size of the thing."

We reached its foot near dark, and dark was the first time I understood it properly, because in the failing light I could no longer see the whole impossible length of it and was left with only the part directly above me, which was enough.

It was a bridge. That was the simplest true thing to say about it, the way Third would have said it. A bridge wide enough that I counted, out loud, like a child — twelve men could have walked it shoulder to shoulder, and there would have been room left over for their packs. It stood on arches that lifted the roadway six men high, and the arches marched away into the dark in both directions with the patience of something that had been marching since before there was anyone to watch. The stone was a single piece. I walked up and put my hand on the nearest pillar and there was no seam in it, no mortar, no mark of a tool. It was not built the way a wall is built, stone on stone. It was simply one stone, the size of a bridge that crossed the world, and someone had decided it should be that shape and it had agreed.

"It does not connect to anything," Third said, beside me, his eye moving along the dark span. "I have walked considerable lengths of it, in my time. It begins where it begins and ends where it ends and neither place is anywhere. There is a theory that it moved troops during the war. The theory does not survive contact with the route. No front it could have served lies along its line, and the kinds of things the Titans made for war did not march in tidy columns that required a road." The bellows drew, once, low. "I think it was made because making it was possible. I think a great deal of the world was made for that reason. It is the explanation that fits the evidence, and it is also the one that frightens people, which is perhaps why they prefer the emperor who never existed."

I took my hand off the stone. I didn't like the way it was not-cold — not warm either, just absent of temperature, the way deep water is — or how I imagined the inside of the Scar might be, if you could touch it.

"You've walked it," I said. "When?"

A pause. Not the arithmetic kind. The other kind.

"Early," Third said. "I was very new to thinking, and it was a good place to do it. Long and straight and empty, and nothing on it that wanted anything from me." The eye came to me, the glow steady. "I have not been back to it until now. I find that I had forgotten how large it is. That is unusual. I do not generally forget sizes."

He didn't explain further, and I had learned by then not to push at the doors Third left ajar — he closed them if you pushed, and opened them on his own if you waited. So I waited, and he didn't open it, not then, and we made camp at the foot of the nearest ramp.

We did not make it under the arches, and Third was particular about that. The underside of the span, where the pillars swelled to meet the roadway, was pocked with gaps, and in the gaps — once I knew to look — were the crabs: broad flat things the exact mottled grey of the stone they wedged into, so still and so well matched that the whole vast belly of the causeway might have been crawling and you'd never have known it by daylight. "They eat what the road lets fall," Third said. "In the dark phase they are less particular about whether it has stopped moving first." So we slept out in the open, a clear stone's-throw from the nearest pillar, and I lay a long time listening to the small dry scrape of them working the arches overhead, and decided the bare ground was the friendliest thing the causeway had shown us yet.

*  *  *

The ramps were how you got up onto it. They corkscrewed around the pillars at intervals Third said ran a thousand paces apart, give or take, the whole length of the thing — gentle spirals a cart could have climbed, if there had been carts, or anything to pull them. The embankments where they met the ground were furred with driftcane, the one growing thing that seemed to want the causeway's company: pale jointed stalks rooted shallow and wide in the scoured soil, rattling at every gust. Twice on the climb a whole stand of it tore loose somewhere above and came bowling down the spiral past us, a tumbling haystack of cane that made Roan flinch and swear and me laugh at him, which I needed. Third gathered an armful of the dry fallen stuff without breaking stride — cut and bound, he said, it was worth the carrying: light, and strong, and good for a splint or a tent-pole or a dozen other things the road would eventually ask for.

In the morning I climbed one and got my first proper look at the road by daylight, and that was when the causeway stopped being a wonder and started being a wrongness. Nothing grew on it. Not the way nothing grows on a swept floor — the way nothing grows on glass. The roadway ran out ahead of us, bare grey stone the width of twelve men, and the moss and blown soil that should have found a seam to root in over an age had found none, because there were no seams; there was nothing for a root to take hold of, the same one impossible stone all the way down. Only at the very lip of the road, where grit and leaf-fall banked against the low parapet, did anything green get a start, and even that looked provisional, as if the road might shrug it off whenever it remembered to. And the trees. I have to describe the trees, because they were the part that made Roan go quiet, and Roan did not go quiet easily.

On both sides of the causeway, for a stone's throw out, there were no trees. The forest came up to that line — a hard, clean line, running the whole visible length of the road — and stopped, as if it had been told to. Beyond the line the forest was ordinary, dense and green and getting on with its business. Inside it, nothing taller than grass. Not cut. Not burned. Just held, the way you'd hold a dog back by the collar, spin on spin on spin, without ever quite letting it forget it wanted to come forward.

"Something holds this back," I said.

"The road does," Third said. He said it without drama, the way you'd state the weather.

"It has held the forest off itself since before either of us, and it will go on holding it off after. It is of a piece with the rest — the stone that takes no seam, the cold that is not cold, the span that joins nowhere to nowhere. It does not rot, because it does not take part in the rules the rest of us rot under. I do not know how it does any of it. I have had three hundred thousand spins to not know, and the time has not helped." The eye travelled the bare stone ahead, level and unhurried.

"What I do know is that it is the fastest, most open ground in a country that offers neither, and that in all my walking of it nothing has ever once objected to my company. I would rather walk a road that unsettles me for reasons I can name than a trail that hides the ones I cannot." He started forward.

"We walk it in daylight. And we keep to the centre, well clear of the edge."

"Why the edge?"

"Because it is a long way down," he said, "into a forest that has spent an age wanting up." I looked at the held-back green lying quiet on either side, six men below the parapet, and decided I had not needed that explained.

*  *  *

We had meant to resupply at the causeway. Third had said, back in the planning, that the ramps sometimes drew people — that a raised road with cleared sightlines and a stone's-throw of open ground on either side was, to the right kind of traveller, the safest camp in a dangerous stretch of country, and that where travellers gathered, someone always turned up to sell them things at a markup.

We found the someones near midday, at the fourth ramp along, and it was not the lone pedlar I'd pictured from Third's telling. It was a camp. A loose ring of carts drawn up on the broad apron where the ramp met the ground — two dozen and more, painted in colours that had long since given up being bright, shafts turned inward, canvas sides let down into walls; cook-fires smoking; a picket line of goats, lean and patient, and here and there a dog dozing in a cart's shade; washing strung between wheels; a knot of children chasing something I couldn't see, and two old women not watching them with great skill. A whole travelling town of folk who lived on the move and had decided, the same as Third would have, that a raised stone road with a stone's-throw of cleared ground on every side was the one place in this country a person might close both eyes at once. Where travellers gathered, Third had said, someone always turned up to sell them things — and here were a hundred someones, selling to each other and to whatever the road brought, waiting out the bad stretch in the best spot it had. The ramps sometimes drew people. He had undersold it.

We picked our trader for no better reason than that he looked us over with neither fear nor appetite — one stall among the many, a Hin, broad and unhurried, with what I took to be his grown children working the cart at his back: dried goods, waxed cord, the small necessary nothings that a journey eats. He watched us come with the flat patience of a man who has sold to stranger customers than two nervous youths.

Third stayed up on the road.

He did it without discussion, the way he did most of the things that made him easier to be near — drifted a little apart as we approached, found a place at the edge of the road where he could watch both the span and us, and became, in the way he had, part of the scenery. I understood it by now. People haggled better when they were not being watched by something that had been built to end them. And someone had to keep watching the road.

So I went down and did the trading, with Roan at my shoulder learning how it was done, and the Hin sold me waxed cord and dried fish and a block of the dense travel-fat that you melt into everything to make the walking possible, and he took Klar's overpayment instincts out of me one Black at a time, and it was — I want to set this down plainly, because there was so little of it on that road and I did not know yet how little was coming — it was *nice*. A small ordinary transaction in the morning air, a Hin grumbling about his prices the way Tondi grumbled about her seams, Roan asking too many questions and getting answers anyway.

"You'll be going anti-spin," the Hin said. Not a question. He was wrapping the fish.

"We will," I said.

He glanced up the long bare run of the road, and then — quickly, the way you touch a bruise to check it's still there — at Third, up on the high stone with his back to us, watching the distance.

"Folk go anti-spin up the high road," he said, quiet, just for me, "and you don't always see 'em come back down it. Could be it's only a long road. Could be it ain't. We sell at the foot of the ramp and we sleep in the trees and we let that road keep its own counsel." He knotted the cord on the parcel.

"No charge for the saying. You've the look of folk who'll want every Black." I paid him anyway, and carried the warning up the ramp along with the fish, and gave it to Third, who received it the way he received most things — as confirmation of a sum he had already finished adding.

"He is wise," was all Third said. Then he shouldered back into his cloak, and we walked on, anti-spinward, down the centre of a road that held a whole forest at arm's length on either side, under a sky with a wound rotating slowly across it.

Behind us the camp grew small — the carts, the smoke, the strung washing, the Hin lifting one broad hand without looking up from his weighing — and then the ramp grew small too, and then there was only the road, running straight as a drawn line toward the place none of us could see yet: bare and patient and wholly indifferent, holding the green world off its back as it had long before us, and meaning to long after.

*  *  *


# Eleven

The carts had been on the road a long time.

There were four of them, hand-carts of the kind the Hin trader had, overturned and dragged half across the road and left — not recently, the wood gone grey and splitting, one wheel still turning a slow finger's-width back and forth when the wind found it. Whatever had happened here had happened to people going the other way, spinward, toward the town and the trader and the ordinary world, and had happened all at once, the way the bad things on this road seemed to.

"Pulled across deliberate," Roan said, crouching at the nearest. He'd got better at this — at reading a scene before walking into it, at not touching the first thing his hands wanted to touch. "Like a barricade. Someone tried to make a wall of them."

"It didn't work," I said.

"No." He stood. "It didn't."

There was no blood, which somehow was worse than blood would have been; blood at least tells you a story with an ending. This just told you people had been here, and had been afraid enough to throw their whole livelihood into a wall across the road, and then were not here anymore, and the carts had stayed to think about it for a few hundred spins.

The wall of them was too high and tangled to climb cleanly with packs, and the cleared margin on either side dropped off the edge of the causeway into the held-back forest six men below. Roan and I stood there working out the puzzle of it — over, or down and around, or unstack the topmost cart and risk the whole rotten heap coming down — the ordinary arithmetic of an obstacle, the kind of problem I'd have welcomed an hour earlier for being a problem I could actually solve.

Third had gone quiet beside me. I noticed it the way you notice a sound stopping. He was looking at the wall of carts and not, I thought, really seeing it — the glow of his eye had gone soft and far-off, the way a person's eyes go when they're doing sums in their head or remembering a road, and he had begun, very slightly, to drift. Not toward the carts. Past them, somehow, at an angle I couldn't have drawn. He took a step like a man stepping off a kerb he'd walked a thousand times without looking.

"Third," I said, turning to ask him which way he thought, "do we—"

He wasn't there.

I want to be precise about this, because I have tried since to describe it to people who weren't on that road and have always failed. He had been standing at my shoulder. He did not walk away. He did not flicker or fade or do any of the things a story would give him to do. He simply stepped — sideways, at an angle that my eye refused to hold, the way you can't quite look at the place a reflection comes from — and the step took him out of the world, and the space where he had been was just air, with the dead carts beyond it.

It did not look like a decision. That was the thing I kept, after. It looked like a habit. It looked like the most natural thing in the world for him to do, and the most natural thing in the world is the one you do without remembering you have company.

Roan made a sound. I don't think he knew he made it.

&nbsp;

<p align="center">◆ &nbsp; ◆ &nbsp; ◆</p>


Sideways was grey.

It was always grey, and it was always quiet in the particular way that is not the absence of sound but its opposite, a pressure, a held breath the size of a world. Third had stepped this way more times than he could have counted if counting had mattered here, and it never mattered here. The causeway hung beside him as a smear of pale light, the carts a darker smudge in it, the held-back forest a green rumour far below. He had only to walk a little way along the grey and step back into the world past the obstruction. Seconds, on the other side. It was, as he would tell the Humin girl when he stepped back, faster.

But the grey did not only carry him forward. It had never only carried him forward. The grey was where the things he did not look at directly went to wait for him, and it was patient, and it was very old, and when he walked through it the old things rose up to walk beside him whether he had asked them to or not.

The carts were overturned. A wall of overturned things, thrown up by the frightened, that had not held.

And he was somewhere else, and it was long ago, and the things thrown down around him were not carts.

*  *  *

The village had been small. He understood that the way he understood everything in those first spins of his thinking — as information, arriving flat and complete, with no one yet home behind it to be moved by it. Small village. Virek. Perhaps forty dwellings, the woven-wall kind the green ones favoured, most of them open now, their walls broken outward.

His single eye moved across it and lit what it found. Blood on a wall, dark and dried at the edges, fresh and wet at the centre, pooled below the nearest body. The body was Virek and had been for a long time and would not be much longer; already the green was going grey. There were a great many bodies. He had counted them before he chose to, the count arriving the way the smallness of the village had arrived, unbidden and exact, and he made himself stop knowing the number because the number had begun to feel like a thing he should not be holding so lightly.

His hand held a glaive. The blade was wet.

He looked at the blade for a long time.

*You are Forged to eliminate the Virek,* the thoughts said. They were not cruel. They had never been cruel. Cruelty would have been easier to set down. They were only certain, the way the ground is certain, the way falling is. *This is the purpose. This is the whole of the purpose.*

There was an arrow in his garb, high on the shoulder, driven through the woven cords that served him for muscle and stopped by the wood beneath. It was a clean arrow, unfired-looking, absurd. And the blood on the cords around it was older than the arrow — he could see that, he could see that the blood had dried before the shaft ever found him, which meant the blood was not from this wound, which meant the blood was from the blade, which meant —

He did not finish the thought. It was the first thing he had ever declined to finish.

He could not tell. That was the truth of it, then and after and for every spin that would ever come after: he stood in the wreck of a village with a wet blade in his hand and blood on his armour that was not his own and he could not tell whether he had been one of the ones who made the wall of bodies, or had only arrived in time to stand among it. The others like him had done this. He knew that. He had perhaps done this. He would never know, and the not-knowing would walk in the grey with him forever, and it was walking with him now.

Then the crying.

It was small and furious and entirely alive, and it came from a bundle near the broken wall of the nearest dwelling, and when his eye found it the crying did not stop, which was itself a kind of marvel — every other living thing he had come near in those early spins had stopped, had frozen or fled. This one only lay on its back and flailed its small green limbs and howled at the torn sky as if the sky had personally done this and owed it an answer.

*A Virek,* the thoughts said. *Eliminate it. The purpose is not complete while one remains.*

"It seems immobile," he said. His voice surprised him, in the quiet, among the dead. It always surprised him then. "It cannot have done anything. It cannot even turn over."

*That is not the measure. The measure is what it is, not what it has done.*

He looked at the small howling thing. He looked at the wet blade in his hand. And he watched his own arm — he remembered this exactly, the watching, as though the arm belonged to someone he was only distantly responsible for — reach down, open, and gather the bundle up by its wrappings, the blade forgotten, fallen, thumping once on the bloodied ground.

The wrappings began to tear.

"These will not hold for long," he told it, as though it had asked.

So he knelt among the people he might have killed, and he took the clothing from one of the dead — gently, which no one was there to see, which the thoughts informed him was wasted gentleness, which he did anyway — and he fashioned from it a harness, a sling, the kind he had seen the green ones carry their young in, before. He worked the knots with two fingers and a thumb of each bifurcated hand, slow, clumsy, learning it as he did it.

"This will hold for longer."

He put the child into the sling and drew it against the wooden cage of his chest, against the slow bellows that moved there, and the crying changed. It frayed, and softened, and came apart into long shuddering sobs, and then into something that was almost only breathing.

He went very still.

"That was unexpected," he said. "If I were to guess — you find this comfortable. The sound. The motion of the bellows, perhaps." The thing against his chest hitched once and was quiet. "I did not know I could be of use in that way. I have not, until now, been built for it."

*It is consuming time. It will consume more. Set it down. The purpose —*

"I have heard the purpose," he said, and it was the first time he had answered the thoughts aloud, and the sound of his own refusal in the dead village was the strangest thing that had happened to him since the dust. "I have heard it a great deal. I am choosing, instead, this."

And he turned away from the direction his own kind had gone — he could feel them out there, the unwoken ones, moving on to the next thing their thoughts told them to move on to — and he walked the other way, out of the village, into the trees, with a Virek child breathing against the place where his heart was not.

Behind him in the pale red dust that lay over everything in those spins, the dust that had woken him and had not stopped settling since, he left two cloven prints and the small dragged smudge of a sling, and nothing else, and no name yet for the thing he carried, because he did not yet know it would need one, or that he would be the one to give it, or what it would cost him, in the end, to have been the one who did.

*  *  *

The grey took it back. It always took it back. It gave him these things and then it took them back and left him holding only the walking, which was its single mercy and the reason, he suspected, that he had never stopped.

He stepped sideways, into the world, on the far side of the carts.

&nbsp;

<p align="center">◆ &nbsp; ◆ &nbsp; ◆</p>


He was on the far side of the cart-wall.

Not appearing, exactly — just suddenly accounted for, standing on the swept stone beyond the wreck as though he'd been there the whole time and we'd merely failed to notice him. For a breath he stayed facing anti-spinward, away from us, already half a step into walking on, the way you carry on down a road you've walked alone for longer than there have been roads.

Then he stopped.

I watched it happen — watched the moment the habit let go of him and he remembered. His head came round, and the glow of his eye came back up from wherever it had gone, and he looked at the two of us standing on the wrong side of a wall of dead carts, and something in the set of him changed. He had left us. He had not meant to, and he had done it as easily as breathing, and now he saw that he had done it.

He came back — not sideways, and not over. He did not climb to our side and start the crossing again from where we stood. He stayed where his own habit had carried him, on the far side of the wreck, and he set his hands to the barricade from there, and began, without a word, to take it apart toward us.

It was not the effortless thing a story would have given him. He was stronger than a man — stronger than Roan and me together, I'd have guessed — but he was not a windlass, and the carts were heavy and snarled and half-rotted into one another, and he worked them the way a patient person works a knot: a cart rocked up onto its edge and walked round on one corner, a wheel freed and rolled aside, a shaft lifted clear and laid down out of the path. What he had that we did not was not size. It was that he never strained against a thing and gave up, never stopped to breathe, never needed the small rests a body needs. He simply continued, at the same steady rate, until the stone showed through between us.

"That one next," he said, when he reached the rotten cart at the top, the one Roan and I had been afraid to touch. "Take the wheel side. I will take the weight." And he eased it down clear of the path, the two of them, as gently as if it might still have had someone in it.

So we opened the barricade between us, Third from his side and Roan and I from ours, meeting in the middle of it, and none of us spoke of the other thing, the sideways thing, because Roan plainly did not have words for it and I plainly did, and mine were all questions, and there is a time for questions and it is not while you are standing on a road that keeps itself otherwise clear for reasons of its own.

But I looked at him while we worked. I couldn't help it. The glow of his eye had gone down to its lowest ember, the way a banked fire goes, and once — easing a shaft down to the stone — his bellows drew long and slow, the sound it made only at the weights I had learned to watch for, though the shaft was no great burden and nothing we'd handled all day had drawn that sound from him before. He stood a moment when the path was clear, among the carts he'd moved, as though the few grey seconds he'd spent crossing had been a great deal longer than ours, and had asked something of him that the work never could.

"Third," I said. "Are you—"

"I am the same when I return as when I leave," he said. "It costs me nothing you need fear."

It was, I would understand much later — when I knew more than I wanted to and could not give the knowing back — the only thing Third ever told me that I am certain he believed and that was not true.

"We should keep moving," he said, before I could find the shape of any of it. "We are losing the light. And I find I would like to be off this road before dark, today, more than I usually do."

He didn't say why. I didn't ask. We walked on anti-spinward down the swept and watched-over road, the three of us together, and I did not learn for a long time what he had carried across it, or that he had carried it at all.

*  *  *


# Twelve

We came off the causeway after travelling it for two days, by one of the corkscrew ramps. The last of the high country had been more of the world insisting on itself — a stretch where the trail crossed a field of low sterile rings, perfect circles of bare grey dirt a few paces across with a sickly green tree dead-centre of each, no grass, no weed, nothing growing under them but the next identical sapling pushing up at the ring's edge to start its own circle of starvation. These I knew — occluding trees, the farmers near home called them, and hated them — and we walked between the rings the way you'd walk between graves, and nobody spoke, and the trees drank the life out of the dirt in their tidy obsessive circles and asked us for nothing. So when the ramp let us down into ordinary country that felt like a held breath let out, and for half a day the trail was just a trail and the trees were just trees, I believed in the world again — not because the world had earned it, but because I wanted it so badly that half a day of plain green was enough to buy me.

Then the trees changed.

It wasn't sudden. That was the first wrong thing about it — there was no edge to the place, no line like the held-back margin of the causeway, no moment you could point to and say *here, it starts here.* The ordinary forest simply thinned and was replaced, tree by tree, the way one word in a sentence gets replaced until you look up and the sentence means something else, and by the time I understood what I was looking at we had been walking in it for some while already, which I did not like, because it meant the place had got in before I noticed the door.

Every tree was bent the same way.

Not bent like wind-bent, all leaning from a prevailing weather. Not bent like reaching, the way trees crowd toward light. Each trunk came up out of the ground and then turned — a long, smooth, deliberate-looking curve, low down, near the root — and then straightened again and went up true, so that every tree in that forest stood vertical at the top and yet carried, down near the earth, the identical memory of having once leaned hard in a single direction. The same direction. Every one. As far into the green as I could see, a whole forest of trunks all making the same turn at the same height, like a field of people caught in the instant of flinching from the same blow.

"Snake Forest," Roan said. His voice came out wrong, too flat, the voice of someone keeping a lid on. "My gran used to — there's a rhyme. About not counting the trees." He stopped. "I don't remember how it goes."

"Then do not try to remember it," Third said.

*  *  *

There is nothing to tell about the Snake Forest, which is exactly the thing that is hard to tell. Nothing happened. Nothing moved. Nothing came out of the green at us with teeth. If you asked me to list the events of the worst stretch of country we crossed in all that journey, I would have nothing to give you but a list of things that did not occur, and the slow unbearable accumulation of them.

The trees did not move. I want that on the record, because the whole long day was a war against the conviction that they had. You would look at one — a particular trunk, its curve, the way it almost made a shape if you held it wrong in your eye — and you would walk, and you would look away to mind your feet, and when you looked back the conviction would be on you cold and total that it had shifted while your eyes were elsewhere, that the curve was at a different angle now, that it had been facing the path and was now facing *you.* And you could not prove it hadn't. That was the engine of the place. You could never prove it hadn't, because you had looked away, and looking away was the one thing a body cannot stop itself doing.

It got into your head through that crack and then it widened the crack.

The thing I have never been able to explain to anyone is how a place with no enemy in it can exhaust you more thoroughly than a fight. By midday — if it was midday; the Scar's light came down grey and even through the canopy and gave us nothing to steer by — Roan had stopped talking entirely and walked with his hand wrapped white-knuckled around the haft of his spear, turning, every few paces, to check the trees behind us. There was nothing behind us. He checked anyway. He could not stop checking, and the checking was eating him, and there was nothing I could say that the place did not immediately turn sour in my mouth before I could say it.

Because it was getting into me too, and it had found the door it wanted.

I am good at not thinking about things. I have said this already and it is the truest thing about me and it is, I understand now, the reason the Snake Forest nearly had me — because a place whose whole craft is making you think about things had, in me, found a person who had spent her whole life building a door against exactly that, and there is nothing a pressure loves like a door. It got my father first. It got him up out of wherever I keep him and stood him in front of me on the trail — not a memory, nothing so kind as a memory, just the fact of him, the *elsewhere* of him, the question I never let myself ask about why elsewhere had always been so easy for him to choose. Then it got the inn, and Tondi, and the needles over my heart, and the cold arithmetic of how unlikely it was that I would ever use them anywhere she would hear how it went. Then it got the simplest, worst one, the one I had been not-thinking since the gate: *what are you doing out here. You serve stew. You are going to die in the green for a wooden man's quarrel and no one will ever find the pieces.*

"He's not even one of us," Roan said.

I don't know how long he'd been walking with that in him before it came out. It came out quiet and reasonable and absolutely poisonous, and it was aimed at Third's back, ten paces ahead.

"He doesn't feel it," Roan said. "Look at him. Walks through here like it's a meadow. He doesn't feel any of it, and he's the reason we're in it, and we're going to leave our minds in these trees so he can chase a — a thing he isn't even sure is real—"

"Roan." It came out of me harder than I meant, and meaner, and the meanness felt *good*, which was how I knew. "Shut your mouth."

"You shut yours. You've wanted to say it too. I've watched you—"

"I have known you four days and every one of them you've needed someone to tell you you're brave—"

I heard it land. I watched it land on him, the exact cruel place I'd aimed it without deciding to aim anywhere, and the small hot satisfaction of having hurt him curdled even as it arrived, and that was the thing, that was the whole horror of the place laid bare in a sentence: it did not invent anything. It had nothing to put in us that we had not brought in ourselves. It only reached in, and found the unkindest true thing, and helped us say it.

*  *  *

"Stop walking," Third said.

He had turned around. The glow of his eye moved over the two of us — Roan with his spear half come up off his shoulder, me with my hands in fists I didn't remember making — and whatever he read there, he set himself down on the path between us and the green ahead, facing back the way we'd look, and he said:

"Listen to me, because the trees would prefer that you did not. They do not move. I have been watching them since we entered, every one within reach of my eye, without once looking away — I do not need to look away; it is among the few advantages of being made the way I was made. They have not moved. Not one of them, not the width of a hair. The thing in your minds telling you otherwise is the only danger in this place, and it is lying, and I am the proof of it. You do not have to watch the trees. I will watch the trees. That is mine. Yours is only to walk, and to know that what you have just said to each other was said by the forest, in your voices, because it has no voice of its own."

Nobody said anything for a moment.

"I'm sorry," Roan said, to the ground.

"Me too," I said, and meant it, and felt the meaning come back to me from a long way off, like something thawing.

"You will both be sorry several more times before we are clear," Third said, and stood, and something in the flatness of it was almost — not a joke. Third did not make jokes. But the shape where a joke would go, offered to us deliberately, like a hand. "It is the nature of the place. Apologise as often as you need to. It is cheaper than the alternative, and I will not keep count."

So we walked, and Third walked ahead of us facing backward as often as forward, his eye on the trees, taking the watching off us and onto himself, and it helped. It genuinely helped, to be relieved of the trees, to have one fixed certain thing — *he is watching; if they move he will say* — to lean the mind against.

It was perhaps an hour after that, in the worst of the grey afternoon, that I heard Third say, low, not to me:

"No."

I looked up. He was facing forward, the eye on the path, and there was no one he could have been answering.

"No," he said again, quieter, the way you'd answer a thing that had asked the same question many times before and would ask it many more. And then a word I didn't catch, and then nothing, and his hand had closed and opened once at his side, the way Roan's kept closing on his spear.

"Third?" I said. "Who are you talking to?"

The eye came to me. For a moment — only a moment — the glow had a hardness in it I hadn't seen since the night the wounded traveller spoke of the thing that walked through the dead, a held thing, a door of his own with something leaning on it from the other side.

"Myself," he said. "It is an old conversation. The forest has merely turned up its volume." The glow eased. "Do not be concerned. I have been refusing this particular argument for a very long time. The trees have nothing to teach me about it that I have not already declined to learn."

He didn't explain, and I had no idea then what the argument was, or who the other voice in it belonged to, or that it had been speaking to him without rest since before my grandmother's grandmother served her first stew. I only knew that whatever it was, the Snake Forest had found his door the same as it had found mine and Roan's — and that the difference between us was not that the place could not reach him.

It was that he had had three hundred thousand spins of practice keeping the door shut.

*  *  *

We came out of it the way we'd gone in, with no edge to mark the leaving, the bent trunks giving way tree by tree to honest crooked ordinary ones, until I looked up and the forest behind us was just a forest and the thing in my head had let go its grip so gently I couldn't say when.

We made camp early, well clear of the last of the bent trees, and none of us suggested going further though there was light left. Roan built the fire too big. I let him. We didn't talk about the things we'd said, but Roan took first watch without being asked, and when I woke in the dark he was sitting close to the coals with his spear across his knees, and he looked over at me, and in the firelight he looked like a child — younger than his spins, young enough that guarding anything seemed a cruel joke to have handed him.

"It wasn't true," he said. "What you said. I mean — it was. But it wasn't the why of it. I don't need someone to tell me I'm brave." He poked the fire. "I need to find out if I am. They turn out to be different things, when you're actually out here. I didn't know that before."

"It wasn't true what I said either," I told him. "You're the least cowardly person I've ever watched be frightened. That's a better thing than not being frightened. Mathen would have a line for it." I stopped. I didn't know who Mathen was. I had never heard the name. It had come up out of me from nowhere, the way the bent trees came up out of the ground, and I put it down to the forest, to a day of having my mind turned over, and I let it go.

Across the fire, Third sat with his eye on the dark, watching nothing, watching everything, keeping a door shut that I would not understand for a long time yet.

In the morning the trail was clear and the trees were honest and we walked on toward the geysers, where everything would change again, though we didn't know that yet either.

*  *  *


# Thirteen

There was a shard tree at the edge of our camp that first night past the Snake Forest, and the pull of it told me I still hadn't entirely learned my lesson. I knew them by now. We had come up through a whole forest of them on the causeway road, and that was where I'd learned what the prettiest thing on that road costs you — I still wore the thin white line across the back of my hand where one had taught me. But the forest had been a thousand of them, a roof of glass twenty men overhead, beauty and slaughter in one breath. This was a single tree, standing a little apart from the honest ones, slimmer, paler, its leaves long and narrow and the colour of dark honey; and when the night breeze moved them — gently, only gently — they made a sound I have never heard anything else make: a thin bright tinkling, like a shelf of glass cups touched all at once, delicate and lovely and entirely out of place in a wood at night. I got up and took a step toward it before the back of my hand reminded me, and I sat down again. One of the few beautiful things on that road that would also open your throat — and I had no intention of finding out whether the wind meant to stay soft. So I let it sing from where I was. I stayed by the banked fire, where Roan was a long breathing shape under his blanket, and I listened to the glass leaves sing, and across the camp Third sat his watch with his eye on the dark, and after a while I noticed that he had stopped watching the dark.

He was watching the tree.

I had begun, by then, to learn the difference between Third's stillnesses. There was the watchful one, which was most of them, the eye tracking, the body a held readiness. And there was the other one — the one I'd first really seen on the causeway, when he'd come back from the carts diminished — where the stillness went *down* into him somehow, where the glow of the eye softened and turned its attention to a place that wasn't the place the rest of us were in. He was doing the second one now. Watching the singing tree, the eye gone soft and far — and then, between one breath of mine and the next, he was not there. I want to be careful about this, because I was three-quarters asleep and the fire was low. But I do not think I dreamed it. One moment his still shape sat against the dark with the glass leaves singing over it; the next, the place by the fire was simply empty, in a way my eye slid off and would not hold; and then — no more than a breath later, before the leaves had finished their phrase — he was back, sitting exactly as he'd sat, as though he had never moved. In the only time that counted to us, he never had: whatever while he spent away, he spent where time runs faster than it has any business to, and stepped home into the gap of a single breath. I didn't understand it then. I thought I'd nodded and missed him shifting. I know now that he had stepped — gone bodily out of the world to where they were, because the glass leaves were singing and the glass leaves had sung that day too, and after three hundred thousand spins he would step a little way beside the world and be with them again, and that it cost him almost no time at all, and that this was the cruellest mercy he had ever been given.

&nbsp;

<p align="center">◆ &nbsp; ◆ &nbsp; ◆</p>


The child would not eat meat, and this was a problem, because Third had nothing else to offer and no understanding of what else there might be.

He had carried it three spins by then, the small green thing in its stolen sling, and it had made its needs known in the only language it had, which was volume, and Third had learned to read the volume the way he had learned everything in those days — by trial, and by failure, and by the slow accretion of a competence no one had built him to have. Raw meat, refused. Water, accepted, with suspicion. A paste he made of river-tubers mashed between two flat stones, accepted, and then worn, much of it, and then — this was the part the unbidden voice found most contemptible and the part Third found, privately, the most interesting — produced again at the far end some hours later, without warning or apology, down the length of his arm.

*It is consuming your time and your resources,* the voice observed, as it observed most things, flatly, certainly, without malice and without end. *It would be more efficient to set it down.*

"I have heard you," Third told it, scraping his arm with a leaf. "You are very consistent."

The child watched him clean himself, and made a sound that was not quite a word and not quite a laugh, and Third went still, because it was the first sound it had made that was aimed *at* him rather than at the indifferent sky, and he did not yet have anywhere to put what that did.

Her name came out of his, in a manner of speaking — out of the only thing he had ever called her. He had no name for her and felt no need of one; she was the child, which was accurate, and accuracy was the whole of what he asked of words in those days. But on the long walks he had taken to talking to her, low, to keep the volume down, and the thing he said most, when he lifted her or settled her or warned her back from an edge, was *little one.* Little one. It was not a name. It was only true.

And one day, reaching up at him from the sling with both green hands, pleased with herself, she gave it back to him in the shape her mouth could manage: "Li-wan."

"Little one," he said.

"*Li-wan,*" she insisted, as though correcting *him,* and would not be moved, and that was that. He had said a true thing to her a thousand times and she had handed it back to him broken into a name, and he found — turning the discovery over, the way he turned over every new thing the child made him feel — that he liked the broken version better than the true one. That accuracy had stopped, somewhere on those walks, being the only thing he wanted from a word.

*  *  *

Wally came out of the stories.

Third should have expected it, having memory of the Hin from before, having known in some flat archived way that a tale told often enough and meant hard enough will, on Anka, sometimes get up and walk. But knowing a thing as fact is not the same as turning from the fire one evening to find that the fact has acquired a face.

And a beard. It had started because Liwon would not sleep, and Third had no songs — songs were not in him — so he told her instead the only stories he had, which were the things he knew: the trees, the weather, the shapes of the dangerous world. He told them as stories because she would not take them as warnings. He gave the Shard trees a voice and the Squid trees a temper and the sleep-breathing shrubs a sly cowardice, and he told her the same ones over and over because she demanded the same ones over and over, and somewhere in the thirtieth-plus spin of this a small person was sitting at the edge of the firelight listening too.

He was about Liwon's height, which is to say small, and he had the look of someone who had always been there, and he was already laughing at the part of the Squid-tree story that Liwon always laughed at, on cue, as though he had heard it as many times as she had, which — Third came to understand — in every way that mattered, he had.

"You tell it wrong," the small person said, comfortably, to a being made to end his kind. "The Squid tree wouldn't say *that.* It'd say it grumbling. Like this." And he said it grumbling, and Liwon shrieked with delight, and Third sat with the particular stillness of a construct revising its model of the world in real time.

"You are Hin," Third said.

"I'm Wally," said Wally, as if these were the same thing, which for him they were. He had arrived complete — no infancy, no arrival, simply a yesterday in which he had not been and a today in which he had, and a perfect untroubled certainty that this was the normal way for a person to begin. He remembered the stories as his own childhood because the stories were, precisely, his childhood. He had been made of them. He found Third's careful questions about *when* and *how* very funny, and answered them the way you'd answer a child asking where the wind keeps its things.

Third did not tell either of them what he privately concluded, which was that he had talked a person into being out of grief and golden firelight and the need to keep one child asleep, and that this was either the best thing he had ever done or a thing he had no right to have done, and that he could not tell which, and that the not-telling had begun to feel familiar.

*  *  *

She learned the trees the way other children learn rhymes.

"Da goad ones," Liwon recited, marching beside him on the stone road with Wally marching behind doing an exaggerated version of her march, "have leefs dat hurt in wind. Or when dey drop." A solemn pause. "Da ones wif green smoke can stop my breaving. An' da ones wif da eye at da bottom is *safe.*" Triumphant. "'Cept when hurt."

"Good," Third said. "Yes. And when are they hurt?"

"When sumfin *bites* dem," she said, with relish, biting the air.

"When something bites them. Yes."

It had taken him, with the voice helping, the better part of their first spins together to learn to read the dangers of the wood. It took her a handful of tellings, and Wally fewer than that, and Third walked the Titan road behind the two small persons he had — there was no other honest word for it, and he had stopped fighting the word — *made,* one carried out of a massacre and one talked out of the dark, and he listened to them get the rhymes right, and the voice in his head said *this is not the purpose,* and Third said, internally, where only the voice could hear, *no. It is better than the purpose,* and the voice, for once, had nothing to add.

*  *  *

The village took her in the end, as he had always known a village would have to.

He found it by following the road to where the road found people — a Virek place, walled the way they walled, the tree-trunk spikes that always looked to Third like a porcupine's failed idea — and he came to it because Liwon was growing, and growing needed things he was not built to give: others of her kind, hands that knew what her body would do next, a place that did not move every dawn. He had not been able to give her stillness. In all the spins since his waking — and they were not so many, then; he was still new enough to be counting them — it was the one thing he had never once managed to be.

A woman came out to him. Zala. She was unarmed and unafraid in a way that the men on the wall, with their bows, very much were not, and she looked at the green child on the back of the thing that had been built to murder green children, and she did the arithmetic faster than her own people could, and what she landed on was not *monster.* What she landed on was a question she asked gently, already half-knowing: "You've been caring for her?"

"As best I was able," Third said. "My limits are becoming apparent as she ages. That is the only reason I have come."

She let them in. It took the men longer — an arrow came, badly aimed, and Liwon cried at the shouting, and Wally went and stood very deliberately between the child and the wall with his small bearded chin up, which did nothing and meant everything — but Zala's voice carried more than her rank, and in the end the gate held open and the three of them went through it, and that was where Liwon began, by slow degrees, to belong to someone other than him.

He watched it happen over the spins that followed. He had asked for it. He had walked her here precisely so that it would happen. And still there was a thing in him — he located it eventually in the place the bellows lived, low in the wooden cage of his chest — that registered each step of her moving toward them and away from him, the way you'd register a slow tide going out: not as a wound, exactly. As a temperature. As the world getting cooler by a degree you only noticed in sum.

She ran to the Virek children now, not to him. She learned their words faster than the ones he'd given her, and then better than them, and then she dreamed in them, which he knew because Wally told him, Wally who did not sleep much either and who sat with Third on the long nights and reported the small treasons of growing up with no cruelty and no comfort, just the plain Hin-honesty of a person who could not imagine why a true thing would hurt.

"She still asks for the tree story," Wally offered once, into one of Third's silences. "The one you tell wrong."

"Does she."

"I tell it now. I tell it right." A pause. "She says mine isn't as good." Wally seemed to find this satisfactory in a way Third was slow to follow. "She means yours. She means she wants *yours.* She just doesn't know that's what she means yet."

Third sat with that a while, on the wall he was permitted to walk now, an honorary warden of a village of the people he'd been forged to end, watching the dark for the things that came out of it.

"It is as it should be," he said, at last. He had decided to believe it. He had given her a place to be still in, which was the one gift he had never been able to give her himself, and a people whose hands knew her, and a future that did not move every dawn. That it cost him the degree of warmth he had only just learned he could hold — that was a fair price, and he was, he told himself, content to have paid it.

He believed it almost entirely.

The almost is the part the grey keeps. The almost is what was waiting for him under the singing of the glass leaves, all those spins later, on a road with a Humin girl asleep beside a fire and a whole catastrophe still ahead of them both that he had not yet let himself walk back into.

He stayed in the warm part. Just this once, he let himself stay in the warm part, and watch her get the rhyme right, and not go forward to the night the sky filled with red veins.

He was permitted that much. The grey is patient. It would still be there when he stepped back.

&nbsp;

<p align="center">◆ &nbsp; ◆ &nbsp; ◆</p>


I woke fully near the end of his watch and he was Third again — the ordinary watchful stillness back in him, the eye on the dark, the night unremarkable. But the glow had gone down to its lowest ember, the banked-fire dimness I had seen on the causeway, and when he turned his head to mark me waking, it took a moment to come back up, the way a person surfaces from a long way down.

"You went somewhere," I said. I didn't mean it as a question and it didn't come out as one.

The eye held on me.

"Yes," he said.

I waited to see if there was more, the way I'd learned to wait. The glass leaves sang. Roan turned over and muttered and slept on.

"Was it somewhere good?" I asked. I don't know why I asked it that way. It seemed, suddenly, the only thing worth knowing.

Third was quiet for a long moment, the ember of his eye steady on the singing tree.

"It was good," he said, "for a while. The good ones are. That is what makes them the ones worth walking back into." And then, before I could find the next question: "Sleep, Tasha. You have the dawn watch, and we walk hard tomorrow. We are close to the geysers now."

I slept. I did not know what geysers meant yet, or who we'd find there, or that the warm part of whatever Third had gone into had an end he hadn't shown me. I only knew that he had gone somewhere good and come back lessened, and that the two facts sitting together bothered me more than either would have alone, and I carried that small wrongness down into sleep and did not solve it.

I wouldn't solve it for a long time.

*  *  *


# Fourteen

You smell the geysers before you see them, and the smell is the first argument the place makes that you should not be there. Hot copper and wet stone and something underneath that's almost sweet, the way meat is almost sweet just before it turns — a red smell, if a smell can have a colour, and this one could.

Then you come over the rise and there they are: a field of chimneys. Not built ones — grown ones, tapering spires of pale mineral four and five humins high, hollow, rough-sided, the spit and image of the great termite towers you sometimes find in dry country, except that these wept. From the open tops of the living ones a fine continuous spray went up, on no schedule and from no pump anyone could name, and fell back red, and ran down the outsides, and dried as it went to something else entirely — a crust the colour of nothing I had a word for, a blue-white that was almost clear, glassy, catching the grey light and throwing it back cold. The red ran. The blue-white stayed.

And the red collected, at the foot of each living tower, in a pool.

That was where the people were — a dozen of them, kneeling not under the spray but at the pools' edges, well back from the falling rain, dipping wooden things into the still red surface and drawing them out darker. Anointing. Turning a carved stick into a blade that could open you. Nobody, I noticed, let the spray touch them. Mathen told me later why, in the flat way he delivered the facts a body ought to have before it did something stupid: wet on a blade, the blood makes hexwood. Wet on a person, it makes a very brief and very dangerous lunatic. You take it from the pool, where it's gone still and you can choose how much of you it touches. You never, ever stand in the rain.

The pools fed things. That was the next thing I saw — that each red pool had a ring of growth around it, and the growth was wrong: too dark, too glossy, fat-leaved and faintly moving when there was no wind, fed fat on the blood of a dead god and grown into shapes that had clearly stopped asking permission of the ordinary rules. Around two of the pools the growth had closed over entirely, a thicket nobody was working, nodding to itself.

And the dead towers. The oldest had sealed at the top, capped in blue-white glass, gone silent among the spraying ones like grave-markers among the still-breathing — and at the foot of each dead one the pool had dried too, dried and gone black, a flat black sheet of the same glass veining away from the tower in cracks across the pale ground, reaching, I saw now, toward the black cracks of the next dead pool over, and the next, so that the whole field was stitched beneath its living red with a dried black web of all the red that had ever pooled here and stopped. I had seen those black cracks before. Everyone has. They run through the whole world. I had simply never stood at the place where one was still wet.

"This is where hexwood comes from," Roan said, quiet, the way you'd speak in a place that might be holy or might be a slaughterhouse and you hadn't decided which.

"It is one place," Third said. "There are many. They do not last." He looked across the field at the sealed silent towers, the dead ones, with something I would not have called grief on a face that could make the expression, but which sat where grief would sit. "Each one closes itself, in time. The spray builds the tower, and the tower builds its own lid, and one spin the red stops coming and the place is only stone. Then I find the next." A pause. "This one is free. I have kept a great many free, over the spins. They keep ending the argument by dying."

He said *free* with a small weight on it, and I learned the rest of why over the next while, watching him.

*  *  *

There were two reasons Third had brought us a day off our line to a field that smelled of the inside of a wound, and he gave them both without my asking, which was his way when a thing mattered to him — he stated his reasons plainly, as if daring you to find them foolish.

The first was the blood itself. He needed it. I watched him do a thing I'd never seen him do and have never quite stopped seeing since: he stood at one of the weeping towers, away from the others, and held a wad of fresh-cut wood into the red pool until it darkened and drew up all it could, and then he opened a seam along his own forearm — the vine-cord that served him for muscle parting under his fingers like a purse — and for half a breath, before he packed the soaked wood into the worn place beneath, I saw inside him.

I am not going to be able to tell it properly. There were gears in there, and rods, turning, and the trouble was not that they were strange — I had expected strange. The trouble was that they did not *work.* Not work as in broken; work as in *make sense.* They turned against each other in ways that should have locked solid or flown apart, drove nothing I could follow into nothing I could find, and the longer my eye tried to trace one rod to the thing it turned the more something behind my eyes began to ache, a low sick wrongness, like a word you've said so many times it stops being a word. I looked away. I had to. Whatever kept Third moving was not clockwork, however much it wore the shape of it. Clockwork you can follow. This you could only flinch from, and understand that it went on turning whether you flinched or not.

He packed the soaked wood into himself, into the worn place beneath, the way you'd pack a wound except he was packing the wound *with* the thing that had made the world bleed, and the seam drew closed over the part I couldn't look at, and I breathed again. He saw me watching.

"I am made of anointed wood," he said. "The same as their blades. I wear, as the blood drains and then the wood wears. So I mend, as wood is mended. The pool gives me that, and the pool is free." He pressed the forearm seam flat. "That is the easy mending."

Then he did the other thing, and the other thing was worse, because the other thing he could not finish.

He opened his chest. Not wide — a hand's-span seam down the centre of him, drawn back the way the arm had drawn back — and inside, past more of the turning that hurt to look at, were two small lights. Blue-white. The cold clear blue-white of the dried crust on the dead towers, the blue-white that stayed. They sat in him like a pair of held breaths, side by side, and one burned steady and one had gone dim, guttered down to a wick-end of itself, and Third looked at the dim one a moment the way you look at a thing you have no answer for.

"This, the pool cannot give me," he said. "The wet mends the body. The lights want the dried — and not the dried that crusts a tower, which is too young and too rough, but the made dried, the kind that comes only from places I cannot simply walk up to and dip a cup. So I check them, here, where it is quiet. I watch the dim one. I calculate how long it has." He drew the seam closed over the two lights, and they were gone behind him, and I understood without being told that I had just been shown the most private thing about him and possibly the most dangerous. "Long enough, for now. But not long. A bottle this size burns sixty spins, eighty if I hold still, and then it is a dead coal and I am down to one, hunting the next before that one goes too. The big vessels last — a made jar the size of your two fists would last me lifetimes — but those the makers keep, or sell to things that can pay in coin or worse, and a thing like me takes what it can scrounge: small ones, a few at a time, never enough ahead. I have been hunting the next since before your people had a word for the colour. They are the part of me I did not make, and cannot make, and that is a difficult thing to carry, in a world where the only people who make the dried keep the making a secret and would like very much to charge me for it."

I didn't have anything to say to that. The thing that hardened the swords kept his body whole, for free, at any weeping pool. But the thing that kept him *alive* — the two cold lights — came from somewhere else, somewhere fenced and priced and held close, and he carried two of them and one was going dim, and he had decided not to be afraid about it, the way he decided most things, by stating them plainly and walking on.

The second reason I worked out for myself, from the way the working people glanced at him and then eased, and the way two hard-looking men with a hand-cart and a ledger came over the far rise, looked at the field, looked at the Forged standing among the towers in the middle of it, and turned their cart around.

"The men with the ledger," I said. "You knew them."

"Not them. Their employer." The eye tracked the cart until it was gone. "There is a guild that would like to put a fence around this field and a gate in the fence and a price on the gate. They have done it to three fields I know of. They have not managed it here." A pause. "I find I am here a great deal, when I can be. I am difficult to fence. And a field that something like me visits without warning is a poor investment for an employer who wants to charge admission." The glow settled, level and dry.

It was more than standing there, though. I watched him do the rest of it before we left — go to the two choked pools, the ones ringed round with the fat dark growth that had closed over and stopped anyone working them, and take a Falka to the thicket. Not hacking. Methodical, the way he did everything, cutting the wrong-shaped stuff back to the dirt and dragging it clear and stacking it to dry for burning, until the red surface showed again and a person could kneel to it without putting a hand into something that moved. The growth had teeth of a kind — a vine came round at his arm twice and he simply held it till it gave up, untiring, the way he'd cleared the carts — and when he was done two dead pools were working pools again, free for whoever came next.

"They grow back fast on the blood," he said, when he caught me watching. "A fenced field, a man would clear them once and charge you to use what he cleared. An unfenced one, someone has to clear them for nothing, every time, or the field closes itself to the poor before it ever closes itself for good." He wiped the Falka on the grass. "It is a small thing. The world should have a few free things in it. I have decided this is one of them. It costs me a detour."

That was the most I'd ever heard Third say about wanting anything. He wanted there to be a place where the poor could harden a blade for nothing, and he kept it open by the simple act of being too strange and too old to bill. I was still turning that over when the shouting started.

*  *  *

It started as the ordinary shouting of a man who has done something stupid and is refusing, loudly, to admit it.

"I'm *not* touching it!" — this from a Humin a few towers over, on his feet, a freshly dark blade in one fist and the other fist trailing red drops he plainly hadn't noticed. He was arguing with Third, who had risen and taken a few unhurried steps back, his glaive coming up not as a threat but the way you raise an arm between a child and a fire.

"You have it on your hand," Third said. "It is running off your knuckles. In a moment it will be in you, through the small cuts every hand has, and then we will not be able to continue this conversation, because you will be trying to kill me. I would prefer we skipped that part."

"I've anointed a dozen blades, I know how to—"

He didn't finish. I watched it take him. It came up through him like a tide finding a low field — his face going from indignant to confused to something with no person left behind it, the jaw setting, the eyes flattening — and the man who'd been protesting his competence a breath before came at Third with the new blade and a sound I don't want to describe, all the fury a body can hold and none of the aim.

Roan started forward. Third said, "No," without looking, and Roan stopped, and we watched.

I have tried to describe what Third did and the words keep coming out wrong, because every word for it is a fighting word and what Third did was the opposite of fighting. The man swung to open him from shoulder to hip. Third wasn't there — not dodged, exactly; *declined*, the way water declines a fist. The blade slid the length of the glaive's shaft and found only air at the end of it, and Third had turned a quarter-turn so the man's own fury carried him past, and then it happened again, and again, the man spending himself in great wild arcs and Third spending nothing, a quarter-turn here, the shaft tilted there, a thing that looked less like combat than like a very patient person walking a large dog that believed it was walking him.

And the whole time — I only understood this later, when I knew to look — the whole time, Third's free hand kept closing and opening at his side. Closing and opening. The way it had in the Snake Forest, when he'd said *no* to something none of us could hear. The voice was in him even here, I think, telling him the eight quick ways to end the man, and Third was answering it the only way he ever did, which was to keep choosing not to, one quarter-turn at a time, for as long as it took.

It took about a minute. The man folded onto the stained stone, the blade thumping down beside him, his chest heaving, the fury gone out of him as fast as it had come.

"You will be yourself again after thirty deep breaths or so," Third told him, not unkindly. "Most Humins are merely felled by it. You stayed upright and swinging for some time. That is unusual. You should not feel badly about failing to land a strike — I have been studying the art since before this field had a name."

Pale moths had come up off the pools while we watched — I noticed them only now, broad soft wings the colour of the dried crust, drifting in slow clouds above the red where the spray kept the air warm and wet. They were the loveliest thing in that whole red field, and I had learned, by then, to distrust that. "They drink the spray and carry it," Third said, to me, though his eye was on Mathen, folded and breathing on the stained stone. "A crushed wing, a swallowed moth, and a man takes a smaller helping of what you just had — enough to sour him for an hour, not enough to fell him." He let that sit. "It could have been worse, swordsman. A moth might have found you somewhere I was not — a market, a road, among people who cannot decline a blade the way I can. You would not have failed to land your strikes there. You'd have succeeded, on a local, or two, before it passed — and woken thirty breaths later a different kind of man than the one who lay down." Mathen didn't answer. He looked at the moths a long while. So did I.

*  *  *

He came back to himself the way you surface from a bad dream, all at once and ashamed. He got an elbow under himself and looked at the blade, and at Third, and at the two of us, and I watched him decide what kind of man he was going to be about it, which is a thing you can actually watch some people do.

"I'd apologise," he said, "but I think a thank-you's the more honest coin. You could've split me like kindling and I'd have had it coming." He worked his jaw. "I thought I'd taken every safe step. Turns out I'd taken every safe step but one, and it's always the one."

"No apology is needed," Third said. "And you are welcome."

"Mathen." He got himself the rest of the way up, waving off the hand Third didn't quite offer. "Known far and wide as Mathen, swordsman extraordin—" He stopped. He looked at the blade on the ground, and at the Forged who'd just made his finest fury look like weather. "Known far and wide as Mathen," he amended. "The extraordinary part could use some paring, I'll grant you, having just paraded it about for the whole field."

"You were measuring yourself against an experienced opponent," Third said. "Against most others the moniker might hold."

It was, I realised, a kindness — Third doling out the exact amount of dignity the situation could bear and not a grain more, because more would have been a lie and Third did not lie. Mathen heard it for what it was. Something eased in his face. And I felt the thing happen that I didn't have a name for yet: the air of our small grim company changed, the way a room changes when someone opens a window you didn't know was painted shut.

He asked to see the glaive. Third let him, on the condition he could see Mathen's blade in turn, and the two of them stood in the bloody field passing weapons back and forth like boys trading knucklebones. Mathen's was a good blade, an heirloom — his father's chip-carving under his own, he said, three generations of marks before he'd judged it worthy of the red. He was proud of it and trying not to be, which made me like him.

Then he turned Third's glaive in his hands, and went still, the way people went still around Third when the wrongness of him landed.

"This is old work," Mathen said slowly. "This flourish, here — nobody carves like this. This is from—" He looked up. "Who made this?"

"The one who made me," Third said.

"And who repaired it? Since. It'd have to have been repaired, wood doesn't—"

"It has never been repaired. It is the glaive I was created holding. I have carried it since my waking."

Mathen laughed, the easy laugh of a man waiting for the rest of a joke. Then he didn't. He looked at the glaive, and at Third's forearm where I'd watched the seam close, and at the single off-centre eye of him, the body visibly mended a hundred times over and the weapon in it untouched, original, older than mended.

"Your waking," Mathen said carefully. "When was your waking."

"When the last of the Titan fell," Third said. "I was on the field. I felt the dust come down. I have been awake since."

I knew it already — or I'd been told it, which I was learning is not the same as knowing. But I watched Mathen do the sum the way I should have done it long before, watched him reach for a number and find that the numbers ran out before the answer did, watched a swordsman who'd been proud of three generations of marks on a blade stand in front of a thing that remembered the day the sky was torn. He didn't go pale. He went *quiet*, which on Mathen, I'd come to learn, was the louder reaction.

"Right," he said, at last, very softly. "Right. So when you said I shouldn't feel badly about not landing a strike." A breath of something that wanted to be a laugh and wasn't quite. "You were being generous."

"I was being accurate," Third said. "The generosity was a separate thing, offered alongside."

*  *  *

He didn't ask to come with us. That's the part I keep, looking back — nobody recruited anybody.

He helped us refill our water, because he knew the clean seeps at the field's edge from the tainted run-off and we didn't. He told us, unprompted, the trick of telling anointed water from clean. He walked us to the far edge of the field naming the people who came regular, which ones would trade fair and which would short you, and somewhere in the naming it stopped being directions for travellers passing through and started being the talk of someone who'd decided, without announcing it, to keep walking the same direction we were walking.

It was Third who said it, finally, in the flat way he said the things that mattered.

"We are going anti-spinward, to stop a man who is gathering the tools of the Titans, with which he intends to call one back. It is dangerous, and most who hear it do not believe the danger, and of those who believe it, most very sensibly decline to come. You should know what you would be following, before you follow it any further."

Mathen was quiet a moment. The geysers breathed their red breath behind us.

"A man trying to wake a Titan," he said. "And a Forged who's been awake since the last one fell, walking out to stop him." He shouldered his pack. "Ser, I have been looking my whole life for a story worth telling, and you've just told me there's one walking up the road, and you expected me to let it walk off without me?" He fell in beside Roan as if the matter were long settled. "I'd never forgive myself. And I'm a *terrible* audience for my own regrets, you'll find."

And that was how Mathen came among us — not because Third sought a companion, and not because anything was written, but because some people, shown the edge of a thing too big to refuse, simply step over it and ask what's for supper.

I caught Third watching him go ahead with Roan, the two of them already arguing amiably about something. The glow of his eye was steady, ordinary, unreadable. But he stood a moment before he followed, and I'd learned to notice when Third stood a moment.

"What," I said.

"Nothing," he said. "I am only counting. It is a habit." He started walking. "I find I have acquired three of you. I had not expected to acquire any."

He didn't say it like a complaint. He didn't quite say it like a gift, either. But it was the nearest to the second that I'd heard from him, and I walked the rest of that day with it warming a spot under my ribs that the Snake Forest had left cold.

*  *  *


# Fifteen

A party of three has a shape, and the shape is a line. We'd walked it for a tenday without my ever thinking about it: Third in front because Third was always in front, me behind him because his back was the safest place in the world to put your eyes, and Roan behind me with his spear and his checking. A line. You don't talk much in a line. You talk to the back of a head, or you don't, and mostly we hadn't.

Four made a different shape, and Mathen made it on purpose.

He'd worked out by the second morning that the line was a thing to be dismantled, and he went about it the way I'd later learn he went about everything — sideways, cheerfully, and without once announcing what he was doing. He'd fall back and walk with Roan a while and get him talking about the militia, about the town, about the girl Roan never quite named; then he'd drift up and walk with me and ask the kind of question that has no defensible reason not to answer, so that I'd answer it before I'd decided to; then he'd lengthen his stride and pull even with Third, which nobody had done, and just *walk there*, companionable, as if walking beside the oldest thing in the world were a thing a person did.

By the fourth day the line was gone. We walked in a loose knot that rearranged itself like weather, and there was talk in it, the ordinary unimportant talk that I hadn't known a group could make until I heard ours start making it, and I understood that this was a thing Mathen *did* — that he was, in some way I didn't have a trade-word for, a mender too. Tondi fixed seams. Mathen fixed the spaces between people. He'd just walked into the middle of three strangers and begun quietly stitching them into a company.

*  *  *

He cooked, too, and he cooked *well*, which after a tenday of my road-fat-and-resentment was its own small revolution. The first night he took over the fire was the night we camped under the wall.

I call it a wall. It was a wall the way the causeway was a bridge — a thing that wore the word loosely and was really something else. It rose out of the grassland a half-day spinward of us, sheer and seamless and grey, as high as a hill and longer than sight, and it went *nowhere.* It did not ring a thing, or hold back a thing, or join a thing. It started out of flat ground at one end and stopped at flat ground at the other and in between it was simply a wall, a colossal straight nothing the Titan had begun and never finished, or finished and never explained, and the wind came round its end with a low constant note like someone blowing across a jug the size of the world. We camped in the lee of it because the lee was out of the wind. That was the only use it had ever been to anyone, and it had not been built for it, and a hundred generations of travellers had warmed themselves against the side of a thing whose entire purpose had died with the child that wanted it.

And there, in the windbreak of an abandoned god-sized whim, the first night he took over the fire, Mathen produced from the same dried fish and tubers I'd been ruining something a person would willingly eat twice, and he did it while talking the entire time, and he served Third a portion.

"I don't eat," Third said. It was not a complaint. Third stated facts about himself the way he stated facts about the weather.

"I know," Mathen said, setting the bowl down by Third's hoof anyway. "But a man cooks for his company, and you're my company, and if I leave you out it'll show on the count. Let it go cold. Let the smell do the work. My gran used to set a bowl for her brother forty spins after he passed, and nobody ever told her it was strange, because it wasn't." He sat. "You can give it to the guard when I'm not looking. He's a dozen kinds of hungry."

"I'm seven thousand spins old," Roan said, around a mouthful, with the dignity of a grown man who has been a guardsman for some spins and would like that noticed.

"You're a growing boy," said Mathen serenely, "and you've a growing boy's appetite, and I respect it," and Roan, who'd spent four spins trying to be taken seriously by an actual swordsman, found himself grinning at being called a boy, which is a difficult trick to do to a grown man and Mathen did it without seeming to try.

*  *  *

The trouble started, as it would keep starting, with a story.
