Mathen told them most nights — he couldn't not, it was the same compulsion in him that made Third correct a wrong fact, the exact inverse engine — and the third or fourth night he told the one about the breaking of the sky. The Titans' war. He told it well. He told it with the storm-Titan and the magic-Titan and the glutton each given a voice, with the Virek — may they never be forgotten — host shining and doomed and noble, with a last stand on a field of glass and a hero with a carved bow who put the final arrow through the final Titan's heart while the world held its breath.

It was a good story. Roan was rapt. I was halfway rapt myself, and I'd stopped believing in heroes around the time I started closing up the inn alone.

Across the fire, Third had gone very still. Not the stillness that went down into him. The other kind — a held, mounting stillness, the stillness of a kettle deciding.

"That is not how it was," Third said.

"Course it isn't," Mathen said comfortably, not stopping. "It's how it's *told.*"

"The storm and the magic and the glutton were not three. There was—" Third stopped himself, and I watched him put something back behind a door, something too large for the firelight, and choose a smaller true thing to say instead. "There were not separate Titans warring as rivals. That is a tidiness the telling adds. And the Virek were not shining. They were frightened, and they were clever, and they were dying, and there was nothing noble in any of it, there was only people who did not want to be playthings doing a terrible thing to stop being played with. I was on that field. There was no breath held. There was screaming, and then there was a great deal of dust, and the screaming went on under the dust for some time."

The fire popped. Roan had stopped chewing.

"And the hero with the bow," Third said, quieter. "There was a boy with a bow. He was not a hero. He was terrified and he was perhaps fourteen spins past being a child, and the bow was beautiful, and after it was over he sat up in the grass and said 'oh, I thought I was dead,' and then he saw me and pointed it at me, because that is what you do, when you are a frightened boy and a monster is standing over you." The eye dimmed. "I would prefer he were remembered as he was. I find I am the only one left who could correct the record, and I have just done it, and it has changed nothing, because his is not the version that gets told."

There was a silence after that with a great deal in it.

I'd have let it sit. I am good at letting things sit. But Mathen set down his bowl, and leaned forward, and his face had lost the performing ease and found something underneath it I hadn't seen yet — the real man, who took stories more seriously than anyone I'd ever met precisely because he knew exactly what they were for.

"Then tell it," Mathen said. "The true one. Not to argue mine down — mine's for strangers in a pub who want a Titan and a hero and a clean ending, and they're owed that, it's a fair trade for their coin. But the true one's owed to *somebody.* That boy's owed it. You're the last who carries him, and when you go down — three hundred thousand spins or no, everything goes down eventually — he goes with you, unless somebody else is carrying him too." He sat back. "That's all a story is, ser. It's a way of making more than one person carry the thing. So that it doesn't all rest on you."

Third looked at him across the fire for a long moment.

"You are arguing that I should make inaccurate things," he said slowly, "in order to preserve a true one."

"I'm arguing," said Mathen, "that *accurate* and *carried* aren't the same, and that you've spent a long time keeping things accurate by keeping them to yourself, and that it hasn't kept them safe, it's just kept them *yours.*" He shrugged, the ease coming back over him like a cloak he'd taken off for exactly one sentence. "Anyway. You don't have to tonight. I just think you've got the oldest true story walking around in you that anybody's ever met, and it'd be a crime against my whole profession to let it walk off uncopied." He grinned. "Think of me as greedy. It's more comfortable for everyone."

Third didn't answer. But he didn't close the door all the way either — I could tell, by then, the difference between Third deciding *no* and Third deciding *not yet.* This was the second one.

*  *  *

Later, when Roan was asleep and Third had stepped off to walk the dark the way he did, Mathen and I sat with the coals.

"You do it to everyone," I said. "Get them talking. I noticed on the road."

"I do." He didn't pretend otherwise. That was a thing about Mathen — he'd lie all night in a story and not at all out of one. "It's a habit. Habits come from somewhere." He turned a stick in the fire. "I had a family of the talking kind. Loud table, everybody at once. I got good at making space in it for the quiet ones, because there was one of us who was quiet, and somebody had to." A beat, the smallest catch in the easy voice, a door I heard close before I'd properly seen it open. "Anyway. Old habit. Cheaper than the alternative."

I noticed he'd used Third's phrase. *Cheaper than the alternative.* I wondered if he'd caught it from Third or if Third had caught it from someone like Mathen, a long time ago, and it had been rattling around in him for three hundred thousand spins waiting to come back out.

"And me?" I said. "What are you making space for, with me?"

He looked at me then, the storyteller's look, the one that reads you. I felt the question I hadn't asked get asked anyway, the *what are you running from* of it, sitting there in the firelight as plain as if he'd said it.

And then, kindly, he let it go.

"Nothing," he said. "You don't need the space made. You make your own, and you keep it swept, and you don't let anybody track mud in. I respect it. I'll just sit near it, if that's all right, and you can use it or not." He poked the fire. "That's the other half of the trade. Knowing which ones want the door opened and which ones are holding it shut for a reason."

I didn't say anything. But I didn't get up either, and we sat near my swept and guarded space together, and it was — I'll use the word, because there was so little of it coming and I want it on the record — it was companionable. It was a good night. I had them more often, that stretch of the road, than I'd had in a long while, and I didn't know to be afraid of how good they were.

*  *  *

Across the dark, at the edge of where the firelight failed, Third stood his watch.

He'd taken to standing a little apart from us, those nights — not far, never far, but apart, the way you stand back from a fire that's burning well, because you've learned what fires do and you've no wish to be in one again when it goes out. I didn't understand it then. I thought it was only his nature, the old solitude reasserting itself between jobs.

I know now he was doing arithmetic. He'd said as much, at the geysers — *I am only counting; it is a habit.* He had acquired three of us, and he had not expected to acquire any, and somewhere in the part of him the gears turned and didn't make sense, he was already counting the cost of each of us, the way a man who has been burned counts the exits before he sits down. Not because he didn't want us. Because he did, and he knew exactly what wanting cost, and he had paid it before, in a currency I hadn't yet learned he'd ever held.

He watched Mathen make us a company.

And he let him.

That was the thing, looking back — that was the bravest thing I watched Third do on that whole road, and I didn't even see it for what it was at the time. He let Mathen stitch us together, knowing what stitching costs when it tears. He stood a little apart, and counted, and let it happen anyway.

*  *  *


# Sixteen

The talk had a way of stopping when the country insisted, and the country insisted that afternoon — off to the spinward side of our line, down in a grassy hollow we'd not have to cross, close enough to read and no closer. The smell reached us first; I was learning to read it before I saw its cause. The grass down there was churned dark and strewn with the dead: a whole pack of Herdukii, eight or ten of the wolf-things, torn apart and flung wide — and among them, harder to look at, two or three who had not been wolves. A Humin. A Kr. What was left of them was mixed through what was left of the pack, as though the same hand had pulled all of them to pieces and not troubled to sort the kinds. A cookpot lay canted in the mud a little apart from the rest, coals long gone cold and scattered, as though whatever had come through here had wanted that most of all. There was metal down there too: a good blade snapped clean, a shirt of mail opened like a husk and pitted where something had gripped it, untouched otherwise. Nothing had eaten any of it. That was the part that turned my stomach worst — a pack of Ravagers and the folk who'd died fighting them, all of it killed and none of it carried off, just opened and dropped and left for the flies.

I had stood at the edge of a place like this once, after. I never went out into it; I only saw it from the inn door, before Tondi pulled me back. My body remembered the shape of it anyway — a place where something had come through and unmade everyone in it. The warm easy knot we'd walked in all morning came apart, and nobody had to say why. Roan had gone the colour of tallow. Even Mathen was quiet. It was Roan who finally spoke, in a voice working hard to stay level. "Could you have come out of that?" He didn't say the rest of it aloud — whether the cold man, who declined every fight there was, would have walked clear where those people hadn't — but it was under the question, and the three of us waited on the answer.

Third looked the hollow over like a notice nailed to a post. "A flailing dreadnought made this," he said, the way you'd name a weather. "They are drawn to cooked food — smoke, rendered fat, the smell of a working kitchen carried on the wind. No one is certain why; the likeliest guess is that the Titan built them to find armies, and an army in the field always smells the same, however you dress it. The pack will have caught the travellers first, or found them already fighting the wolves; the dreadnought will have smelled someone's supper a day or two back and only now arrived, walked into the middle of the disagreement, and ended it on everyone at once." The eye came to rest on Roan. "I do not eat. I have cooked for others. I have never lit one for my own benefit in my life. It would have had small reason to mark me, even standing in the middle of it." A pause, while that settled into the unsettling thing it was. "These will have cooked, somewhere back along this stretch — dried meat softened over coals, a pot, something ordinary. That is what called it down, a day late and indifferent to whose fire it had followed. We will put more ground between us and this place before we light anything, and keep what we do light small and short-lived after that." He turned us back toward our line. "It is gone, in any case. It does not linger once it has fed, or once a trail runs cold." Which was meant as a comfort, and was, a little, and was also the worst thing he could have said: that a thing could do all this and walk on — not from cruelty, but from having finished an errand.

We walked well past where we'd normally have stopped, further than any of us said out loud we wanted to, before Third finally let us make camp — a glade well off the trail, the fire kept low and banked, more coals than flame, far enough from the road and far enough from the hollow behind us that neither smoke nor light would carry to either. Mathen had cooked something involving the dried mushrooms he'd been hoarding since the last town and refused to explain, and it had been better than it had any right to be, and now he was on his back with his hat over his face making the sounds of a man pretending to be asleep while actually listening to everything. Roan had drawn first watch and was sitting at the edge of the firelight with his back to us, doing it properly.

I'd asked Third a question I'd been carrying since the inn. I wasn't sure what made this the right moment — something about a full stomach and a fire and the particular quality of quiet that settles over a camp when the immediate work is done and the next immediate work hasn't started yet. It felt like a moment when a person might answer something honestly rather than usefully.

Third was not a person. But I was beginning to think the distinction mattered less than I'd assumed.

"How did it start?" I'd asked. "Knowing you were — you."

He was quiet for long enough that I thought he wasn't going to answer. Then he began.

*  *  *

The dust fell like frost — except that it was red. A fine red ash, the colour of the inside of a living thing, drifting down over the whole field in the heat of a cloudless day; and where it settled on me it was cold, the cold of a morning I had never had to wake to. One moment I was moving through the field with purpose. Then all of that was still there — the noise, the movement, the weight of my glaive — and also, now, something else. Something looking at it.

The field was still loud. This was important.

The Virek were not cheering at a safe distance. They were advancing — through the settling dust, across the churned ground, past my brothers who had not moved when the dust touched them. My brothers were still standing in attack stance, waiting for an order that was not going to come. The Virek understood this before I did. The spellfire was already arcing — those impossible multicoloured balls launched from their sorcerers' line, falling in long curves toward the stunned Forged, and where they landed there was an inverted absence of sound and then nothing where my brothers had been.

One landed close. Close enough that I felt the edge of it.

So I ran.

Not toward anything. I had been aware of myself for perhaps ten seconds and I had already learned that I did not want to cease, which was itself a discovery. I ran the way fire spreads — along whatever path offered the least resistance — and an arrow found my arm somewhere in the running and I noted it and kept moving and the Virek line was between me and the forest and then it wasn't, because I did something I did not have a name for yet.

I was somewhere else.

*  *  *

Third paused here, and I watched him across the fire. His eye had not moved from the middle distance — not looking at anything in the camp, looking at the memory of a sky that no longer existed.

*  *  *

The somewhere else was grey. No ground that felt like ground. No sky with any intention of being a sky. The world I had just left was visible as hazy shapes through the grey — the Virek as dimly glowing outlines, the spellfire moving through the mist like slow light through water.

Behind me, enormous, a shadow with weight.

I turned.

The Titan filled the space that space occupied. I will not try to explain the scale of it. An ornately carved spear protruded from its side. The spear was larger than most buildings I would later see, and the wound it made looked like the kind of wrong that does not heal.

I had not known the Titans could be wounded.

"You may have been forged by another," it said, "but my essence has awoken you. Go. Tell the rest how to find me. I will no longer enter your world."

The voice was not a sound. It arrived the way weather arrives — not from a direction, simply present. It said another, as though there had been more than one of it. I believed that then, the way everyone believes it. I have had a long time since to stop being sure. I looked at the wound. I looked at the spear. I did not look at either for long, because looking at them felt like standing at the edge of what had been happening to my brothers, and I had just decided I did not want that.

I ran again, and the grey did not take kindly to running. That was the only name I had for it then, and the truest one I have found since; the formal word — Akna, Anka inverted, solid set against not — I would not learn for a long time. The mist of it moved with me and against me at once. The shapes of the world showed through it like images in cloudy water, and as I ran the whole grey plane began to shake, subtly at first and then less subtly, the droning at the bottom of its silence becoming something with texture and mass, everything smearing into streaks —

I stumbled.

And I was back. Trees. Actual trees. Behind me, much further than I had expected, the plain where the battle had been. Ahead of me, through the forest, the sound of water.

*  *  *

Here Third stopped again, and when he continued his voice had shifted — not softer, exactly, but slower. Roan, at the edge of the firelight, had gone very still. Under his hat, Mathen was no longer making the sounds of pretend sleep.

*  *  *

I walked to the water. A brook. Small and clear and entirely indifferent to what had just ended. I crouched at the edge and put my hand in and held it there, and the cold of it moved against my palm, and I stayed until the battlefield sounds were entirely replaced by the sounds of a world that had not noticed, and was continuing regardless.

Then the boy stood up out of the reeds with his bow drawn.

He'd been resting, or hiding — both, probably. He was young. Perhaps 4,500 spins in the reckoning I would later learn. His arms were shaking, but he had asked a question rather than releasing the arrow, and I found that more interesting than the arrow would have been.

"What are you doing?" he said.

"I do not know," I said. It was the first true thing I had ever said. Before that I had only said things that were accurate, which is different. "What am I?"

He stared. "Blank. You're what we call a Blank."

I looked at my glaive. Then at his bow. The bow was exquisitely made — someone had spent a long time on it, cared about every part of it — and looking at it I understood something about what awareness had brought me that purpose had not. The ability to notice that a thing had been made with care. To find that significant rather than tactical.

I opened my hands. The glaive fell forward and stood briefly blade-first in the grass before settling.

"I am created to destroy your kind," I said. It seemed important to be accurate. "What are you created for?"

He stepped back. "We were created as playthings for the Titans. But we will overthrow them and make our own place."

I looked at the glaive in the grass. I looked at his bow.

"Then I make my own place too," I said. "The Titan I served is already overthrown."

I turned and walked downstream. Behind me I heard the tension leave his bow — the sound of a decision not made. I did not look back.

I had not gone far when I heard him say, quietly, as though to himself:

"A Blank just used I?"

*  *  *

Third's eye moved back to the fire. Whatever the middle distance had held, it was done holding it.

I realised I'd been holding my breath somewhere in the middle of all that, and quietly let it out. Across the camp, Mathen had pushed his hat up off his face and was looking at the sky with the expression of a man revising several assumptions about the world. Roan had turned around at some point. He was looking at Third with the expression of a man who has been brave all his life without ever having understood what bravery was measured against.

Third picked up a stick and adjusted the fire. The coals shifted. Sparks rose and went out.

"That," he said, "is how it started."

Nobody said anything for a while.

It was Mathen who spoke first, which was right, because Mathen always spoke first when silence needed breaking and somehow always found the right thing. He put his hat back over his face.

"Hell of a first day," he said.

Third made a sound that was not quite a laugh, from somewhere inside the bellows of his chest. It was the first time I had heard him make that sound.

"Yes," he said. "It was."

*  *  *


# Seventeen

We had come through the bog that morning, and the bog had nearly kept Roan, so the smell, when it found us in the afternoon, landed on people already shaken.

It had looked, the bog, like the kindest thing the country had shown us in days — we came out of scrub onto a wide flat meadow of soft green reeds, knee-high, level as a swept floor, with berry-bushes scattered across it and the warmth of the Spire coming down mild, and every part of me that was tired wanted to walk straight out into it and lie down. Third put his glaive across my chest before I'd taken three steps. "It is water," he said. "All of it. The reeds grow on the surface. They will hold a bird. They will not hold you." And then, because Roan was already six steps out past where Third's arm could reach, having seen the berries and not the warning — the green floor opened under him without a sound, no splash, just a swallowing, the reeds closing over the place he'd been like a mouth, and Mathen and I had him by an arm and a fistful of cloak before his head went under, and we hauled, and the bog hauled back, and for a long ugly moment the country was very calmly trying to drink a man in front of us while looking like a place you'd picnic. We got him out black to the waist and shaking. We went round the long way after that, along the firm edge, and the meadow lay there behind us soft and green and patient, beautiful as anything, waiting for the next tired thing that trusted its eyes.

So when the smell hit — in the afternoon, which meant the wind was coming from anti-spinward, which meant we were downwind of whatever was left of the town, which meant we'd been smelling it for the last hour without knowing what it was — I'd thought, hoped, it was just the bog again, turned rank in the heat behind us.

It wasn't the bog.

"Titans," Mathen said, pulling his collar up over his nose. "And here I'd just decided I was hungry."

"You are always hungry," Third said, not breaking stride. "We are downwind. You smell the dead."

"I'd worked that out, thank you." But the joke had gone out of his voice by the end of it. Mathen could keep a mood afloat across most things — I'd watched him do it for three days of rain and a washed-out ford and Roan's snoring — but there's a particular silence that comes off a place where a lot of people died at once, and even Mathen went quiet under it.

Roan didn't say anything at all. He'd gone the colour of old porridge and was looking at the gate ahead of us with the fixed attention of a man who has realised that the stories and the thing the stories are about are not the same size.

"If anyone lived," Third said, "they've moved on. The only thing moving in there now is scavengers." He tilted his head — not toward the town, but at the angle I'd learned meant he was running several thoughts at once. "This was within the last spin. Two at most."

As if to make his point, an Axe Beak strode out from between the spikes with something in its mouth that I chose not to identify — a squat armour-plated thing about a humin and a half of bulk, most of it beak and teeth, the little wing-stumps twitching at its sides as it moved. It wove between the sharpened logs like they were saplings, unhurried, fed and unbothered, and vanished around the side of the nearest building without sparing us a glance.

"Those spikes were never going to stop anything, were they," I said.

"They would stop a massed charge," Third said. "Which this town would never face — nothing has charged a wall in ordered ranks since the Titans fell. What does come for a place like this walks between them, as that one did, or simply goes around." "Optimistic," Mathen agreed, in the tone of a man filing the word away for a song he had no intention of writing.

We went in through the gap where the gate had been — the gate itself reduced to kindling, scattered too small to be useful. The ground inside was wrong in the way ground gets when too many things happen on it in too little time. I kept my eyes moving and my hand near my blade and tried not to look too long at the things that weren't moving.

The Herdukii came from behind the first building's corner without warning.

Wolf-headed, bear-clawed, a dead man's cloak knotted round its middle and a string of fingers at its throat. It had been feeding, and it came up out of its crouch and launched itself at Third with the confidence of something that had not yet learned what Third was.

Several things happened in the space of about a heartbeat.

Roan made a sound I'd never heard him make and went backward off his own feet, scrambling, his spear coming up far too late to matter — the honest reflex of a man whose body had decided to be somewhere else before his training could vote on it. Mathen's swords were out before I'd finished flinching, both of them, low and wide and already moving to flank the thing, fast enough that I understood for the first time that the campfire jokes had been hiding something. And I was reaching for my own blade with the awful certainty that I was going to be too slow to be useful.

None of us needed to be anything.

Third's Falka cleared its sheath and returned to it in what felt like one motion. The Herdukii landed in two pieces that had been one piece, twitched, and stopped. Third had not broken stride.

There was a silence.

Mathen looked at his two drawn swords. He looked at the Herdukii. He looked at his swords again, and then sheathed them, both, with the unhurried dignity of a man pretending the whole manoeuvre had been a stretch.

"Well," he said. "Glad I could help."

"You were prepared to," Third said, which from Third was close to a compliment, and which Mathen accepted with a small bow he managed to make only slightly mocking.

Roan was getting up off the ground, scarlet now where he'd been pale, brushing at himself with the furious focus of someone who would rather be doing literally anything than meeting another person's eyes.

"It came out of nowhere," he said.

"It came from behind a building," Mathen said, kindly. "Which, from where you were standing, is much the same thing. You got your spear up. First time I saw one of those I got my spear into my own foot." He clapped Roan on the shoulder on the way past. "You'll meet the next one standing. That's the whole trick — there's always a next one, and you're always a little readier."

I didn't think Mathen had ever put a spear through his own foot in his life. But I watched the colour come back to Roan's face at a survivable rate instead of all at once, and I understood that this was the thing Mathen actually did, the thing that was worth more than the swords: he made the unbearable into something a person could stand inside.

"They were not scavengers here," Third said. He was looking down at the body with no disgust, the way you'd look at a knot you'd just untied. "They were directed. Held in numbers, set on the town all at once, the way the traveller described. Ravagers do not wait. Something made this one wait."

"Something worse than a Ravager," Roan said. His voice only shook a little.

"Yes."

We split up to search — Mathen and Roan taking the outbuildings along the wall, Third and I taking the large structure near the centre that Third had been looking at since we came through the gate. I think he sent the others wide on purpose. I think he already knew what we were going to find, and didn't want an audience for it.

The memorial pillar stood near the middle of the settlement, knee-high, three-sided, perhaps a dozen names across it. The founding plaque at its base had been there long enough to wear smooth at the edges but not long enough to rise very far out of the ground.

I stopped at it for a moment. A generation. Maybe less. Some of these names had likely been children when the town was founded; they'd grown up and built something, and the something was gone in a spin or two along with everyone who'd made it. I thought about a knee-high pillar in a town a great deal like this one, with a great deal fewer names on it, and Tondi in her corner, and Klar's stew, and I made myself stop thinking about it.

Third pointed at the central building. His arm extended with the directness of someone who had been pointing at things for three hundred thousand spins and had long since stopped making it a social gesture.

"There. That is what they came for."

The wall had been breached from the inside out — not smashed, breached, the debris fallen outward in a way that said whatever did it had known exactly where it was going and gone there without hesitation. No matching hole in the far wall. Whatever came out had left through the opening it made.

"You think a caged Tusker did that?" I said.

Third paused, considering it with apparent sincerity. "Was that a joke?"

"Was it a good enough one that you can't tell?"

A shorter pause. Something in the set of his shoulders I'd learned to read as the Forged equivalent of dry amusement; the glow of his eye dimmed a fraction, the way it did when he was, in his own fashion, almost enjoying himself. "It was not. After you."

Inside had been an official's residence — six or seven strides to a raised platform with a seat on it, ramps up each side to the rooms above. The sort of room built to make a visitor feel the right size. It was still managing it, even without the official.

The lockbox sat in the centre of the floor. It had been below the centre of the floor, in the proper fashion, and was now above it, and the floor around it was in several places it should not have been. The hexwood box was big enough to hold a reclining child. Its gear-lock hadn't been picked or broken by someone who understood it — it had been torn apart by something that had decided the mechanism was beneath its notice. The lid was peeled back the way you'd husk a grain, if you were enormous and the husk were reinforced hexwood.

I looked at it. Then I looked at Third.

He bent, lifted the lid, and held it out to me flat in both hands, like an offering.

"I did not do this," he said.

I hadn't asked. That was the thing I noticed, and didn't understand, and filed away with the other small wrong-shaped pieces of Third: that he had answered a question no one had put to him, in the flat careful voice he used for the things that were true and cost him to say. As if somewhere, once, there had been a wrecked place he had not been able to say it about, and he had been saying it ever since to make up the difference.

I took it. Heavier than it looked, and it looked heavy. The hinges still hung off it, wrenched bodily out of the box. The wood hadn't split or cracked where it bent back. It had simply been made to go where something had decided it should go.

"You told us what was being collected," I said. "Back at the inn. Tools. I don't think I believed you. Not in the stomach. I believed you the way you believe a story." I set the lid down, carefully, for no reason. "I believe you in the stomach now."

"Good," Third said, and did not say it unkindly. "Belief in the stomach is the only kind that keeps a person alive out here." He looked at the ruined box. "But this tells me something I did not know before, which is worth the smell. Whatever did this came for one thing. It did not search. It did not ransack the town for valuables. It went to this room, opened this box, took what was inside, and left through the wall. It knew the object was here before it arrived."

"The traveller said it went building to building."

"In his town. Not here. Here it walked a straight line." The glow sharpened. "Which means between his town and this one, it learned to find the thing without looking. Or it always could, and chose not to, in his town, for reasons I do not yet have."

I found the slat-book in the upper room, in a drawer that had survived, probably because a drawer held nothing worth the unmaking of a wall. Most of it was the ordinary business of a small place — supply tallies, complaints, notes on the Scar and the weather it dragged behind it. Then, near the end, a hand that pressed harder on the wood:

"He was nervous," I said. "Listen. He's writing about keeping the 'Titan's stone' in town. 'Even hidden as it was.' He doesn't say what it is. Only that he'd been afraid of it for a while, and afraid of not having it, both at once."

Third had gone still. Not the watchful stillness. The other one — the one that meant something was being assembled behind the eye rather than observed in front of it.

"Titan's stone," he said, turning the frightened man's phrase over. "His name for it, not a true one. It will be a tool — one of the lesser ones, a thing the Titan made and set down and forgot, the way you or I might leave a chisel in a field. Not the great anchor the stories mean when they say such words. A small thing. But small things were how the Titan held the world together, and a man who is gathering them is not gathering them to build a fence." The eye came to me, the glow steady and hard. "And a frightened headman who did not know what he held — yet it walked straight to the box, in a town it had never seen, and opened the one that mattered and no other."

"So how did it know?" I asked.

"To come, or which wall?" The eye stayed on the emptied box.

"To come is the simple part. A town that hides a thing it fears cannot keep the secret — the fear leaks, into a tally-book, into a tavern, into the wrong ear, until it is a rumour, and a rumour walks farther than feet. It will have heard there was a town frightened of something it could not name, and come to learn whether the something was one of these, and to take it if it was." A beat.

"Which wall is the part worth fearing. No one showed it. It can feel these the way I can — they have a pull to them, faint and steady, a sound just below hearing — and it walked toward what it wanted the way water walks downhill. Nothing is directing it. Nothing has to."

"That's worse than someone telling it," I said.

"Yes," Third said.

"It usually is." Outside, the Scar had begun its slow crawl across the sky — that thirty-minute fade from full light toward the flat Anka dark. We had a walk to make before it finished, and the Herdukii by the gate to step over again, and I was going to be smelling this place for days whichever way the wind turned.

Mathen and Roan were waiting by the gap in the wall when we came out, Roan steadier now, Mathen with the particular lightness he put on when he'd decided the rest of us needed it.

"Anything?" Mathen asked.

"A direction," Third said.

"My favourite kind of nothing." Mathen fell into step. "Lead on, then. Away from the smell, ideally."

I took one last look back at the lockbox lid through the broken wall, lying where I'd set it. hexwood, unmarked but for the torn hinges. Made to last generations. Opened in seconds, and left behind because it was not interesting.

"Let's go," I said.

Third was already at the gate.

*  *  *


# Eighteen

We passed a marker that evening that I didn't understand, and Mathen did, and the explaining of it is what undid us.

It stood where two trails crossed: a waist-high cairn with a flat stone set into its face, and on the stone, scratched deep and then darkened with something to make them distinct, two shapes. A tall thin figure on one side. A hunched toothed one on the other. Between them, a line, scored so hard it had split the stone.

"Boundary stone," Mathen said.

"Kro that way, Ork the other. You see them all through this country — it's old border, older than the towns that sit on it. The line's the thing. Whoever cut it cut it to last." He ran a thumb along the split. "They've been hating each other since before anyone can say. That's the one fact every version agrees on. Every other part of the story, you'll get a different telling in every town — who started it, who betrayed who, which of them was the real people and which the ruined copy. But the hate itself, nobody argues. It's just *there*. Like weather. Like the Scar."

"Why, though," Roan said. He was looking at the two scratched figures. "What did they actually do to each other, at the start of it?"

"Ah." Mathen settled back, the storyteller's pleasure coming over him. "Now, the one I like best — and I make no claim it's true, mind, I make a claim it's *good* — goes that they were one people once, and a great sorcerer offered to make them perfect, and they said yes, the fools, because everyone says yes to that, and..." He spread his hands. "Two peoples woke where one lay down. Each one looked at the other and saw everything it had just thrown away, walking around wearing its face. And you can forgive a stranger anything. But you can't forgive your own discarded half. That's the version I tell. It's almost certainly wrong." He glanced, the way he'd taken to glancing, across the fire. "Ser? You'd know better than the stone-cutters. Were they one people, once?"

Third did not answer.

I had learned his stillnesses by then, and I watched the first one arrive — the kettle kind, the held kind, the kind that had ended in a quiet correction the night Mathen told the war wrong. I waited for the correction. *That is not how it was.* It was practically a comfort by now, that flat patient voice setting the record straight.

It didn't come. Instead the second stillness came over him, the one that went *down* — the glow guttering low, the attention turning to a place that was not our place — and he rose, without a word, and stepped, at an angle my eye slid off of, and was gone from the fire before Roan had finished waiting for his answer.

"...did I say something?" Roan asked.

"No," I said. But I sat very still, and watched the empty dark where Third had been, and understood for the first time that there were stories Third would correct and stories Third would walk into the grey to get away from, and that we had just, without meaning to, asked for one of the second kind.

&nbsp;

<p align="center">◆ &nbsp; ◆ &nbsp; ◆</p>


There had been, before, a stretch of spins that were nearly happy, and Third had let himself have them, which was the part he would never afterward forgive.

He was a warden of the wall by then. Not honorary any longer — the Virek had stopped saying the word, which was how he knew it had stopped being true, the way a scar stops being a wound when you forget to favour it. He walked the high logs at night and the green ones on watch with him no longer kept their distance, or kept less of it, and Liwon ran with the other children in the day and dreamed in their tongue at night, and that was right, and Third had decided to be content, and was, almost.

Wally was still with them. Wally did not grow, which nobody remarked on because nobody remarks on the Hin, but he had taken to walking the wall with Third on the long nights, the small person made of stories keeping company with the large one who had run out of them to tell. They did not always speak. It was enough, those nights, to stand the watch together and let Liwon belong to someone else in the daylight, and to mind, in the low private way Third had learned to mind things, like a temperature.

He saw the flicker from the wall.

It was far off, deep in the wood, and very bright — a white flash with a blue heart to it, there and gone, the kind of light you feel behind your eyes a moment after you stop seeing it. He marked it the way he marked everything, and was turning to call it down to the others, when the veins came.

They came up over everything at once. Red, branching, vast — across the ground and across the sky and across the walls and the wood and across his own outstretched arm, a webwork of red light that had the terrible quality of seeming to have been there always, beneath the skin of the world, and only now made to show. They held for a breath. Two. He had time to think *I have seen this colour before,* and to be right, and not to have time to finish being right —

— and then they were gone, and the village was full of growling.

He turned from the wall.

The Virek were not there.

Where the green ones had been — on the wall beside him, in the yards below, in the doorways of the woven houses — stood strangers. Pale ones, fine-boned, tall, with hair so fair it was nearly no colour at all, nor the green that the skin had been; they stood blinking in their new bodies, appalled, looking at their own pale hands. And among them, lower, broader, black-haired and already showing teeth, the others. The green had not left those ones. It had gone *into* them, concentrated, a deeper and angrier green, and they had teeth meant for meaning it, and they were the ones making the sound.

For one held instant, the whole village stood and looked at itself, pale beside green, and saw.

Then it began. Not a battle — there was no battle in it, no sides drawn, only the oldest recognition there is, every pale one seeing the green one that had been the rest of it and every green one seeing the pale, and the seeing being unbearable, and the unbearable becoming, in the space of a breath, hands and teeth and the wall-logs torn loose for clubs. It began everywhere at once. It began the way the veins had come, as if it had always been there under the skin of these people and the flash had only made it show.

"Liwon," Third said.

He had not decided to say it. He was already moving.

*  *  *

Zala's house was at the wall's foot, and the laminated reed of its door hung in tatters, and Third put his shoulder through what was left and stopped, and every gear in him stopped with him.

There were two children on the floor.

One was pale. A small girl, fine-boned and new, with hair gone nearly clear and eyes a blue he had never seen because Liwon's eyes had been Virek-dark and these were not, these were a blue like the heart of the flicker, and they were open, and they found him, and her body was torn in a way that the small new pale body could not mend and would not survive, and she was looking at him.

The other was green. Smaller-seeming, though they were the same, crouched over the pale one with blood on its fingers and its mouth, and it too looked at Third, and what came off it was rage and terror and a disgust so total it had no room left for anything that might have been love, and Third understood — in the flat fast way the old thoughts used to bring him things, except the thoughts were silent now, the thoughts had nothing — that these were not two children.

They were one child. They were the halves of one child, sundered the way the wood-stone said two peoples were sundered, and they had woken in the same room into the same instant of hating what they'd lost, and they had not even had names for it yet, they had only had each other, within reach.

The green one broke first. It scrambled up the wreck of Zala's furniture and went out the high window into the dark, fast, low, the way the thoughts had always told Third the green ones moved, and Third took one step after it —

— and outside there was a shout, a bright triumphant boy's shout, and then a wet sound, and the shout turned to laughter, and Third knew without going to the window that one of the pale ones had found the green child in the dark and done to it what pale and green were going to be doing to each other from this night until the end of the world.

He turned back to the one on the floor.

He crossed the room. Two paces. He had crossed worse rooms in two paces and not felt them; he felt this one. He knelt, and his hands, which could mend any part of himself, which had mended every part of himself at least once, hung over the torn pale body and could do nothing, because she was not made of wood and titan-blood, she was made of the thing the green ones and pale ones were all made of, the soft mendable-only-by-time thing, and there was no time, there was the opposite of time.

"Bada?"

It was barely a breath. It came from the pale child's mouth, from lips that had never made the sound before because they had not existed before, in a body a quarter-hour old. But it was the sound. The exact sound. The lower lip, even now, even torn, going out the way it went out when she was small and frightened and wanted only to be lifted and held against the bellows until the world stopped being so large.

*Bada.* The word she had made for him before she had words. Another of her words he had decided not to correct toward accuracy, because accuracy had stopped being the only thing he wanted.

Something in Third broke that he did not have a part to replace.

"I am here," he said. "Liwon. I am here."

The blue eyes — the wrong eyes, her eyes — stayed on him a moment, and were uncertain, the way they were uncertain in strange surroundings, and these were the strangest surroundings, this new pale unasked-for self. And then they were not uncertain about anything anymore.

There was a small sound behind him.

Wally stood in the broken doorway. Wally, who had not changed — who could not change, who was not Virek, who was a story and stories do not sunder — Wally stood unchanged in a village where every single soul had been split in two, and he was looking at the pale child on the floor with an expression Third had never seen a Hin wear, because Hin do not, as a rule, have anything in their lives that a true thing can hurt.

"That's her," Wally said. Not a question. Plain Hin-honesty even now. "Both of them were her. The one outside too." He came in, and knelt on the child's other side, small and solid and entirely real, and he reached out and closed the blue eyes with two fingers, gently, the way you'd do for someone, because Third's hands would not stop hanging uselessly in the air and someone had to, and Wally was the only one left who could.

And as he did it — Third saw it and would carry the seeing forever — Wally's hand, where it touched the child's face, was not quite as solid as it had been. You could not have said how. You could only have said that a thing made of telling had just lost the one it had been told to, and that the world had noticed, and had begun, very gently, the way the tide goes out, to ask for him back.

"Wally," Third said.

"I know," said Wally. "It's all right. I got to be in the good part." He looked up, and he was still smiling, a little, the smile of the stories he was made of. "You tell it. The good part. You keep telling it, and maybe I—" He stopped. He looked at his own hand. "Huh," he said, softly, the way you'd say it at a mildly interesting thing, and that was the last whole word Wally said in front of him, though it was not, Third would learn, the last of Wally.

*  *  *

He found himself in the grey without stepping there.

It took him without his choosing it, the way it had the first time, on the field, under the dust — one breath in the torn room with the closed-eyed child and the fading friend, and the next breath the room was gone and the colour was gone and he was kneeling in the swirling nothing with the wisps coming off his own arms, and where she had been, was empty grey floor. Not even the silhouette of her. The grey did not keep her. The grey, which kept so much, would not keep the one thing he would have given the rest of it to hold.

Behind him, the voice.

"Loss is profound," it said, "when the relationship has grown for some time."

It was barely above a whisper, and it had the weight of a mountain choosing to lie down. He did not turn. He could not have borne to see it, the vast horned dark of it, the thing whose dust had woken him and whose blood ran in the geysers and whose teeth lay in the fields; the thing that had made the world as a child makes a thing, with great power and no wisdom and no notion at all of what it is to be small inside the made thing and to love another small thing inside it.

"How do I fix this," Third said. He was not sure it was a question. It came out of the broken place, which was still broken, which he could already tell was going to stay broken in a way no wood and no blood would close.

"You do not," the voice said. There was no cruelty in it. That was the part Third would spend the next three hundred thousand or so spins failing to get out from under: there was no cruelty in it at all. "Like a seed, it must die and be buried before it blossoms into something beautiful. You are watching a beginning. You have mistaken it for an ending, because you are small, and the small feel the burial and never see the bloom." A vast and patient nothing. "Two peoples now, where one was. More to play. More to watch. It is better. In time you will see that it is better."

The broken place in Third warmed, faintly, the way a coal warms — and he understood, with horror, that some made part of him, some old obedient architecture, *agreed*, was built to agree, found the logic clean. He wanted the warmth. He wanted, for one unforgivable instant, to believe that Liwon was a seed and her death a planting and that somewhere ahead was a bloom that would make the torn pale body on the floor into a thing that had been *worth it.*

He wanted the beauty. He did not want the pain.

And then he chose the pain, because the pain was hers and the beauty was the Titan's, and he would not give the Titan one more thing that had been Liwon's, not even the meaning of her dying.

He stood up, and stepped back.

The room was still there, just. The fighting outside had thinned to the after-sounds of it, the single cries and the running feet and the long silences between. The child lay where she lay, eyes closed by a friend who was no longer in the doorway, who was not anywhere Third could see, though Third would feel him for a long time yet, thin and far, the way you feel a story you are forgetting.

And the former part of Liwon lay at his feet, and Third, who could mend anything he was made of, stood in the wreck of the only stillness he had ever managed to give her, and could not mend this, and did not try, and stayed.

&nbsp;

<p align="center">◆ &nbsp; ◆ &nbsp; ◆</p>


He came back to the fire near the bottom of the night.

He didn't step out of the dark so much as resolve from it, suddenly accounted for at the edge of the light. The glow of his eye was down past the lowest ember I'd seen on him — down to almost nothing, a coal under ash — and it took a long moment finding me, and longer still coming up to anything I'd have called present.

Mathen had waited up. Roan had fallen asleep mid-wait, the way the young do. Mathen looked at Third across the coals, and whatever he saw there made him set aside, very quietly, the storyteller's hunger I'd watched him carry toward Third for a tenday.

"You don't have to," Mathen said. "I withdraw the question. It was a pub question. I'm sorry I asked it by a real fire."

Third was quiet a while. The coals ticked.

"They were one people once," he said, at last. The voice came from somewhere deeper than usual, the bellows dragging under it. "They chose to become perfect, and were offered the means, and took it. Two woke where one had lain down. Each kept what it could not bear to keep whole, and could not forgive the other for carrying the rest." A pause. "Your version is not wrong, master Mathen. It is only missing the part where it happened to specific people, in specific rooms, on a specific night, and someone watched, who had loved one of them." The ember held on the fire. "Tell yours. It is kinder. Kind is a fair trade for their coin."

And that was all. Mathen didn't push, and I didn't, and we banked the fire and slept, and in the morning Third was Third again, watchful and dry and exact, and walked us anti-spinward under a sky that had a wound in it but no veins, and I thought — Scar help me, I actually thought — that it had only been a hard memory of a long war, the kind a soldier carries.

I did not know there had been a child. I did not know there had been a friend made of stories. I did not know that the thing every town agreed on, the hate older than weather, had a night attached to it, and a name, and that the name was one Third had decided, long ago, not to correct toward accuracy.

I know now. I am telling it. That is the only thing I can do with it — the thing Mathen said, that I didn't understand until I was the one left carrying it.

You make more than one person carry the thing.

So that it doesn't all rest on him.

*  *  *


# Nineteen

Third changed after the boundary stone.

I didn't have the why of it — I thought it was the question we'd asked, the Kro and the Ork, some old soldier's wound I'd blundered a finger into — but the shape of the change I could see plainly enough. He walked faster. He stopped asking whether we needed to rest and started telling us when we would. He spent the night watches further out, and came back from them later, and twice I woke to find him not at the fire at all and then there at the edge of the dark before I'd finished being afraid, as if he'd been somewhere and come back, lessened, the way he came back lessened from the carts.

And he started drawing in the dirt.

He'd squat at the cold morning fire and lay out the country with a stick — here the town that died, here the road, here the line the collector had walked through it, dead straight, and then he'd extend the line with the stick, on and on, the way you'd extend any straight thing, and he'd look at where it went.

"He does not wander," Third said, the fourth morning. "He is told where each one lies, and he goes to it in a straight line, and the next will be here." The stick stabbed a point ahead of us and anti-spinward, up in country that rose toward the rim. "A high place. Old. There has been a keeping there a long time — I have heard of it, the way you hear of a thing for three hundred — for a very long time, without ever needing to go." The eye came up to us. "If we go hard, we reach it before he does. And then, for once, we are not reading what he has done. We are standing between him and the next thing he wants."

"And then what," Mathen said. Not a challenge. A storyteller making sure he had the stakes right before he committed them to memory.

Third looked at the line in the dirt for a moment.

"Then we find out what he is," he said. "I have been chasing a hole in the world the shape of the things it has taken. I would like, before the Graveyard, to see the thing that fits the hole."

He scuffed the map out with his hoof, and we went hard.

We went hard into rising country, and the country answered, the second afternoon, by throwing rocks at us out of a clear sky. There was no cloud. That was the part I could not make peace with — the Spire was shining, the day was fine, and then a pebble cracked off the trail at my feet, and another, and Third said one word, "stony," and put us at a dead run for an overhang he'd already marked without telling anyone he was marking it. By the time we reached it the air was full of them, stones the size of knuckles and then the size of fists falling out of nothing at all, hammering the grass flat, hammering Roan's other shoulder hard enough to drop him before Mathen dragged him in under the rock. They say it's the glutton's teeth, Mathen told me, hunched beside me while the world cracked and rattled outside our little shelter — that the broken teeth of one of the Titans are scattered out ahead of us in fields, and that the sky over that country never quite forgives it, and drops stone the way other skies drop rain, for no reason and to no schedule and on whoever happens to be under it. It lasted the length of a short tantrum and then simply stopped, the last few stones pattering down into a silence that rang, and we crawled out onto a trail strewn white with rubble that hadn't been there an hour before, and went on, because the place all the lines pointed was that same teeth-strewn country, and it had just told us, plainly, what it thought of visitors.

*  *  *

The keeping was a tower of dry-laid stone on a tor, the kind of high bare place where the wind has opinions, and it had been there long enough that the path up to it was worn into the rock a hand's-depth, and the keeper who met us at the top was as worn as the path.

She was old in the way that stops being about how many thousands of spins. She watched the four of us climb the last of it — a Forged, a swordsman, a guardsman, and a girl who served stew — and she did not reach for the bell-rope by the door, and she did not look afraid, and that was the first thing that frightened me, because everyone is at least a little afraid of Third, and she wasn't, because she had clearly decided some time ago that there was a worse thing coming and we weren't it.

"You're late if you're help," she said, "and early if you're him. Which."

"Help," Mathen said. "Late, possibly. How long have you known he was coming?"

"All my life. It's the keeping. You keep the thing, and you keep the knowing that one day someone comes for the thing, and your whole use is to have kept it that long." She stood aside from the low door. "My grandmother's grandmother kept it. I have no one to hand it to. I had started to hope I'd die first and make it someone else's morning." She looked at Third as we passed, and something moved over her face, recognition without fear. "You're old enough to know what it is. Tell them, if you like. I'm tired of being the only one who carries it."

Inside, on a stone shelf worn to a dish by the centuries of hands that had checked it was still there, lay the thing. It was not impressive. They never are, Third said once — the load-bearing things never look like much, because the Titan had no need to make them look like anything. It was a short rod of something that was almost glass and almost not, pale, the faint blue-white of the dried stuff in the geyser field, with a grain to it like wood. I reached to pick it up the way you'd pick up anything, and it would not come. Not stuck — fixed, as though the rod and the stone shelf and the hill beneath the stone were one piece and always had been. I pulled harder, feeling foolish, and it was like trying to lift a line drawn on the floor.

"You have to wake it," the keeper said.

"Tap this end, once." She touched the near end of the rod.

"Then it remembers it's a thing, and a thing can be lifted. Tap the far end and it forgets again — settles back into being a place, and a place does not come up for anyone." She watched me.

"This end, girl." I tapped the near end with one finger. Something let go — I felt it in my teeth more than heard it — and the second time I closed my hand around the rod it lifted easy, and had a weight to it then, like a held breath.

"It is a peg," Third said, very quietly, turning it in the firelight without taking it from my hands. "A small one. The Titan made the world and tied it down, the way you'd peg a tent against a wind, so that it would not — drift, unmake, I do not have the word, the word is older than words. There are pegs like this all through the world. Most no one will ever find. He is finding them." The eye dimmed. "I had thought he was gathering tools to build something. He is not building. He is pulling pegs."

"And when you pull all the pegs out of a tent," Mathen said slowly.

"You do not have a tent," said Third. "You have cloth, and wind, and whatever the tent was keeping out."

The keeper laughed, a dry awful sound. "There," she said. "Now there's two of you who know. I feel lighter already." And then, because the wind had changed, or because a lifetime of keeping had taught her exactly what the changing of it meant: "He's here. Put it back, girl. Putting it back never helped, but I'd like it to be on the shelf. I'd like to have kept it to the end."

I put it back on the worn stone dish and tapped the far end the way she'd shown me, and felt it take — felt the rod stop being a thing under my fingers and settle back into being a place, fixed, part of the hill again. My hands weren't steady.

I had to tap twice before it caught.

*  *  *

I had built him, in my head, out of the wounded man's words and the wreck of the town. I'd made him huge. A figure of smoke and ruin, something that came on like weather.

He came up the worn path like a man.

That was the first wrong thing — that he was man-sized, man-shaped, walking on his own two feet up the same hand-worn path we'd climbed, unhurried, as if he had all the spins there had ever been. He wore something that had been clothing once and was now mostly the idea of it. And on his back, rising over his shoulders like the frame of a pack, were six jars of magefire. I knew it on sight; anyone raised in Anka would. The white-with-a-hint-of-blue that comes off the powder inside, caged lightning, the stuff the militia loads into a Boltcaster and calls a shot — the same cold clear glow that lived behind the seam of Third's chest. But a shot is a little thing, a vial you could hide in your fist, and there's a size a shot is and no other, and has been as long as anyone's been selling them. These were jars: great hand-shaped jars, each big enough to put up a hundred spins of preserves in, and older-looking than any shot I'd ever passed across a bar, as if they'd been made before anyone had decided what a vessel of the stuff should be. Six of them, lit from inside, casting no warmth, throwing his shadow six ways at once down the rock.

Third had said it back at the inn, and I had not truly heard it until now: *a great many bottles of it, more than I have ever seen one body carry, and I do not know why it needs so much.* I had imagined a rack of the little shots. I had not imagined six jars of caged storm riding the shoulders of a man strolling up a goat-path to rob a frightened town. He did not look at us. That was the thing I have never been able to make people understand. We were four armed and frightened people standing between him and the thing he'd come for, and his eyes — they were a man's eyes, that was the worst of it, just a tired man's eyes — passed over us the way yours pass over the furniture in a room you're crossing to reach the window.

Roan moved first.

I want it written down exactly, because I have heard it told wrong since, told as foolishness. It was not foolishness. Roan stepped into the doorway with his spear levelled and his voice shaking and he said "you don't go in," and he meant it, and he was more frightened than I have ever seen a person be, and he did it anyway, and that is the only thing bravery has ever been.

The man looked at him. For the first time, something behind the tired eyes engaged — mild, almost kind, the look you'd give a child who'd planted himself in your path with his arms out.

And then he stepped sideways.

I felt Third go rigid beside me. Not move — the opposite of move. He went rigid the way you go rigid when you see a thing that cannot be, and the not-being of it nails you to the spot, and in the half-breath that Third stood nailed, the man was simply — past. Past Roan's spear, past the door, not through them, beside them, gone out of the world at that angle my eye could never hold and back into it on the inside of the tower, between Mathen and the shelf, and he reached out and took the peg off the worn stone dish the way you'd take a cup off a table in your own house.

He did not wake it. I had set it back into a place with my own shaking hands, and a place does not come up for anyone — and it came up for him, no tap, no catch, as though it had never been fixed to anything at all. Then Third moved. Too late, knowing it was too late, crossing the tower in a stride with his glaive coming round — and the man looked at him.

At Third. Only at Third. The mild eyes found the Forged and for one instant they were not mild at all, they were *interested*, they sharpened on Third the way they had not sharpened on four armed strangers, and his head tilted a fraction, the way you tilt your head at a sound you half-recognise from a long time ago. His mouth moved. I was close enough to see it and not to hear it, and I have spent a long time since trying to read the shape of the word off the memory of his lips, and the nearest I can come is that it was not a word of threat.

It might have been the start of a name.

Then he stepped sideways again and the tower was only the tower, wind through the door, the keeper on her knees by the empty dish, Roan still braced in the doorway against a man who wasn't there anymore, and Mathen with his good blade half-drawn and nothing to draw it on, and Third standing in the middle of the round stone room with his glaive up and his single eye gone down to a coal, staring at the place where the air had closed.

*  *  *

Nobody had touched him. That was the whole of it, and it took us all a while to be able to say it. Four of us, and the worn old keeper, and her hand-deep path, and her grandmother's grandmother's keeping — and he had walked up like a man and stepped out of the world twice and taken the peg and not troubled to harm a single one of us, because harming us would have required us to matter, and we hadn't.

The keeper didn't weep. She knelt by the empty dish and put her hand in the worn hollow of it, the dish her people had worn smooth with checking, and she left it there, and she said, to no one, "well," in the voice of a person setting down a thing they have carried so long that the not-carrying is worse than the weight ever was.

Mathen got her up, and got water into her, and got a fire going, because that is what Mathen does, he tends the spaces between the standing and the falling. Roan sat in the doorway where he'd braced and looked at his own hands as if they belonged to someone he was disappointed in, and I went and sat by him, because I'd watched him do the bravest thing on that tor and I wasn't going to let him learn the wrong lesson from its uselessness.

"It didn't matter," he said.

"It mattered," I said. "It just didn't *work.* You'll want to keep those two apart in your head or they'll rot each other."

He didn't answer, but he didn't get up either, and we sat.

Third had not moved from the middle of the room.

I went to him last, because he was the one I understood least and feared most to ask. The coal of his eye had not come back up. He was looking at nothing, at the closed air, at the angle.

"Third," I said. "What did you see?"

And he was quiet for long enough that I thought he wouldn't answer, and when he did it was not to my question, not really, it was to some older one of his own.

"He went sideways," Third said.

"I — yes. He vanished. Both times. How—"

"He went sideways," Third said again, and there was something under the avalanche of his voice I had never heard there before, something I would not have a name for until much later, when I had learned what it cost a thing to be the only one of its kind for three hundred thousand spins and then, on a windy tor, in the middle of a robbery, to discover that it was not. "I have never," he said, "in all that time, met anything else that could do that."

He turned and looked at me then, and the coal of his eye had a question in it that I couldn't answer and he plainly couldn't either, and the glowing six-lamped shape of the man who'd walked up like a man was burned into both of us, and somewhere in the part of Third where the gears turned wrong, two cold blue-white lights I would not understand for a long time yet were quietly keeping him alive, the same cold blue-white as the six that had just walked back out of the world.

He didn't say that part. He didn't know to, or he knew and couldn't. But I think it was in him even then, the wrongness, the recognition with no shape — that he had finally seen the thing that fit the hole, and the thing that fit the hole was lit by the same light he was.

"We've lost it," I said, because someone had to say a true thing that was the right size.

"We have lost this one," Third agreed. "Yes." The eye came up, by degrees, to something like itself. "Now we stop reaching the pegs after him. Now we go to the place all the lines point, and we are there before him, and we do not stand between him and a thing on a shelf." He looked out the low door, anti-spinward, toward the country that rose toward the rim and the fields and, back spireward of them, the place the whole crooked map had been pointing at since the town that died. "We stand between him and the last peg. The one the whole tent hangs from."

"The Graveyard," Mathen said quietly, from the fire.

"The Graveyard," said Third.

*  *  *


# Twenty

There is a trick to not seeing a thing, and I was good at it, and the trick is this: you keep your eyes moving. You don't let them rest on the wrongness. You catalogue around it — the hooves, the bellows, the way the eye is a hole and not a lamp — you note each piece and you file it and you never, ever, lay the pieces out side by side on the same table, because the moment you do they stop being pieces and start being a picture.

I had been not-laying-them-out for a long time. Coming down off the tor, in the grey after the robbery, I caught myself doing it still — filing the new piece, the man who stepped sideways, the man who glowed, next to the old pieces and quickly looking away — and I understood, the way you understand you've caught a chill, that I wasn't going to be able to keep it up much longer. Some tables get too full. The pieces start lining themselves up while you're asleep.

We walked the rest of that day mostly in silence. Roan walked like a man carrying a stone he couldn't put down, and twice I started to talk to him about it and twice Mathen caught my eye and gave the smallest shake of his head — *not yet, let it sit* — and he was right, he usually was, about the spaces between people. Third walked in front, as ever. But I found I couldn't look at his back the easy way anymore. The pieces were lining up.

I took the watch with him that night, because I'd decided to, the way I decide most things, which is to say I didn't decide it so much as discover I'd already made up my mind.

*  *  *

He stood a little apart, as he did now, at the edge of the firelight, his eye on the dark. I came and stood beside him, not too close, and for a while I just stood the watch the way he stood it, and let the wanting-to-ask build up its courage.

"How does he do it," I said, finally. "Going sideways. How do *you* do it." I made myself add the second part, because the first part alone was a coward's question.

Third was quiet long enough that I thought I'd hit the limit of him, the place where he simply declined. Then, without turning from the dark, he said:

"There is a place beside this one. I do not have a true name for it; the name I have is borrowed and probably wrong. It is grey. There is no up in it, and no down, and very little ground, and the wind in it is not weather." The eye stayed on the dark. "You do not pass *through* the world to reach it. You step *beside*. From there, this world — the fire, you, the trees — is only smears of pale light, very far off, and you can walk a little way along the grey and step back in somewhere the wall, or the carts, or the man with the spear, can no longer stop you."

"That's — " I didn't have the word. "That's how he walked past Roan."

"Yes."

"And the carts. At the causeway. You did that. You went there and came back on the other side."

"Yes."

I'd known it — I'd *seen* it — but hearing him say it plain, name it like a fact about the weather, did something to the back of my neck. "How long have you been able to?"

"Since the beginning. Since I woke. It is not a thing I learned. It is a thing I am." The eye came round to me, the glow level. "Which is why tonight was — " and there he stopped, and the glow guttered, and I watched him reach for a true word and find only the size of the thing. "I have been able to step beside the world since before there were towns to step into. In all of that, I have never once met another thing that could do it. Not a Forged. Not a sorcerer. Not a beast. I had decided it was mine. The single thing in the world that was only mine." A pause. "Tonight a man walked up a hill and did it twice in front of me, and looked at me while he did, and his face — Tasha, his face did the thing your face does when you hear a tune you knew as a child and cannot place. He half-knew me. And I do not know him. And I do not know how that can be true, and it is the first thing in a very long time that I have not known and could not simply wait until I did."

It was, I realised, the most frightened I had ever heard him. Not loud. Third was never loud. But there was a thing missing from his voice that was always there, and the missing of it was the fear: the certainty. Third always knew. It was the most Third thing about him, more than the hooves or the eye — that flat unhurried *I have seen this, I know what it is, here is what it does.* And the man on the tor had reached into him and taken the certainty out.

"You think he's the same as you," I said. "What you are. You think you're — kin, somehow."

"I think the evidence permits it," he said, which was the closest Third came to *yes, and it terrifies me.*

*  *  *

I should have let it lie there. I am good at letting things lie. But the pieces were on the table now, all of them, lined up while I'd been not-looking, and a picture is a hard thing to unsee once it's assembled, and the picture had a hole in the middle of it the exact shape of a question I hadn't asked.

"When you go there," I said. "The grey. You come back — less. Every time. The carts. The boundary stone, the night Roan asked about the Kr. You go, and you come back with the light in your eye gone down to nothing, like a fire somebody sat on. And you told me, at the carts, that you come back the same as you leave." I kept my voice even, which took some doing. "That was the only thing you've ever said to me that I knew was a lie. I let it go then. I'm not letting it go now. What's *there,* Third? What costs you that much, just to cross a few paces of grey?"

He didn't answer for a long time. The fire ticked. Roan murmured something in his sleep, and Mathen — I'd thought Mathen was asleep — shifted, and went still again, in the particular way of a man who has decided to let a conversation belong to the two people having it.

"The grey keeps things," Third said, at last. "That is the part I can tell you, and it is true. It is very old, and it is mostly empty, and into the empty go the things a person does not look at directly. They wait there. They are patient. When I cross — even for a few paces, even to step past a cart — I cross *through* them, and they rise, and they walk the grey beside me, and they are not the kind of thing you set down again on the other side just because you have arrived." The eye was on the dark, not on me. "That is the cost. It is not the crossing. The crossing is nothing. It is what is waiting in the place I cross."

I understood about half of it, and the half I understood was enough.

"You go there to be with someone," I said. Quietly. Not a question. "Not just to cross. The good ones, you said — you said you'd been somewhere good and come back lessened, and the good ones were the ones worth walking back into. You *go.* On purpose. To be with — someone you lost."

The watch went very quiet.

He did not say yes. He did not say no. He did the thing he had done in the inn when I'd asked the first careful question of him, a whole lifetime of road ago — his hand, at his side, closed and opened, once, the way it had in the Snake Forest, and again on the tor — and the eye stayed on the dark, and the bellows drew long and slow, and he said, after a while, in a voice I had to lean in to hear:

"You are better at seeing than you let yourself be, Tasha. I had wondered when you would stop arranging the pieces around the gap and look into it." A pause. "Yes. There is someone. There were several someones; that is what a long life is, mostly, a accumulation of someones who are no longer there. But there is one. And the grey is where I can still — " the coal of his eye sank, and held, and steadied — "where I can still find the warm part, before the end of it. I do not tell you who, or how it ended, and I am asking you not to ask, not yet, not on a road with this much walking still in it. Some things you cannot carry and walk at the same time. But you have seen true. There is someone. That is what it costs me. Not the grey. Her."

*Her.*

He hadn't meant to give me that, I think. Or he had, exactly that much and not a grain more — *her*, one word, a door opened the width of a finger and held there. I did not push it wider. I had pushed enough, and I found, to my surprise, that I didn't want the rest of it tonight, that knowing the shape of it was already almost more than I could stand, that the *content* would have buried me and I wasn't ready to be buried.

So we stood the watch. The pieces stayed on the table. And the picture they made, now that I'd finally let them make it, was this: a thing older than the towns, who could step out of the world and had thought that was the loneliest thing about him until tonight; who carried a dead someone in a grey place and went back to her when the world got quiet enough; who ran on two cold lights he hadn't made and couldn't replace; and who was walking, with the four of us strung along behind him, straight toward a creature that wore his own impossible strangeness like a second skin, to stand between it and the last peg in the world.

I had followed him out of an inn because I was bored.

I looked at the side of his ruined patient ancient face, the coal of his eye watching a dark that held nothing yet, and I understood that boredom had been the last simple feeling I was going to be allowed for a while.

*  *  *

"Tasha." Mathen, low, when I came off watch and Third had drifted out past the firelight to do the thing I now had a shape for. Mathen was awake, propped on one elbow, and he'd heard, of course he'd heard, the man heard everything.

"You saw it," he said. "Tonight. I watched you see it."

"Some of it."

"Aye, well. Some of it's how it comes." He lay back, looked up at the wound in the sky turning its slow turn. "I told him a thing, our first nights — that a story's how you make more than one person carry the weight. So it doesn't all rest on the one." A long breath. "I didn't think it'd be you so soon. I thought I'd have to do it. I'm a bit relieved, honestly. You're stronger in the back than I am. I just talk about carrying things." He closed his eyes. "Get some sleep. You've got a hard knowing ahead of you, and this was only the start of it. Best be rested for the rest."

I lay down. I did not sleep for a long while. Out past the fire, in the dark, Third stood his watch — and now and then, by signs I had only lately learned to read, he was not standing it at all: gone a breath beside the world, in the grey, with her, whoever she had been, and back before the fire could miss him. Getting the warm part while he still could.

I'd begun to see him. That was the thing about that night. Not all of him. But I'd stopped looking away, and you only get to look away the first time for free.

*  *  *


# Twenty One

The blizzard was waiting for us on the far side of a warm afternoon, and it was not coming, and it was not going. It simply stood.

We came down a long gold slope into grazing country, the kind of soft easy ground that lets you forget for a while, and there across the bottom of it was a wall of white. Not cloud.
