Snow — falling snow, a curtain of it from the ground to as high as I could see, churning, the wind inside it loud enough to hear from where we stood in the warm — and on our side of the line the grass was green and the air was mild and a few paces beyond it the world was a screaming whiteout, and the line between the two was as straight and as hard as the edge of a table.

I had stopped walking without deciding to. "That's—"

"Weather," Mathen said, in the tone of a man who has seen enough of Anka not to argue with it. "It does that, out here. Stands. Or walks at you in a wall, which is worse — you can outrun a storm that knows where it's going. This kind just sits and waits to see if you're fool enough to need the ground it's sitting on." He squinted at it. "We need the ground it's sitting on."

"It'll end," Third said. "They do. But not soon, and not for our convenience. We go through. Keep close, keep the rope, and do not stop inside it — inside it is where it wants you to stop."

We tied on, and we went through the table-edge of it, and the warm afternoon was simply gone, cut off behind us like a closed door, and we were in the white.

I have been cold since. I have not been in anything like that. It was not the cold that took you, it was the *sameness* — no up, no down, no forward that looked different from back, no sound but the one enormous sound, the world reduced to a roaring white nothing with four roped figures struggling through the middle of it. Roan vanished to a shape at rope's-length. The wind ate our voices. And in the middle of it, where you could see nothing and hear nothing and the world had stopped being a world and become only a white waiting, I felt the rope go slack ahead of me, and I knew, the way I knew things about Third now, that he had gone — not fallen, not lost. Stepped. Beside. Into the only quiet there was.

I held the slack rope and I waited, in the screaming white, for him to come back from wherever the stillness had sent him.

&nbsp;

<p align="center">◆ &nbsp; ◆ &nbsp; ◆</p>


After Liwon, Third stopped.

He did not decide to. There was no moment he could afterward point to and say *there, that is where I chose to stop* — there was only the carrying of her, the small weight of what had been her, to a place with a tree he thought she would have liked, and the putting of her into the ground, and the sitting down beside the place to make certain nothing disturbed it, and then the not getting up.

He told himself, at first, that he was keeping watch. That was a thing he knew how to do; he could do it for a long time. But the watch had no end written into it, because the thing he was guarding was past being harmed, and a watch with no end is only a way of describing a thing that has stopped.

The Scar swung overhead, dark and light and dark, faster than it should have, or his sense of it loosened — he could not afterward say which. Greenery came up around him. A vine found his still arm and climbed it and he did not brush it off, because brushing it off was a thing a person did, and he had stopped being sure he was one. The weather passed over him the way water passes over a stone in a stream: he felt it, distantly, as pressure and temperature, and it wore at him, and he let it. He stepped to the grey more and more — a breath gone from the hillside, a long while on the far side of it, the warm part, the part where she still got the rhyme right — and he stayed longer and longer inside each breath, and minded less and less the coming back, until the coming back was the only part he still did out of habit, and even the habit was wearing thin.

He was, he understood without alarm, dying. Not the way the soft ones died. The way a thing winds down. The lights in his chest he could always have kept — he had kept them three hundred thousand spins, a bottle scrounged here, bought there, pried from some dead place, none of the small ones lasting much past a hundred spins before they went to ash, so that staying lit had never been the hard part, only the bothering. And it was the bothering that was failing now: the will of him, the part the Titan had not built and had no name for, going out like a banked fire nobody fed.

He watched the dim light gutter, and did not get up, and did not go looking for the next. He had been there long enough to be more shrub than statue when the woman sat down in front of him.

She did it without ceremony, the way you'd sit on any convenient rock, and she looked at him — at the eye, which still lit, the one thing the vines had not taken — and she said, "You're not dead. I've buried dead. You're something else."

Third did not answer. Answering was a thing a person did.

"That's all right," she said. "I'll talk. I'm good at it, and you appear to have the time."

Her name, he would learn over the slow spins that followed, was Lujian, and she was a wanderer of the particular kind that the loss of everything sometimes makes — not aimless, but unanchored, moving because the places she stopped all had holes in them shaped like people who weren't there. She had been a scholar of something once; she still had the habits of it, the naming, the patience, the need to understand a thing before she would consent to fear it. She had lost — she told him this plainly, early, the way you'd hand a stranger your name — a husband and both her children, in one of the small endless wars the sundered peoples had been making of the world since the night the veins came, and she had walked away from the graves of them and not stopped walking, and she was not, she said, looking for anything, except possibly a reason to be looking for something.

She found the stopped Forged, and she did not loot it, and she did not fear it, and she did not try to wake it. She simply began, each day, to sit in front of it and talk, and to clear, idly, a little of the growth from its joints while she did, the way you'd worry a loose thread.

It took a long time. She talked about the war, and the towns, and the two peoples who could not stop killing each other, and the grey philosophies of a poet she'd loved whose name Third would hear again much later from another mouth on another road. And one day — he did not know how many spins in, the count had stopped meaning anything — she said the thing that woke him, and she did not say it to wake him, she said it because it was true and she was tired and she had stopped, a little, performing patience.

"I don't know what you lost," Lujian said, to the silent overgrown thing. "Something. You've the shape of a thing that lost something. I know the shape; I wear it." She pulled a vine off his wrist. "Here's what I've worked out, in all my walking. There's a thing the world wants you to believe, when it takes someone. It wants you to believe it was *for* something. That it'll make sense later. That they're a — seed, or a lesson, or a step on the way to some better thing, and if you just hold still and trust the design, the grief'll turn into a meaning and you'll be glad." Her voice had gone hard, not at him. "It's a lie. It's the kindest-sounding lie there is and it's still a lie. They weren't *for* anything. They were *theirs.* And the only thing your stopping does — this, the vines, the winding down — the only thing it does is finish the job. It turns them into exactly what the liar wanted. A thing that happened on the way to you sitting down."

The eye, which had not moved in a thousand spins, moved.

"There it is," Lujian said softly, not surprised, only tired and a little glad. "No. Don't — you don't have to say anything. I'm only telling you. If you want them to have been a person and not a *reason*, you don't get to stop. Stopping agrees with the liar. Going on is the only way left to call it what it was." She sat back. "It's not a comfort. I know it's not. It's the opposite of a comfort. It's a debt. But it's theirs, and it's yours, and I'd think a thing that's carried a glaive since before there were towns to carry it past could carry one more thing that heavy."

Third's hand, when he lifted it — the first thing he had moved on purpose in longer than he could hold — closed around the vine she had been worrying, and pulled it, slow, from the wood of his arm.

"They were a child," he said. The voice came out of him cracked and disused, a landslide that had forgotten the shape of words. "I called her little one. She made it into a name."

Lujian nodded, the way you nod at a thing set finally down between you. "Then she had a name," she said. "That's more than most of mine got, at the end. Come on. You've a great deal of going on to do, and the first step's the worst of it; after that it's only walking."

He stood. The growth tore away from him in sheets. He had been a long time stopped, and the standing was its own kind of agony, every wound he had let go unmended announcing itself at once — and under the agony, faint, unwanted, the terrible relief of a debt accepted.

He did not stay stopped again. Whatever else the spins did to him after — and they did the usual things, they gave him Lujian's company on the road for a good span and then, in the way of all of it, they took her too, quieter than Liwon, an old woman's ordinary end with Third sitting the watch he actually knew how to sit this time, the kind with an end in it — he did not, ever again, sit down to wind himself out. She had made that impossible. She had turned his stopping into a thing he would have been doing *to* Liwon, and he would not do one more thing to Liwon, not even the mercy of an ending.

It was, he understood much later, the same refusal twice. The Titan had said *seed.* Lujian had said *hers.* And every spin Third kept walking was a spin he spent on Lujian's side of it.

He stepped back into the white.

&nbsp;

<p align="center">◆ &nbsp; ◆ &nbsp; ◆</p>


The rope went taut. He was there, ahead of me, a dark shape resolving in the white, leaning back into the line of it, and we struggled the last of the way through the blizzard's far wall and out the table-edge of it into — of course — a mild grey ordinary evening, dripping, the screaming cold sealed behind us like it had never been, already shrinking at our backs to a white wall standing patient over grass.

We were all of us soaked and shaking, except Third, who did not shake, but whose eye had gone to the coal again, the lowest ember, the look I had learned meant he had been somewhere and paid for it.

I got the rope off and went to him while Mathen got a fire fighting the damp and Roan stamped feeling back into his feet.

"You went," I said. Not a question; we were past those.

"I did."

"Her again?" I had earned that much, I thought — the one word he'd given me, *her*, and the right to use it.

"Not only her, this time." The ember considered the shrinking white wall. "Someone who came after. Who found me when I had — stopped. And made me start." A pause. "She has been gone a long time also. They are all gone a long time, Tasha. That is the part the grey does not change. I can have the warm parts back, as often as I like, for as long as I can stand them. I cannot have any of them *back.*"

I didn't say anything for a moment. The pieces were on the table, and I had learned to look at them now, and a new one had just been set down, and I did not like the shape it made with the others.

"How many," I said. "Have there been. People who — found you. Started you."

The eye came round to me, and held, and I understood he knew exactly why I was asking, and that he was going to answer honestly because he had decided, somewhere back there in the white, that he was done managing me.

"A few," Third said. "Across a long time. Not many. The child. The one in the snow just now. A swordsman who told a joke about a Grask. A woman who was unwell." The ember was steady. "And a girl who served stew, who followed me out of an inn because she was bored, and who has lately begun to look at me as though she has worked out that she is on a list."

I had. That was the thing. I had just worked out exactly that.

"It's not a curse," he said, gently, which from Third was an enormous gentleness. "I want to be clear. You are not doomed by standing near me. Most of them died of the ordinary things, in the ordinary times, that the soft ones die of, and would have died of them whether they had met me or not. I am not the thing that kills them." The ember dimmed. "I am only the thing that remembers all of them. That is a different curse, and it is mine, and it is not catching."

"That's not better," I said. My voice came out smaller than I meant it.

"No," Third agreed. "It is not better. But it is true, and you have lately preferred true, even when it is not better. I thought you should have this one accurately."

Behind us the blizzard stood its patient ground over the grass, going nowhere, waiting for the next fools who needed the dirt beneath it. Ahead the trail ran on alongside the rim, toward the fields where the glutton's teeth lay scattered, toward the place all the lines pointed. And I walked it knowing, now, that I was the latest entry in a list a very old creature had been keeping for three hundred thousand spins, and that the list had only ever ended one way, and that I was going to keep walking it anyway — which was, I understood with a lurch, exactly the choice a woman named Lujian had once talked him into, in a different ruin, a long time ago.

Going on was the only way to call it what it was.

*  *  *


# Twenty Two

The country had been getting meaner for two days. The trees thinned and then gave up; the green went to a low grey scrub that crunched underfoot and smelled of nothing; the trail stopped being a trail and started being the least-bad line across a waste of broken stone. We were walking toward the place near the edge of the world where the dead were said to crawl, and the land had begun, in the way of lands, to dress for the occasion.

I started talking about home. I do that when the quiet gets too big to stand — I fill it, and I fill it with whatever is smallest and safest, and home was both. I talked about the inn the way you talk about a place once you've put enough hard road between yourself and it that you can no longer remember being bored there. The stove that drew wrong until you learned its mood. Tondi's bucket she'd never once touched a coin out of. The three rooms upstairs that were empty more often than not, and the way the whole building seemed to lean a little toward the fire of an evening, like the rest of us.

Third walked beside me and let me talk, which he was good at — better than anyone I'd known. He had a way of being a silence you could pour into without it spilling.

"And Kern," I said. "There was always Kern."

"Kern."

"A Kr. A fixture. He'd been coming in longer than I'd been closing up, longer than Tondi'd had the place, maybe. Sat at the window table for one cup he'd make last the whole afternoon and never said three words you didn't dig for. I think I went my whole life finding him as much a part of the room as the window. You don't wonder about the window." I smiled at the scrub. "He had this bow. Always had it with him, leaned against the wall by his knee. A beautiful old thing — carved all the way down its length, this pattern too fine to really follow, worn pale and smooth where his shoulder rode it, and red worked down deep into the bindings, the way the Kro do their fine —"

I stopped.

I stopped because I had heard myself, and what I had heard was a description I had heard before, in a different mouth, on a different night, beside a different fire — *exquisitely made, someone had spent a long time on it, cared about every part of it* — a boy by a brook with a drawn bow and shaking arms, who had asked a question instead of loosing the arrow, and a thing of wood and stone that had not had a name for itself yet standing in the water hearing, for the first time in its existence, that a made thing could be beautiful and not just dangerous.

I turned. Third had stopped walking. He was a few paces back, where I'd left him when my feet had kept going and the rest of me hadn't, and the coal of his eye was on me, level and very still, and I understood that he had stopped some sentences ago.

"Third," I said. "Third, that's — the boy. From your story. The one by the water. The one who said—"

"A Blank just used I." His voice came slow, the avalanche moving one stone at a time. "Yes."

"It can't be the same bow." But I said it the way you say a thing you already know is wrong.

"Describe the carving again," he said. "The pattern too fine to follow. Tell me where it starts."

"At the grip. It — it spirals out from the grip, both ways, like it's growing from his hand—"

"From the grip, both ways, like a thing growing," Third said. "Yes. He had only just finished it, I think, when I knew it. There was no smoothness anywhere on it then. The carving was sharp enough to cut a thumb on; I remember thinking a person could ruin their hands keeping a thing that detailed. The red in the bindings was new and bright, not gone to rust the way you would have seen it. And his hands were not steady on it. He was very young, and very frightened, and he had made the most careful thing I had ever seen, and he was pointing it at me with his arms shaking." The eye did not move. "It does not wear smooth in one lifetime, Tasha. It does not wear smooth in fifty. What you wiped around at your window table — what some Kro made last an afternoon over a single cup — may be the same bow, worn by every shoulder that carried it between his and yours. That is what the smoothness is. That is what your hand was brushing past on its way to the next table. The whole length of the world, gone soft under a hundred hundred grips."

I sat down. I didn't decide to; my legs did it. There was a flat stone and I was on it.

"He's dead," I said. Stupidly. "The boy. He's—"

"He was Virek." Third said it gently, which from Third was its own kind of terrible. "You have seen, by now, more than I have told you, what became of the Virek. They are not dead, exactly. They are the Kr. They are the things in the dark with the teeth, too. They went into a light one night and came out as two halves of themselves and neither half remembered being whole. So no — I do not think the boy is dead in the way you mean. I think the boy is *gone* in a way that has no word, the way a river is gone when it reaches the sea — still there, still water, no longer the river. Whatever he became, he was not himself by morning, and he has not been, for longer than there have been words for any of it." A pause, and then the thing that I think he had not meant to say out loud, because his voice dropped under it: "He was the first one. The first thing that ever looked at me and saw a someone instead of a weapon. I have carried that for the entire age of the world, and he forgot it inside a single night, and could not have helped the forgetting, and I do not get to be angry, because there is no one left to be angry at. There is only a bow."

The wind moved the grey scrub. Far off, the thing we were walking toward sat at the edge of everything, patient.

"And it ended up in the corner of a room you were bored in," Third said. "Leaned against a wall. Warming itself at a stove that drew wrong. Some Kro who could not have told you whose hands carved it, drinking one slow cup, keeping it by his knee out of a habit handed down so many times that the reason had worn off it like the carving. That is what I cannot — " and here the avalanche stopped, the long bellows of his chest drawing once, slow, the only sound he made that I would have called grief if I'd dared call it anything. "He is gone past finding. His whole kind is gone past finding. And the bow is leaning on a wall in a town not worth a name, whole, cared for, carried all this way by people who never knew what they were carrying. I do not know what to do with that. I have not known what to do with it since you opened your mouth. It is the best thing I have heard in a thousand spins and I cannot tell whether it is a comfort or the cruelest thing in the world."

I looked up at him. The pieces I'd been carrying since the fireside story slid, all at once, into a different shape — and I saw, the way I was learning to see him now, that this was not only about a boy and a bow. There was something underneath it, something he was holding very still so it wouldn't show, the way you hold a cup you've filled too full. *Her*, I thought, from the night on the watch. *He is thinking about her. He is finding out whether a thing can be gone and not gone at the same time, and he is doing it out loud, on a bow, because the other thing he cannot say yet.*

I did not say any of that. I had learned that much.

"Both," I said instead. "It can be both, Third. I think the worst things and the best things are mostly the same things, looked at from where you're standing." I got up off my stone. "It got carried. That's the part I'd hold onto, if it were mine. Nobody knew, and it got carried anyway. All the way to me."

He looked at me for a long moment, the coal of his eye steady.

"All the way to you," he said. "Yes."

And we walked on, into the meaner country, toward the patient thing — and I found I was no longer thinking about how bored I had been at the inn. I was thinking that I had spent six thousand spins wiping a table beside the first beautiful thing a monster ever saw, and had called it handsome, and in my way, and had never once wondered how it came to be there.

You only get to not-wonder for free the first time.

*  *  *


# Twenty Three

Mathen wanted a story. He always wanted a story — it was the engine that ran him, the way some men run on drink and some on grievance — but that night he wanted one badly enough to go after Third for it, which none of us had ever seen him do.

"You," he said, pointing across the fire with a spoon. "Three hundred thousand spins. Do not tell me you are not carrying a thousand of them. I have met mayflies with better stories than mine and they only got the one afternoon."

"I do not tell stories," Third said. "I report. You have remarked on the difference yourself, at length, the night we met."

"I remarked that you tell the *accurate* one when a person wants the *true* one. So tell me a true one. I'll allow the once. As a favour to an old man who cooked." He'd cooked; we'd eaten; the debt was real and he knew how to collect.

I expected Third to deflect again. He was good at it — a wall you could lean a whole evening against and come away with nothing. Instead the single light of his eye went down a little, the way a coal settles when you stop feeding it, and he was quiet long enough that Roan looked up from oiling a strap, and then Third said, in the voice like stone moving slow over stone:

"I will tell the tree story. It is not mine. But I have the right to it."

And he told it the way you'd tell it to a child, which I understood later was the only way it had ever been told.

There was a little one, he said, who would not sleep. Who cried in a way that had no bottom to it and no door out of it, and there was a person who did not know how to help, who had only ever known how to end things, and who was very frightened of the crying because it was the one thing in the world he could not stop. So one day, out of all his being-frightened, the person did the only thing he had ever known how to do, which was to end things. He broke the wood all around him — pulled the branches down, put his hands through the trunks, made a ruin of a quiet green place — because breaking was the only tool he had, and the crying could not be broken. And when the place was wrecked and the little one was crying still, for of course it was, he picked the little one up, and he stood under the last tree left standing, one of the golden kind, with the leaves that sing when the wind moves them, small and thin and ringing like the inside of a bell — and a bird came down and sat in the branches, not afraid, because the violence was all over by then and the wood had already forgotten it. And the person did not know what to do, so he swayed. Just swayed, under the singing tree, with the bird watching. And the little one stopped crying. And slept. And the person stood there for the whole turn of the dark, not moving, in case moving was the thing that had been wrong all along.

"That is the whole of it," Third said. "There is no battle in it. No one is clever. A frightened thing learns it can be still. That is the entire story."

Mathen, for once in his life, did not say anything.

I was the one who saw it. I'll put it down and you can make of it what you like.

Past the edge of the firelight, where the dark started, there was — I want to be careful — there was the *suggestion* of someone sitting. Small. Knees drawn up the way a child sits to listen, or how a small grown person sits, I couldn't have said which. Listening with its whole self, as you only listen to a story you already know and love anyway. I'd swear the firelight went a little way *into* it instead of stopping at it, the way light goes into smoke. And it was smiling — I could feel that more than see it — the easy fond smile of someone hearing their favourite part land right.

I looked away to find my own breath, and when I looked back the dark was only dark.

"Wally," Third said. Soft. Into the place where the someone had been.

It meant nothing to me. I had never heard the name. But the way he set it down — gentle, like a cup you didn't want to wake anyone with — told me it was a name, and that it belonged to someone, and that the someone was not at this fire and was somehow, also, not entirely not at this fire.

"He liked the tree story best," Third said, and I noticed he said *liked* and then, a breath later, fixed it, quiet, only for himself: "—likes. He says mine is not as good as it used to be." The light of his eye came back up to ordinary. "He may be right. A story wears where you hold it."

"Who's—" Roan started, with the open face of a boy who asks the question everyone's thinking, and I caught his eye across the fire and gave the smallest shake of my head, and bless him, he stopped. Because I'd understood something I couldn't have said out loud yet. That there are tellings a person does for the listener, and tellings a person does *as* a keeping — the way you'd carry water cupped in your hands a long way, not because you're thirsty, but because someone down the road is. That Third had not told us the tree story.

He had told it so that it would go on being told. So that whoever it was out past the light would have, for the length of a story, somewhere warm to be.

"It is a good one," I said, when the quiet had gone on long enough to start to hurt. "Your story. The true one. It's a good one, Third."

"It is the good part," he said. And he put another piece of wood on the fire, though the fire did not need it, and we sat with it a while, the four of us and the maybe-fifth, in the singing nothing where the leaves would have been if there'd been a tree, before Mathen cleared his throat and started, very gently, on one of his own.

*  *  *


# Twenty Four

Mathen had told us about the fields while the stones were still falling, hunched under that overhang with the sky cracking outside, and I had pictured something terrible. I always do. The thing itself was worse and better than the picture, the way the real things on Anka always were: not terrible. Quiet. A quiet that had teeth in it, but you had to earn the teeth.

We came up onto the fields near the middle of a Scar-light, and they ran away from us yellow and red to the rim of sight — heather, knee-high, the colour of an old bruise going gold, rippling in a wind that smelled of nothing at all. And standing up out of the heather, scattered across the whole vast gentle slope of it, were the stones.

They were like plinths. That was the nearest I could come to it — squared-off blocks of white going to the grey of old bone, planted up out of the heather at all different heights, none of them taller than a person, some no higher than my knee, some that I'd have had to look up at a little, no two the same, each one standing alone with a space of grass around it as though the ground itself kept a polite distance. There was no pattern to where they stood, and no pattern to how tall, and that was somehow the worst of it — a field of pale blocks at every height between ankle and brow, scattered to the rim of sight with no more sense to the arrangement than a thing a child sets down and forgets.

"Teeth," Mathen said, soft, because the field was the kind of place that takes your voice down whether you decide to or not. "The glutton's. They pulled them out of his head in the war — imagine the size of the thing that wore these — and they came down all over the world and stuck where they landed, and grass grew up around them, and here we are walking through the dropped teeth of a god, calling it a field." He looked almost happy, in the way Mathen got happy at a thing that was too big to be anything but a story. "You can't make this up. You wouldn't dare."

I put my hand on the nearest one as we passed, the way I'd touched the causeway pillar, because apparently that's the kind of fool I am, I have to touch the impossible thing to believe it's let me. It was warm. Not sun-warm. Warm like a thing that had been alive once and hadn't entirely got the message. I took my hand back.

And then I saw the rocks that floated.

*  *  *

There were clusters of them, here and there among the plinths — fist-sized chunks of the same white, a dozen or so to a clump, and they hung in the air. Not falling. Not rising. Just *there*, at about chest height, turning very slowly around nothing, the way dust turns in a shaft of light, except these were stones, and there was no light-shaft, and there was no reason. They drifted, when the wind took them, but lazily, like they were only being polite to the wind and could have ignored it if they'd liked.

"Don't," Third said, when I drifted toward the nearest clump the way you drift toward anything that shouldn't be. It was the first he'd spoken since we came up onto the field, and I stopped.

"They float," I said, which was stupid, but I was past managing my mouth.

"They float." He came up beside me, his eye on the clump, level and careful. "The teeth do not like the black glass that veins the world; they shy from it, push away from it, and a chip with a vein beneath it will hang in the air, slowly turning, for as long as you leave it be. A small one gets carried home as a charm — a strange, harmless thing that tugs out of your hand toward any glass you bring near it." A pause. "People come here and harvest them. It is a trade. There is a town, rim of the fields, that lives on nothing else."

"So they're safe."

"Those are safe," Third said. "Those." And he did not point, because pointing is a motion, and I was about to learn why that mattered. "The trouble is that not all of them are those. And from here, with your eyes, you cannot tell which are which. Neither can Mathen. Neither can Roan." The eye came round to me. "I can. Mostly. It is the only reason I am useful on this particular ground, and it is why, from here to the far side, you are going to do a thing you will hate, which is exactly what I tell you, exactly when I tell you, and nothing else."

Behind me Roan said, "What's the difference?" and his voice had the careful flatness of a man who has already guessed he won't like the answer.

"The difference," Third said, "is that some of these clouds wake up when something moves."

*  *  *

He showed us, because Mathen asked him to and because we needed to see it once to believe it enough to obey. He found a clump a good way off — *that one, watch it, do not move toward it* — and he took a stone from the ground and threw it, hard, so that it skittered through the grass near the floating cluster.

The cluster came apart.

That's the only way I can say it. The dozen polite turning stones simply *stopped being polite*, and the whole clump swept down off its place in the air and went for the moving stone like a dog off a chain, fast, a swirling shrieking cone of rock that hit the place the thrown stone had stopped and tore the heather to green rags in a circle the width of a room, and hung there, swirling, waiting — and when nothing else moved, it slowed, and rose, and gathered itself back up to chest height a few paces from where it had started, turning slowly around nothing, polite again. Patient again. A dozen fist-sized stones hanging in the gold light, for all the world exactly like the harmless ones forty paces to its left.

Nobody said anything for a moment.

"How many of them are the bad kind," I said.

"On a field this size? Perhaps one in six. Perhaps one in five today; they seem more numerous the closer you come to the Graveyard, and we are coming to the Graveyard." Third turned and looked at the long gold slope of standing teeth running away ahead of us, dotted everywhere with those slow turning clumps, harmless and lethal and identical. "We cross between them. We move when I say, in the line I walk, and we do not hurry, because hurrying is motion and motion is the thing they listen for. When I stop, you stop — not slow down, *stop*, mid-step if I say it mid-step, and you hold, however long it takes, until I move again. A cloud may come near you. It may come very near you. If it has not woken to *you* — if you have given it nothing to wake to — it will lose interest and drift off. The whole of staying alive on this field is being, for as long as necessary, less interesting than a stone."

"And the Scar," Mathen said, looking up. The dark edge of it was a long way off yet, but it was there, spinning its slow way toward us, the way it always was.

"And the Scar," Third agreed. "In the dark I cannot tell them apart either. So we are across before the light goes. Which means we begin now, and we do not waste the moving on being afraid. Be afraid while you are standing still. You will have time."

*  *  *

I will not pretend I can tell it the way it happened, because the way it happened was mostly nothing, for a very long time, and the nothing was the most frightening thing I have ever done.

We went out into the field in a line behind Third, slow, deliberate, threading the gaps between the clumps along a path only he could see. He would walk — six steps, ten — and then his hand would drop, the small flat gesture that meant *stop*, and we would stop, all of us, mid-stride, and stand among the white plinths with our held breath loud in our own ears while somewhere a cloud we maybe hadn't even clocked turned and turned and decided we were furniture. Then his hand would lift, and we would move again. The discipline of it got into my legs as an ache and then as a tremble. You do not know how much your body wants to move until you forbid it to, on open ground, with death hanging at chest height a few paces off and your every nerve screaming that the one correct thing a body can do is *run.*

Roan had it worst. I understood that, watching the back of his neck — that for Roan, who'd spent his whole short life learning that the brave thing and the moving thing were the same thing, this field was a kind of torture, because here the brave thing was to do nothing, perfectly, while your whole self demanded you act. Once, early, a clump woke a little — not at us, at a hare that broke from the heather and bolted — and the cloud went after the hare in a shrieking rush and caught it and there was a sound I won't describe and then a stillness, and the cloud, unsatisfied, came drifting back toward the nearest other moving thing it had half-noticed, which was us, which was *Roan*, on the end of the line, and I watched every muscle in him lock against the want to bolt, and I watched him win, because Third had stopped beside him and gone utterly still in that way only a thing made of wood can manage, and Roan matched him, breath for unbreath, until the cloud lost the thread of us and rose and turned polite again an arm's length from Roan's face. An arm's length. He told me, after, that he had counted the stones. That there were eleven. That he was going to know how many were in a Grinding Teeth cloud for the rest of his life whether he wanted to or not.

I got the one graze of the crossing, and I got it from being good, not bad — I'd stopped clean on Third's signal, but a clump I hadn't seen drifted across our held line on the wind, just *drifting*, not woken, and one of its slow stones clipped the back of my arm as it passed, a single cold line of pain and a single line of blood, the stone not even interested, just close. I did not make a sound. I was proud of that for about a day. The stone turned on past me and rejoined its clump and hung there, and Third's hand lifted, and we went on.

We came off the field on the far side as the dark edge of the Scar was eating the gold out of the heather behind us, close enough that the last hundred paces had a speed in them Third permitted only because the clumps had thinned. And we stood on firm rising ground with our legs shaking from the held stillness more than the walking, and behind us the Gnawing Fields went grey and then went dark, a whole quiet field of a god's pulled teeth with its lethal patient clouds turning in it, invisible now, and I thought: people *harvest* that. There is a town that lives on walking out into that and coming back. The world is full of people doing impossible things and calling it a trade.

*  *  *

It came on us slowly, the way a true horizon does — not a thing you turn and see, but a thing that is gradually more there each time you look up, until you understand it has been resolving in front of you for some while and you only now have the eyes to admit it.

It started as a darker line under the rimward sky, where the failing light went and did not come back, and I took it, the first while, for the dark simply being thicker over there. Then the line had a top edge to it, level and certain, and I thought, weather again, the flat grey kind. Then, as we walked and the light raked along it low, the top edge stayed put and the bottom of it lifted clear of the country, and I understood I was looking at a cliff — a wall of pale grey stone standing sheer out of the land, running off both ways past where the light would let me follow, and so high that I had to tip my head to find the top of it, and tipping my head was when it stopped being scenery and started being a fact about the world I now had to live inside.

And out from the foot of it, leaning into the last of the light, were the sheets.

That was the shape of them. Not spires, not splinters — *sheets.* Slabs of the same pale stone as the cliff, broad and flat and thin for their size, peeling away from the cliff-face and canted over at angles, the way the pages of a great book fall open and lean away from the spine when you crack the cover and lay it down — that, exactly that, a hardcover the size of a country laid open on its back, the cliff its standing spine and the slabs its falling pages, except the pages were each as tall as the causeway was long and there were hundreds of them, near the face crowded close and upright, further out leaning further, further still lying fallen flat in the grass, a whole vast splayed book of stone with its leaves canted every way across the country at the foot of the wall.

"That's it," I said. Not a question. I'd learned, by then, to stop making them questions. "That's where he's going. The last one."

"That is where he is going," Third said. The coal of his eye was steady on the far pale slabs, and his voice had the flatness it got when he was looking at a thing he had already decided to walk into. "And we are going to be standing in it when he arrives."

Behind us the teeth of one dead god did not go dark with the rest of the field. They came up pale instead. Every plinth we'd threaded past wore a fur of chalky moss I hadn't marked in the daylight, and as the light failed the moss began to glow — a soft dead-white light, plinth after plinth of it, until the whole field we'd just crossed lay behind us glowing like a market of grave-candles nobody had lit. It was beautiful. I've given up apologising for the number of terrible places in this account that were also beautiful; the country never seemed to feel the two were in any tension, and had stopped waiting for me to catch up. Mathen named it — bone moss, that grew on nothing but the bone of the old dead and lit up like that every night, so the field-foragers could spot a fresh fall from a mile off. You could chew it for a hurt, he said; it numbed a wound better than anything short of a Mender. And you could always tell the ones who'd worked the Fields too long by their teeth, which it stained a permanent grey. He said it lightly, the way he said most things. I looked back at a field of a dead god's teeth furred in a moss that numbed you and marked you, glowing softly in the dark, and thought: not a garden. Whatever else this was, I had to stop expecting it to turn into a garden.

Ahead of us the great book of stone leaned open in the last of the light, page on canted page. And somewhere between the two, four of us stood on the shaking ground and looked at the place all the lines had been pointing since a town we never saw died with its lockbox open, and none of us said the obvious thing, which was that we had finally nearly arrived, and that arriving had never once, on this whole road, turned out to be the good part.

*  *  *


# Twenty Five

We reached the foot of it the next light, having crossed first a ring of fern and grey moss that softened the country as we came in — almost kind, almost a garden.

It was in that almost-garden that I saw the willows. A whole grove of them, pale-barked and drooping, lovely the way green growth is lovely after a hard country — except that they weren't green. Not one leaf of them. Every tree in the grove had gone a flat, dead, matte grey, crown to root, as though the colour had been lifted out of them in the night and never given back, and they hung over the path utterly still, a grove of grey ghosts that had every other sign of being alive. I slowed. I couldn't help it. "Greywillow," Mathen said, and for once there was none of the performer in it. "They go like that near a thin place — the colour drains out of them when the grey's awake close by. My gran's country had a stand of them, and you did not build downwind of it, and you did not ask why, you just didn't." He looked up the path toward the cliff. "Folk leave the old groves alone. Never knew there was one out here." Third said nothing at all, which by then I'd learned to hear as the loudest thing in the company — Third, who named everything, walking under a grove gone grey because the world was thin here, and not troubling to give us a single fact about it, because the trees had already said the only fact that mattered. We went up between them. Not one leaf moved. And I understood, before Third said a word of it that night, that we'd come to the place where the grey pressed so close it had reached up out of the ground and pulled the colour out of living wood — just to show us it could.

Nearer, the almost-garden gave out to bare rock again, swept clean, as though the slabs did not permit much to grow within reach of them. And then we were in among them, and there is no part of me that was ready for being in among them.

Up close the open book was not a picture anymore. It was a place, and the place had a ceiling. Each slab went up past anything I had a feel for — the causeway laid on its end, and then more — pale grey, broad and flat and thin for its size the way a page is thin, and they leaned. Near the cliff they stood near-upright and crowded close, so that walking in among them was walking into a narrowing dark, slab-faces rising sheer on both sides with a ribbon of Scar-light far up at the top; and the further out you went the further they leaned, and the further apart they stood, until at the rim of the field they lay flat and fallen in the moss, whole toppled pages the length of a village street, with grass growing along their upper edges.

"They fall away from the wall," Third said, low — the slabs took your voice the way the fields had, and worse. "Always away. Never toward it. A new one stands up at the face; the old ones lean out, over a long time, and at the end they go over, out there, at the rim." He put a wooden hand flat against the nearest upright slab, almost gentle. "It is a slow thing. You will not see it happen."

"How slow," Roan said.

"That," said Third, "is the difficulty."

*  *  *

We learned the difficulty the way you learn most true things, which is by not believing them first.

Mathen, who had a mapmaker's habit in him along with all the others, took a stick of char and a scrap of hide and began, as we picked our way in, to set down the slabs we passed — this one tall and leaning spinward, this gap, this fallen one we climbed over, three close-set ones like a splayed hand — building us a way back out, because getting lost in there was a thing your body understood as a danger before your head caught up. It was good work. He was good at it. We went in perhaps three hundred paces, found a fallen slab that made a kind of sheltered angle against an upright one, agreed it would do to leave the packs, and turned to go back out by Mathen's map.

The map was wrong.

Not smudged. Not mistaken. Wrong, the way a room is wrong when you come back and a chair has moved — the splayed hand of three was a splayed hand of two, and there was a new one, tall and pale and upright, standing in a spot Mathen swore on his mother had been open grass when we passed it, and the fallen slab we'd climbed was still fallen but it was *facing the other way.* Mathen stood with his good map in his hand and his mouth open, and turned it over, and turned it back, and Third said, into the quiet:

"Do not trust it. You cannot map this place. I should have said so sooner; I had hoped to let you find a thing to do with your hands." He looked up the narrowing dark toward the unseen cliff. "It moves. Not while you watch — never while you watch, that is the whole cruelty of it — but the moment a place is behind you, or your eyes are shut, or you have only looked away to speak to the person beside you, it remakes itself. A new page at the spine. An old one over at the rim. And everything between, shuffled, by a hand you will never catch at it." A pause. "It has been doing this since before there were eyes here to be fooled. It never grows larger. It never wears down. There are exactly as many slabs in this field today as there were when the Titan dropped the book, and not one of them is where it was."

I looked at the slab beside me — tall, leaning, a crack running up its face like a seam — and I did the thing he'd told us was useless, I *stared* at it, I tried to catch it, I held it with my eyes and dared it to so much as breathe. It didn't. Of course it didn't. And the not-moving, while I watched, was somehow more frightening than if it had lunged, because I understood that the stillness was a courtesy it extended to my attention, and the instant I spent my attention elsewhere the courtesy was withdrawn.

Then, somewhere off in the field, there was a crack like thunder on a clear day — I jumped, we all jumped — and Third said, without turning, "A new one, at the face. Stood up just now." And a while later, far out at the rim behind us, a softer sound, a *pop*, and then a long grinding crash that went on too long and shook the ground through the soles of my boots. "And an old one down, at the edge. The book turned a page." He said it the way you'd report the time. "We are camping at the rim tonight, on the fallen ones, in the open, where nothing stands tall enough to come down on us. Not in here. Things have built homes in here, or on the slabs. They are not here anymore, and neither are the homes."

*  *  *

We made camp the way he said, out on the toppled slabs at the field's edge, a flat pale floor under us the size of a market square that had been, until some unwitnessed night, a standing page of stone a hundred men high. Mathen got a fire going in the lee of a fallen edge and was quiet over it, which was its own kind of alarm; the absence of Mathen's noise told you more about a situation than most men's shouting. Roan kept looking back into the field, into the narrowing dark of the upright slabs, the way you look at a thing you've decided not to turn your back on, which in there was a doomed ambition.

I went and found Third at the edge of the firelight, where he had taken up the watch he didn't need to be told to take.

"So," I said. "We're here." I had stopped, by then, dressing my hard questions up as easy ones. "We can't fight him — the tor showed us that, you can't fight a thing that steps out of the world. We can't map the ground; the ground won't hold still long enough to plan on. He's coming to take the last one, the big one, and it's somewhere up against that cliff, and we're going to be standing in it when he arrives." I looked at the side of his ruined patient face. "Standing in it doing *what,* Third? What's the plan? Because I've followed you a long way now, and I've watched you know things, and I need you to know this one."

He was quiet for a long time. Long enough that the fear had room to grow in it.

"I do not know," Third said.

The fire ticked.

"I have turned it every way I have," he went on, and there was that missing thing in his voice again, the certainty gone out of it, the way it had gone on the tor. "There is no maneuver. You have seen what he is. I cannot stop him taking the thing by standing where the thing is; he will simply be on the other side of me. I cannot kill what I cannot touch. I cannot out-wait a man who does not seem to age and runs on lights like mine. I have no plan, Tasha, of the kind you mean — the kind with steps in it, where you do this and then that and then it is won." The coal of his eye settled on the dark mass of the cliff. "I have only this. That for reasons I do not understand, of everything in the world he might have looked at on that tor, he looked at *me.* That he half-knew me. That a thing this old and this careful does not waste a glance, and he spent one on me, and his mouth moved." A pause. "Whatever this is, I am part of it. Not a sword that happens to be nearby. Part of it. The shape of it has a place in it that is shaped like me. I do not know what the place is for. But I know that if I am not standing in it when he comes, then I have left the one piece of this that might matter sitting back at an inn, being bored." The eye came to me, and there was something in it that was almost an apology. "That is the whole of my plan. To be the piece of it that is shaped like me, in the place where it happens, and to find out what I am for when I find out. It is the thinnest thing I have ever asked anyone to stand on. You may say so."

"It's the thinnest thing you've ever asked anyone to stand on," I said.

"Yes."

"I'm still here."

"I know," he said. "That is the part I cannot make sense of either, and have stopped trying."

*  *  *

He left me at the fire and walked out a little way onto the flat fallen page, alone, into the dark past the light, and I knew the shape of it by now — that he was going to step beside the world — out of it and into the grey — the way you'd walk out into a storm to feel which way it drove, instead of watching it come from a dry window. I had learned not to follow with anything but my eyes, and there was a point each time past which even my eyes lost him: the dark would close over the place he'd stood, and I would sit, and wait for it to give him back. He was gone longer than the carts. Longer than the boundary stone. And when he came back into the firelight he came wrong — not lessened in the grieving way, the lowest-ember way, but *unsteady,* the way you'd come back from a deck in a swell, one hoof set down and tested before the next, his eye flaring and guttering like a flame in a draft that couldn't find its level.

"Third?" Mathen was up. Roan was up.

"The grey is wrong here." He said it quietly and it landed like the grinding crash had. "It is always still. In all the time I have known it — empty, patient, still. Here it is — moving. Churned. There are currents in it I have never felt, all of them running the one way, toward the cliff, the way water runs toward a hole in the bottom of a bucket." He steadied himself against the fallen edge. "Something is draining the grey, here, into that wall. Or out of that wall, into the grey. I could not tell which, and I did not stay to learn, because the longer I stood in it the harder it pulled, and I am not certain — " the ember flickered — "I am not certain that if I had stayed much longer I would have been able to step back out."

Nobody said anything. The fire ticked. Out in the dark field, unwitnessed, a page of the great book turned itself over with a crack like thunder, and none of us so much as flinched at it now, because it had stopped being the frightening thing in the night.

"Then we don't sleep alone," Mathen said at last, practical, gathering us with it. "Watches in pairs. Eyes on the field and eyes on him." He looked at Third, gently. "Especially him."

And that was how we came to wait, the four of us, at the rim of a thing that rearranged itself in the dark, under a sky with a wound turning in it, on the thinnest plan a very old creature had ever asked anyone to stand on, for a man who could walk out of the world — while behind the cliff that held the last peg in creation, the grey itself had begun, quietly, to pour toward the place where it would end.

*  *  *


# Twenty Six

We kept the watches in pairs, the way Mathen said, and I made myself Third's pair before anyone could think too hard about who'd draw the short straw of sitting up beside the one who didn't sleep. It wasn't bravery. I want that on the record. It was that I'd seen him come back off the flat slab unsteady, testing each hoof like a man on a deck, and I had decided — somewhere I hadn't asked permission of — that I was not going to let him do whatever he was about to do with nobody watching but the stones.

So Roan and Mathen took the first half, back to back on the fallen page with the fire between them and the field, and when the Scar swung its dark edge up over us and the cold came down, they woke us, and Mathen put a hand on my shoulder in passing and said nothing, which from Mathen was a whole speech, and then it was me and Third and the fire and the rearranging dark.

For a while we just kept it. The field did its work out there in the black — a crack like thunder, far off; a long grinding fall, further; the book turning its pages where no eye could hold them still. I'd stopped flinching. You'd be amazed what you stop flinching at.

"You're going to go across again," I said, eventually. It wasn't a question. None of mine were, anymore.

"I must." The coal of his eye was on the cliff, that black mass blacker than the dark around it. "He is coming. I do not know when. But the grey will know before the world does — it always knows the weather of a thing before the thing arrives — and I would rather meet him understanding something than meet him understanding nothing." A pause. "It is dangerous, here, to cross. You saw. It pulls. I told you it pulled."

"You told me you weren't sure you could've got back."

"I was not. I am still not." He turned the eye on me, and there was a request in it he plainly hated to make, because Third hated needing things, it was the most stubborn fact about him after the not-dying. "Which is why I am going to ask you for something I do not know how to ask for gracefully. I will not go all the way across tonight — going all the way across, out there, is how I nearly did not come back. I will hold at the edge of it, a foot in each, and reach. But the reaching is its own danger, and I need a thing to hold to on this side. A line back. The grey is quiet and it is patient and tonight it is *pulling,* and the danger is not that something in it hurts me. The danger is that it becomes easier to stay than to come back, and I stop wanting the coming back, and there is no edge to tell me I have waited too long." The fire ticked. "You are loud, Tasha. In the lived world. You have always been the loudest thing in any quiet I have stood in. I would like, if you are willing, to hold to that. To you. Talking. So that there is a thing on this side worth the difficulty of returning to."

I did not trust my voice for a second. Then I said, "You want me to nag you back from the dead."

"Essentially."

"Third, I have been told my whole life that's my worst quality. You're saying it's a rescue technique."

"I am saying," he said, with enormous gravity, "that tonight it may be the most useful thing in the world, and I would take it as a kindness if you did not stop, no matter how little I appear to be listening, and that if I tell you to stop, you must understand that the part of me asking you to stop is the part you are saving me from." The eye held me. "Will you."

"Yeah," I said. "Talk you back. I can do that. I'm good at that."

"I know," he said. "Begin when I close the eye. Do not stop until I open it."

*  *  *

And then he set himself against it — not leaving, though leaving was the word that came; more the way a swimmer sets against a current that wants him, half his weight going out of the lived world and the other half digging in to stay — the eye dimming down not to the grieving coal but to almost-out, the last red wire of it, the wooden shape of him gone rigid with the work of being in two places and surrendering to neither. And I started talking.

I don't remember the half of what I said. That's the truth of it. You don't, after; it isn't speech you're making, it's rope. I talked about the inn. I talked about Tondi and her water cup and the way she never touched a coin, and the stupid sign that said *Only*, and Klar's first attempt at Shredder stew that he'd been so proud of, and the way the militia drank their courage before patrol and Roan was the one who didn't, and the smell of the place in the morning before the stew went on. I talked about my father telling me never to let them see my fear. I talked about the road, every dull stretch of it, the bog and the blizzard and the field of stinging glass, all the things that had nearly killed us made small and ours and *survived,* because that was the point, I understood that even while I was doing it — I wasn't telling him true things, I was telling him *ongoing* things, things with more of them still to come, a world that was not finished and therefore worth the difficulty of returning to.

His eye stayed almost-out. The cold got worse. Out in the field the great book turned a page with a crack that I felt in the fallen slab under me, and I did not stop talking. A while into it — I don't know how long, time goes strange when you're being a rope — the red wire of his eye began, very slightly, to *drift,* the way a moored boat drifts when the line goes slack, his whole still shape leaning a hair toward the cliff, toward the black mass and the pull behind it, and something in me that wasn't thinking said his name instead of all the other words, just his name, *Third, Third, you've got stew to complain about, Third, I'm not done telling you about the sign* — and the drift stopped. Held. Hung there at the end of the slack line that was apparently my idiot voice. And then, slowly, came back.

The eye came up. Not all the way. But up, and toward me, and the rest of him came back into one place, the hoof testing the slab, the deck-in-a-swell carefulness, and I shut up at last because he'd opened the eye and that was the deal, and I was shaking, and I hadn't noticed I'd taken hold of his wooden forearm with both hands at some point and was gripping it like the rail of a thing going down.

"You came back," I said.

"You were very loud," he said. "It was very difficult to stay." And then, lower, the nearest thing to *thank you* his pride would let out the door: "I heard the part about the sign. It is a good detail. The crafter illustrated the only building in town." A breath of the bellows, the one I'd allow him. "I held to that. The absurdity of it. A world that makes a sign like that is a world with more in it. You were right to choose it."

*  *  *

I let him sit a while before I asked. I'd earned the asking and he knew it, but I let him sit.

"What did you see."

The eye went to the cliff again, but it didn't drift this time; he had it on a short line now, the both of us did.

"It is worse than I said, and simpler. The grey is always empty, Tasha. In all the time I have known it — and that is the whole of my time — it has been a still grey nothing with the lived world showing through it like a drowned town through ice. Patient. Untenanted." The wire of his eye flickered. "It is not empty here. It is *draining* — all of it, the whole patient nothing, pouring slow toward that cliff the way a flood finds the one crack in a wall — and it is carrying things with it. Shapes. Far off, and dim, and being drawn the way I was being drawn, toward the wall, toward whatever is behind the wall that has begun, already, before he has even arrived, to pull the world apart at its last pin." He stopped.

"And," I said, because there was an *and,* I could hear the shape of the held-back thing.

"And one of them turned." Very quiet. "Out of all that dim drifting cargo of the grey, one shape, far off, in the churn — it stopped being carried, and it turned, against the current, the way a thing turns when it has noticed it is being looked at. Toward me. And I do not know what it was." The eye came to me, and the not-knowing in it was the worst thing I had seen in his face on the whole road, worse than the lowest coal. "I have spent tonight telling myself it was the man from the tor, come early through the grey, because that would at least be a thing I could name. But I am not certain. It was too far, and there was something in the *way* of it that was older than him, older than me, and it knew me, Tasha, the way the man on the tor knew me, that half-knowing, that hearing-a-childhood-tune — except this was not half. This one knew me entirely. And I have never in three hundred thousand spins been known entirely by anything, and I find I did not like how much I wanted to go to it." The bellows, again, ragged. "That is why I needed the rope. Not the pull of the grey. The pull of being known. I would have gone to it. You stopped me going to it."

I kept hold of his forearm. The fire had burned down and neither of us fed it.

"Then I'll keep the rope," I said. "However many nights it takes. You don't look into that thing without me on the other end of you. That's the deal now. You don't get to be known to death by something in the dark while I'm asleep ten feet away."

He looked at me for a long moment, the eye steadying toward something like its proper light.

"That is a great deal to take on," he said, "for a girl who followed me out of an inn because she was bored."

"Yeah, well." I looked out at the rearranging dark, the field that wouldn't hold still, the cliff that was quietly drinking the grey and everything in it. "I'm not bored now."

We kept the watch. Out past the firelight the book turned its pages where we couldn't see, and far behind the cliff the grey poured toward its ending, carrying its dim cargo of shapes, one of which had turned to look at a thing made of wood and known it entirely — and on the lived side of it all, a girl held a construct's forearm with both hands and talked, every time the eye began to drift, about a sign that said *Only*, and a stew nobody should have been proud of, and all the small unfinished things that are, when you are trying to keep someone in the world, the whole and only argument for staying in it.

*  *  *


# Twenty Seven

The second night of the waiting was worse than the first, because on the first night the dread had been a new thing and you can stand almost anything once for its novelty, and on the second night it was just the furniture. The cliff drank the grey. The book turned its pages out in the dark. We sat our paired watches and ate cold and spoke little, and Roan had got to that brittle place a body gets when it has been afraid for longer than it can keep being interestingly afraid, where a person will either find something to do with the fear or start coming apart at a seam.

It was Mathen who saw it, of course, and Mathen who knew the cure, because the cure was the thing Mathen was for.

"Third," he said, into the cold, in the easy way he had of setting a hook so gently you didn't feel it go in. "You owe me a story. I've told every one I have twice over on this road, and you've told us exactly one, and it was a good one, but a man can't live off one. You've had — how long did you say? Don't answer that, it depresses the children. You've had longer than anyone. You must have a hundred you've never told a soul."

"I am not good at it," Third said. "You are good at it. I state things."

"Then state me one." Mathen settled back against the fallen slab, hat down. "You taught me yourself, near enough — no, *I* taught *you*, that's the one, a story's how you make more than one person carry a thing. You've a great deal you've been carrying alone, master Forged. Set some of it on the fire where the rest of us can warm our hands at it. That's all a story is. Spreading the load."

The coal of Third's eye sat on the fire a while. Then it did a thing I'd not seen it do — it went not to the cliff, not down into him, but somewhere sideways and warm, the look of a person opening a drawer they keep meaning not to.

"I will tell you about Dorn," he said.

I sat up at that. I knew the name. I'd known it since the inn, the corner table, the Boltcaster he carried and never fired — *Dorn's*, he'd said, the way you'd name a grave. I knew the name and the fact of the death and not one thing else about the man, and I found I badly wanted to.

*  *  *

"Dorn was a fighter," Third said, "the way some are born singers — without effort, and without much sense of it being remarkable, which made him very restful to be near. We travelled together a long while. He hunted things that needed hunting, monsters that were troubling the roads, and he never once did it for the bounty, though he took the bounty, because, he said—" and here Third's flat voice did something almost like an impression, a fond clumsy reaching after another man's warmth — "'because it is the right thing to do, Third. The coin is just the icing on the cake.' I did not know what a cake was. He drew me one. He was not a good artist either, but he was a patient teacher of useless things, which it turns out are the things you miss."

Roan, despite himself, huffed a small laugh. Mathen said nothing, watching Third the way one craftsman watches another work in a difficult medium.

"He told jokes," Third went on. "Badly, mostly. But he had one — one he was known for, that he would tell to anyone who would hold still — and it was, I am told, genuinely good, though I am the wrong judge of such things. I have remembered it exactly. May I tell it the way he told it."

"Please," said Mathen, and there was no mockery in it at all.

Third set down, deliberately, the thing he'd been holding without my noticing he'd taken it out — the Boltcaster, Dorn's Boltcaster, the squat ugly wooden thing he carried and never loaded — laid it across his knees like a man laying out a friend's coat. And he told the joke.

"A Mender, a Crafter, and a Grask are arguing over whose work matters most. The Mender says, 'Mine — I keep the whole world from coming apart at the seams; were it not for me you would all go about in rags.' The Crafter says, 'Mine — I *make* the world to begin with; were it not for me you would have no seams to come apart, and nothing to go about in, rags or otherwise.' And they argue. Mending against making. Making against mending. The length of a long night, each entirely certain the world would stop turning without him. And at last, having settled nothing, they turn to put it to the third one — the Grask, who has sat the whole time and said not one word. 'And you,' they say. 'What is it that *you* do, that matters?'"

Third paused, exactly the length Dorn must have paused.

"But there is only a hole in the ground, where the Grask had been sitting. And from somewhere beneath their own two feet, a voice says: 'There you are.'"

There was a beat of nothing — and then Roan barked a real laugh, the first true one I'd heard out of him since the tor, the kind that surprises its owner, and Mathen laughed properly, head back, and even I felt the thing crack open in my chest that a good joke cracks, the relief of it, in that awful place, under that drinking cliff.

"Oh, that's *good,*" Mathen said, wiping his eye. "That's properly good. The two trades so busy being important they don't notice the world's already eating them. Your Dorn had timing, Third, you can hear it even through you, no offence. I'll have to remember that one." He was still smiling. "A Mender, a Crafter, and a Grask. I'll get a tenday's drinks off that, easy."

"He would have liked that you laughed," Third said. "He measured a day by how many he could make laugh. He would have counted tonight a good day, this far from anywhere, with the world the way it is."

And the smile on Mathen's face was the last easy thing about the night, though none of us knew it yet, and the joke about the Grask hung in the cold a moment longer than a joke should, and then the fire popped and we let it go.

*  *  *

"How did he die," I asked, when the laughing had settled, because I'd learned that the only respect worth giving Third was to ask the real thing.

"In the night," Third said. "The way they do." The fond drawer had closed; the voice was flat again, but it was the flatness I'd learned to hear *under* now, the flatness that was holding something down. "We were camped off the road. Hunting a Subsurface Shredder that had been taking travellers on a stretch outside a village — for the bounty, and because it was the right thing to do; he had me saying it by then. He was asleep. I was keeping the watch; I do not sleep, it should always have meant nothing could come on us unseen." The Boltcaster lay across his knees. "It did not come from where seeing helps. It came from below. A Shredder puts a limb up through the ground before any part of it you could watch for breaks the surface, and the first I knew of it was the sound, and the sound was already through him. One limb. Through the chest. Before I had finished standing up."

The fire ticked. Nobody moved.

"I killed it," Third said. "That part was not difficult; they are only difficult to the people who cannot see them coming, and I could see it, after. It is a small skill, killing the thing that has already done the thing. I have a great deal of it." The eye stayed on the fire. "He was alive a little while. Long enough to ask me to remember him, which he said would make him immortal, of a sort, and which I did not understand then and am, perhaps, beginning to. I burned what was left, the way you do. I kept the head of the Shredder, to show the village it was ended. And I kept this." His wooden hand settled on the Boltcaster, not gripping, just resting. "It has no rounds in it. I have never fired it. I do not carry it to use. I carry it because he carried it, and a thing a person carried every day for a life holds some of the shape of them, after, if you keep it near. It is not a sensible reason. I have stopped requiring my reasons to be sensible."

"And the Shredders," I said. Quietly. I had a sudden, cold understanding of a thing I'd been told in an inn a road-and-a-lifetime ago and not known the weight of. "You hunt them. Still. You've hunted them this whole time. The one outside my town — Tondi's bounty, the head you brought back — that's why you took it. Not the job. Them."

"Them," Third agreed. "Wherever I hear of one. I do not take payment for those, when I can avoid it; it feels like the wrong kind of icing." A pause, the nearest he came to the thing underneath. "It does not bring him back. I am aware. I am very old and I am aware of exactly how little it does. I do it anyway. It is the one piece of grief I have found that has somewhere useful to go, and I have a great deal of grief and very little of it goes anywhere useful at all, so I spend that piece where it spends." The eye came up, found me, found Mathen, found Roan. "That is Dorn. That is the story. Thank you for holding still for it. He would have liked you. He liked almost everyone; it was his great flaw as a judge of character and his great gift as a man."

Mathen reached over, after a moment, and put his hand flat on the Boltcaster beside Third's, just for a breath, the way you'd touch a coffin in passing to say you'd known the one inside.

"A Mender, a Crafter, and a Grask," he said softly, to no one, to the dead man. "Aye. I'll remember it."

We kept the rest of the watch in a better silence than the one before — the fire lower, the cliff still drinking, the book still turning its unseen pages — and I lay down when my half came and did not sleep for a long time, thinking about a man I'd never meet who drew cakes for a monster-killing machine because the machine didn't know what a cake was, and about how Third collected us, the warm restful ones, one after another across all that time, and kept our coats and our jokes and our Bolters when we were gone, and how the keeping was the whole of what he had, and how I had somewhere along this road become one of the ones he was going to have to keep.

I did not think, then, about the joke. About which of us was the Mender, and which the Crafter, and how the punchline always comes from under your own two feet while you're busy arguing about something else. I wish I could say I had. It would have changed nothing, but I would have had the warning, and I have since come to set a high value on warnings, having been given so few that did any good.

*  *  *


# Twenty Eight

He came to me the next watch with the thing already decided in him. I could tell. After long enough beside a person you learn the difference between the silence of a creature keeping a thing back and the silence of one carrying a thing forward to set down in front of you, and this was the second kind, and it frightened me more than the first ever had, because Third did not set things down lightly. He'd been carrying whatever it was since the night the grey pulled at him, turning it, the way he turned everything, until it was the right shape to hand over.

Mathen and Roan were asleep at the far edge of the firelight. Third sat down beside me — sat, which he did not do, he stood his watches — and laid the Boltcaster across his knees, Dorn's Boltcaster, the way he had at the telling, and I understood I was about to be told something on the same shelf as that, or worse, and I put down the strip of dried fish I'd been pretending to eat.

"I have worked out what I am for," he said. "I would rather you heard it from me, sitting down, tonight, than worked it out yourself in the middle of the thing, when it will matter that you already understood. So I am going to tell you all of it. The part you have been letting lie. And the part I have only just been able to see." The coal of his eye was steady. "It will take a while. Do not say anything kind until I have finished. The kind things are the ones that make me stop."

"All right," I said.

"Her name was Liwon," Third said, and the grey-draining cliff and the rearranging field and the whole waiting world went very far away.

*  *  *

He told it plainly, which was the only way he had, and the plainness was the worst of it, because there is no flinching in a flat voice for you to flinch along with — you have to do all the flinching yourself.

He told me about a village his own kind had emptied, and a green-skinned infant on its back in the blood, too small to run, and the voice in his head that told him to finish it, and the thing he did instead, which was pick her up. He told me about the spins of learning to keep a small soft thing alive when he had been built to do the opposite — the food, the rivers, the chopping of whole glens to splinters when the voice got loud and the crying got louder and he could fix neither. He told me how she named herself, near enough, out of the only thing he ever called her; he had said *little one* a thousand times because it was the only true thing he had, and she had handed it back to him broken into *Liwon*, and he had liked the broken one better, and that was the first time, he said, that he had wanted a thing to be true over wanting it to be accurate, and he had her to thank for the whole of what he became after.

He told me about the Virek woman who took them in, and the spins of being a gate-warden to a village that had every reason to fear him and slowly, mostly, didn't; about Liwon growing into the people who were her own kind and away from the wooden thing that had carried her, which he said was right, which he said he had been glad of, and I believed about half of the gladness, which I think was all he believed of it himself.

And then he told me about the night the light flickered in the trees and the red veins came up over the world, and the Virek were simply *gone* — and in their place the two peoples you and I have walked among our whole lives, the pale ones and the green-toothed ones, the Kro and the Ork, made in a single instant out of the people who had been there a breath before. He told me it as a thing that had happened to the whole world — the sundering, the un-making of the first people into the two — and as a thing that had happened to one child on one floor, both at once, because they were the same thing, the great history and the small grief, one event wearing two sizes. He found her, he said. Or he found the two girls she had become, dying, one pale and one green, on the floor and beyond the window. And one of them, with the last of her, made a sound he knew. *Bada.* The thing she'd said when she was frightened, when she was small, when she wanted the wooden thing to come.

"And that," Third said, "is the someone. That is the her. You asked, on a different watch, and I gave you the one word, and you were kind enough not to take the rest. I am giving you the rest now, because you are going to need it. Not to grieve with me. I am not telling you so that you will grieve with me." The eye came round. "I am telling you so that you will know exactly what it is, when the time comes, that you are going to have to refuse to let me have."

I didn't understand yet. I almost did. I sat very still and let him bring it the rest of the way.

*  *  *

"The thing in the grey," he said. "The one that turned, on the watch. That knew me entirely. I told you I did not know what it was, and that was true when I said it. I know now." The Boltcaster sat under his stilled hands. "It is the one that made me.
