I have heard it twice before — once when the last of them fell and it looked at me and *smiled,* before it came apart into the dust that woke me; and once on the floor of that village, over Liwon, when it told me her dying was a seed, that it must go into the ground to become something better, that the loss was a kindness I did not yet have the wisdom to see. The same voice. Patient. Gentle. It has always been gentle with me, Tasha. I took it, all this time, for the carelessness of a vast thing toward a small one. I had it backwards. It was not careless of me. It was *courting* me. It has been courting me since before there were towns."

"Courting you to do what," I said, though the cold coming up my spine had already half-answered it.

"To come home. To stop." The eye dimmed, not to grief — to something more frightening, a kind of longing held at arm's length. "I am a made thing, woken by its own essence. I can walk the grey, which nothing else of this world can do — nothing but the man on the tor, who is its hand, who has spent these spins gathering the pins that hold the world pegged to its place. I run on its blood. I am, Tasha — I have been trying to find a gentler word and there is not one — I am the *door.* The last thing it needs to come back through is a thing that belongs to both sides at once and will *open.* The pins are nearly all pulled. The grey is draining toward the last of them, behind that cliff. And the door is sitting on a fallen slab telling a girl who serves stew that he has finally understood what he is for, and what he is for is to *want to go.*"

The fire ticked. Out in the dark a page of the book turned over with a crack, and for once I'd have welcomed any noise that wasn't his voice.

"It cannot take me," he said. "That is the one mercy in the shape of it. It needs the door opened from the inside. It needs me *willing.* And so it will not seize me — it will *offer.* It will offer me the thing it has been saving the whole of my life to offer, the thing it showed me a corner of on the watch and I nearly walked into the grey to reach." His hand turned over, the old tell, closing and opening on nothing. "The grey keeps the warm part, Tasha. I have told you that. The warm part, before the end. It will offer me *all of them.* Not a visit. Not a corner I climb back out of lessened. It will offer me Liwon — and Dorn, and Adura, and the woman in the snow who told me to go on living — kept, whole, in the warm, forever, on the only condition that I stop holding the door shut. That I come home and be known and never be alone again and have every single one of them back." The coal guttered. "And in time, it will be able to offer me you."

*  *  *

I had thought, when I started down this road out of an inn because I was bored, that the worst thing that could happen to me was dying on it. I want to put on the record that I was a child when I thought that, whatever my age, and that I stopped being one somewhere in the next little while, sitting on a fallen page of stone listening to the kindest creature I have ever known explain to me that he was going to be offered his murdered daughter back and that I was the thing that had to say no.

"You can't refuse it," I said. My voice did something I wasn't proud of. "If it offers you that. Third. Nobody could refuse that. *I* couldn't refuse that, and I've known her for the length of one conversation."

"I know." He said it without any drama at all, which was how I knew it was the truest thing he'd said. "That is the difficulty. The woman in the snow — Lujian — gave me the only answer I have, long ago, for exactly this, though neither of us knew it was for this. She said the loss was *hers.* Liwon's. That it was not a seed and not a kindness and not for anything, that it was a person, and that the only way left to call it what it was, was to refuse to let it be made into a meaning. To go on. That stopping agreed with the liar." The eye came up, and there was something in it I had no name for, something older than the road. "If I take the offer, Liwon's death becomes the seed the Titan always said it was. The thing that softened me, and emptied me, and made me lonely enough to come home. Every death it ever arranged or allowed becomes a step on the path to opening the door. I would be agreeing, at the last, that the liar was right. I will not do that. I have decided. I am telling you on the night I have decided it, while I can still mean it cleanly."

"Then what do you need me for," I said, and I already knew, and I needed him to say it anyway.

"Because deciding it tonight is not the same as holding it then." For the first time the flat voice cracked, one hairline, and set again. "Because there will be a moment — at the cliff, in the thick of it, with the warm part open in front of me and her *in* it — when every part of me that is worth anything will want to go, and will believe, with total conviction, that going is right, that it is even good, that it is the loving thing, and I will turn to you, Tasha, and I will ask you with everything I have to let me go to her." The Boltcaster trembled, faintly, under his hand. "And you must not. That is the whole of it. That is what I am for and that is what *you* are for, the two of us, at the end of this road. I am telling you now, with the clean version of myself, so that you will know to distrust the other one. When I beg you — and I will beg you — you are to believe *this* me. Not that one. The me that is asking you, now, calmly, to refuse the me that will be on his knees. Can you —" "Yes," I said. I didn't let him finish it. I couldn't have stood the shape of the whole question in the air. "Yes. I'll hold the rope. I'll say no. I'll be the worst thing that ever happened to you, right at the end, and I'll do it on purpose, and I'll hate it more than anything I've ever done." I was crying by then, which I'd sworn at the inn I would never do in front of any of them, my father's voice in my head the whole time, *never let them see.* I let him see. "But you have to know what you're asking. You're asking me to look at you wanting her — *her,* Third, your little one — and tell you no. You're asking me to be the reason you don't get her back."

"Yes," Third said.

"That's the cruellest thing anyone has ever asked me."

"Yes," he said again. "I am sorry. There is no one else. You are the loudest thing in any quiet I have ever stood in, and you are the only one who has ever held me to the world hard enough to bring me back across it, and I would not ask it of you if the world did not, regrettably, depend on a girl who served stew being willing to break my heart at precisely the right moment." The eye found mine, steady now, the longing folded back away behind it. "I have great confidence in you. It is the only confidence I have left that is not also a fear."

We sat there a long time, on the fallen page, with the cliff drinking the grey and the dead held warm behind it and the whole impossible weight of what I now knew sitting in my lap like a stone, and I did not let go of his forearm, and he did not ask me to, and neither of us said anything else kind, because the kind things were the ones that made him stop, and I had finally, fully, terribly understood that stopping was the one thing in all the world I was now sworn to never, ever let him do.

That was the night I knew. All of it. The shape and the centre both.

I have not been bored since. I would give a great deal, now, to be bored again.

*  *  *


# Twenty Nine

The last ordinary thing Mathen ever did was complain about the cold.

We had three nights of waiting in us by then, and the third had brought a chill up off the bare rock that even the fire couldn't argue with, and Mathen sat working feeling back into his hands and delivering, to no one, a long and structured grievance against the entire concept of weather that stands still. "A storm that *moves,*" he was saying, "I can respect. A storm that moves has somewhere to be. This—" he gestured at the cold, the dark, the cliff, all of it — "this is just a thing that has decided to sit on us out of spite, and I have decided, in return, to take it personally." Roan was almost smiling. That was the gift of Mathen, even then, even there: he could get a boy who'd been afraid for three nights to almost smile, by being theatrically furious at the temperature. He looked over at me to see if I'd liked the bit. I had. I'd liked all his bits. I gave him the small nod that meant *that one landed,* and he looked pleased, and that is the last face I have of him that is whole.

Because that was when the whispering started, and we all made the same mistake at the same time.

We thought it was the grey.

It came up under the wind, under the fire-crackle — a thin braided murmur with no source, words in no language at the edge of hearing, and every one of us turned the same way: toward the cliff. Of course toward the cliff. We had spent three nights learning that the cliff was where the wrong came from, the grey draining into it, the thing that knew Third, the whole vast danger we had walked across the world to stand in front of. So when the world began to whisper, we faced the danger we knew, all four of us, and we braced for the thing we were expecting.

And the ground came up behind us.

*  *  *

I have tried to set the next part down a dozen ways and they are all too slow. It was not slow. It was the fastest thing I have ever lived through and it is the one I can least bear to make quick.

The rock under the fallen slab heaved and split, and the thing came up out of it the way a fist comes up through paper — long, pale, wet, a body like an enormous worm with hands, actual hands, too many of them, sprouting from it at wrong intervals on long clawed arms, and it came up *between* us, into the middle of our braced and facing-the-wrong-way little circle.

And the whispering, which I had taken for the grey, came down on us all at once and *held* us.

I have to tell this part with care, because the way it is always told — in the joke, in the story, in every fireside Grask anyone ever laughed at — leaves out the thing that mattered, and the leaving-out is the whole reason we were no use. In Dorn's joke a Grask was a punchline. It burrowed and it waited and it ate the clever people while they argued and it said its line. A thing you could laugh at, a little, around a fire. The joke never once mentioned the whispering. The joke never said that when a Grask decides on you it puts a sound inside your skull that unstitches you from your own body — that you stand there with every muscle you own handed over to some other keeping, your mind shrieking the orders your training carved into your spine, and your spine simply does not answer. The joke left that out. I have since worked out why the joke leaves it out, and it is the worst thing I know about stories: the people who learn that part do not come back to put it in.

Mathen was the best blade among us. I want that on the record beside everything else. On any other ground, with half a breath of warning, Mathen would have taken that thing apart and made it look easy and told us about it for a tenday. He had no breath of warning and, in the end, he had no body to do it with, because the whispering had him the way it had all of us — frozen, aware, screaming inside a statue. I know he was screaming inside his, because I was screaming inside mine, and our eyes could still move, and his found mine, and I watched a master swordsman understand, in perfect stillness, that the one thing he was for was not going to be permitted to happen.

All of us but Third.

I don't know why the sound didn't take him. I have wondered since whether there is simply nothing in him for it to unstitch — that whatever a Grask reaches for in a mind, Third's is built of other stuff, the Titan's stuff, wrong for the hook, so that the whisper went through him and found no seam to catch. He was not frozen. He was already moving, the instant the rock broke, the way he was always already moving.

He was one step too far away.

That is the whole of it. That is the sentence I have had to live inside since. One step — the thing came up *between* us and Mathen was on its side of the gap and Third was on ours, and a Grask does not spend the breath a step would cost. Its front split open, four jaws peeling back like the petals of something obscene, and in the wet centre of them a pair of eyes, and they found Mathen, frozen, and the whispering drew itself into one clear sentence, almost gentle, in a voice like wet gravel:

*"Found you."*

Not the line. Even then — even frozen, even with the world ending a step away — my idiot filing mind caught it and would not let it go: *that is not the line.* The joke said *there you are,* light, almost a greeting, the sort of thing you'd say glad to a friend across a room. This said *found you,* flat and final, the way you'd say it of a thing that had always been yours and had only briefly been mislaid. The joke had got even that wrong. The joke had made it almost warm. There was nothing warm in it, and I understood in that frozen breath that every Grask anyone had ever laughed at had said this colder truer thing to someone who never lived to correct the joke, and that we had walked into this one armoured in a story that had lied to us about its teeth.

Then Third reached them, one step too late, and the world started again all at once.

The arms had Mathen — round the chest, dragging him toward the jaws and beginning to pull him *down,* toward the broken ground, toward the burrow — and the freezing broke as the thing's attention bent wholly to its prize, and Roan got Mathen's name out in a tearing shout, and I got my own body back and it was no use to anyone, and Mathen — with a Grask's claws in him and the dark coming up to take him — *laughed.* A short, wet, astonished laugh, the laugh of a man who has just watched a joke he told come true in the worst possible way, and he got it out, around the blood, looking not at the thing that was killing him but at us, at me—

"—*told you* it was a good one—"

And then Third was on it.

*  *  *

He did not fight it. Fighting is what you do to a thing that might win. Third arrived at the Grask the way the Scar arrives at the day — completely, without hurry, without doubt — and the Falkas came out and the wrong-jointed arms came off, one, two, the hands still clutching as they fell, and the thing made a sound I will not try to spell, and tried to do the one clever thing it knew, tried to drop back into its hole and take what it had, and Third put himself between it and the ground. He would not let it burrow. He would not let it take Mathen down into the dark where even he could not follow. He cut it off from the earth and then he cut it apart, methodically, the way he cut back the flora at the geysers — the same way he did every necessary terrible thing — until the four jaws stopped working and the eyes in the wet of them went flat and the long pale body slumped down half in and half out of its own ruined hole, and the whispering stopped, and the only sound on that whole rearranging plain was Roan saying *no, no, no,* very fast and very quietly, over Mathen.

Third turned from the dead thing to the dying man and I watched something happen in him that I have no good word for. He knelt. He, who knelt to nothing. And he gathered Mathen up out of the bloodied moss the way I had seen him, in my mind's eye only, gather up a green-skinned infant from another field of the wrongly dead, and he held him, and the rest of us came down around them in the cold.

"Mathen," Third said.

"Don't," Mathen got out. He was going. We all knew the look by now; the road had taught us that look. "Don't do the — flat thing. The stating-facts thing. Not for this." A breath that didn't go all the way in. "Make it a *story.* Promise me you'll — you'll make it a story, not a — not a list of what the thing did to me. Tell it so they laugh at the start." His hand found Third's wooden forearm, the one that held him. "A Mender, a Crafter, and a *Grask.* Eh? You've got the — you've got the ending now. Free of charge."

"I have it," Third said. The avalanche of his voice had gone to almost nothing. "I will tell it well. I will learn to tell it well, the way you would have."

"Spread the weight," Mathen said. It was nearly the last of it. His eyes went, with effort, from Third to me, and stopped on me, and there was something in them he was trying to hand over, the way you hand over a watch. "She knows how. Taught her m'self." The ghost of the grin. "Stronger in the — back. Told you." And then, to Third, fading, the thing I did not understand was a gift until much later, until the cliff, until everything: "Don't you stop, you old — you keep *going.* Whatever's coming. You keep — you go *on.* That's the whole — that's the whole trick of it. You just—"

He didn't finish the sentence. I have spent a long time deciding how it ended, and I have settled, for my own sake, on *you just go on,* because it was the thing he'd have said, and because it was, though he never knew it, the exact armour a dead woman in the snow had once handed Third for exactly this, given again now by a dying man who only meant be brave, be kind, don't let the road harden you — and who could not have known he was loading the one weapon his friend was going to need against the offer of getting him, and all the rest of them, back.

*  *  *

We burned him at the rim, on a fallen page of the great book, the way Mathen had once told me he'd want if it came to it — "somewhere with a view, and don't any of you dare be solemn about it, I'll know." We tried not to be solemn. We were terrible at it. Roan said three words and couldn't do the rest. I did a little better and hated every steady syllable. Third spoke last, and he did not state facts; he told a small story, badly, about a man who cooked well and complained beautifully and could make a frightened boy almost smile on the worst night of his life, and it was the first story Third ever told without being asked, and Mathen would have given him notes, and that is the kindest thing I can think to say about it.

And when it was done, and the others had gone to the fire, I stood a moment longer by the embers with Third, and I felt the new thing settle on me, the thing I had not had room to feel until the burning was over — that Mathen was in the grey now. That the warm part had him. That somewhere behind that drinking cliff the thing that wanted Third to come home had just been handed one more reason, one more face, one more voice that could be offered back, *whole, in the warm, forever, if you only stop holding the door.* The world had not taken Mathen from Third. It had taken Mathen and *given him to the enemy,* to hold up at the end like bait.

I did not say it. Third knew it. I could see he knew it, the coal of his eye on the embers, doing the arithmetic of his own grief and finding, at the bottom of it, the trap.

"Three of us now," he said at last.

"Three of us now," I said. And I took hold of his forearm, where Mathen's hand had been, and I did not let go, because I had a job now, the cruellest job in the world, and it had just gotten one face harder to do.

&nbsp;

<p align="center">◆ &nbsp; ◆ &nbsp; ◆</p>



# Thirty

He should not have gone in. He knew it the way he knew the weather of a blade coming — knew it before his hoof left the lived ground — and he went in anyway, because the alternative was to stand at the embers of his friend and not know whether the friend was anywhere at all, and that was a thing his three hundred thousand spins had not taught him how to bear.

Tasha had her hand on his forearm. He had told her he meant only to look, which was the first lie he had told her since the carts, and he suspected she knew it was one, because her grip was harder than looking warranted and her voice had already started — low, steady, the rope paying out before he'd even stepped — *I'm here, I've got you, you come back when I call, you hear me, you old fool, you come back.* He held the sound of her the way you hold a guide-rope in fog. Then he stepped beside the world, and the lived sound of her thinned to a thread, and the grey took him.

It was wrong, the way it had been wrong on the watch — churned, currented, the whole patient nothing sliding one way, toward the cliff, toward the drain behind it — but he had expected the wrongness and braced against it, leaning his will upcurrent like a man wading. What he had not braced against, what no bracing would have served against, was that the current was warm.

It had never been warm before. The grey was a cold neutral nothing; that was its whole nature. But the flow toward the cliff was warm, and the warmth had a grain to it he knew, and he understood with a lurch that the warmth was *company* — that the thing draining toward the cliff was not empty grey but grey full of the held warm parts of everything that had ever been lost here, all of it flowing home, and that if he let go his will and stopped wading he would flow with it, into the warm, toward the gladness he could feel waiting patient at the bottom of the pull, the thing that knew him, that had always known him, that was so very pleased he had finally come this far in.

And in the warm, a few steps downcurrent — close, so close — was Mathen.

*  *  *

He was sitting the way he sat at a fire, one knee up, easy, and he was *talking,* of course he was talking, telling some story to no one with his hands going, and when he saw Third his whole face did the thing it did, the lighting-up, the pleasure at company, undimmed.

"There he is," Mathen said — warm, glad, and the words went through Third like a blade because they were nearly the other words, the wet-gravel words, *there you are,* and here they were turned back into kindness in the mouth of the man the other mouth had killed. "I wondered if you'd come. I hoped you wouldn't, and I hoped you would, which is the usual way of hoping, I find."

"You are here," Third said. The avalanche of his voice came strange in the grey, muffled, near.

"I'm somewhere. I'm here when you're here. I don't ask it too many questions." Mathen patted the nothing beside him, an invitation to sit, the way he'd patted a thousand fire-logs. "Sit a while. You look terrible, which I'll grant is your natural state, but more so. Sit. Tell me a story. You owe me one — you never did pay back the Dorn telling, and now look, I've gone and given you a second one, the whole dramatic exit, you could dine out on me for a tenday." The grin. "Make it a good one. Laugh at the start."

And Third wanted to sit. That was the thing he would carry out of the grey if he carried nothing else — the size of how much he wanted to sit down beside Mathen in the warm and tell him a story and have it be, for as long as forever lasted, exactly this easy. The grief he had carried up out of the lived world did not lessen in the warm; it was held there, but held the way a note is held, sustained, sweet, unbearable, and the only thing that promised to end the unbearable sweetness was to give in to it, to sit, to stay, to stop wading and let the warm current carry the two of them down to where all the others were, because they were there, he could feel them now, the whole downcurrent abundance of them — a green child, a swordsman with a Boltcaster, a woman who had been unwell, a woman from the snow — all of them, *kept,* all of them a few steps further into the warm, and the gladness at the bottom of it saying, without words, in the voice that had smiled at him as it fell: *yes. all of them. you have only to come the rest of the way. you have been alone so long. let me know you. let me give them back.*

*  *  *

He had been warned. He had warned himself, sitting on a fallen slab with Tasha, naming the trap aloud so that he would recognise it when it closed. He had thought, naming it, that knowing the shape of a trap was most of escaping it.

He had been wrong. Knowing the shape of it did nothing. The shape of it was *Mathen, glad to see him, asking for a story,* and there is no knowledge that armours a creature against being wanted by someone it loves.

So he reached, drowning, for the only thing that was not in the warm.

"Mathen," he said. "Are you Mathen?"

The grin flickered — not gone, but troubled, the way the living Mathen's face had gone troubled when a thing mattered. "Now that," he said slowly, "is a question I can't answer, and the fact that I can't is probably your answer, isn't it. Clever. You were always cleverer than you let the room think." He looked downcurrent, toward the warm gladness, then back, and something old and sad and entirely *himself* came up in him. "I'll tell you what I know. I know I feel like me. I know I'd tell you to stay, if staying were the kind thing — I never could stand to see you carry what you carry. And I know that *because* I'd want you to stay, you can't trust me to tell you to. If I'm really me, I'm compromised by loving you. And if I'm not really me—" the grin came back, rueful, terrible "— then I'm the prettiest hook that's ever been baited, and you should be halfway gone already." He spread his hands. "Either way, old friend, you can't take the word of anyone in here. That's the cruelty they built into the place. You'll never again be able to tell your dead from the thing wearing them. I'm sorry. I'd undo it for you if I could. Which is, of course, exactly what the hook would say."

And that — not the warning, not the will, not the wading — that was the thing that turned him. Because it was so precisely the move the living Mathen would have made, to look the trap in the face and name its teeth even at the cost of his own comfort, that Third could not tell if it proved the man real or proved the bait perfect, and in the not-being-able-to-tell he understood, finally and in his bones, what Tasha had understood for him with only a road's worth of watching: that the warm part was lost to him now. Not Mathen. The whole of it. Every grave he had ever visited in the grey, every warm note he had gone there to sustain — poisoned, every one, because he would never again know whether he was grieving or being fished.

The grey had been the one place his dead were safe. The Titan had made it the one place they were bait. It had taken from him the only comfort his unkillable life had ever found, and it had done it gently, and there had been, as ever, no cruelty in the doing — only the warm, and the gladness, and the patient certainty that he would, in the end, come home.

He would not give it that. He had decided this on a slab in the lived world and he decided it again now in the warm, with Mathen's face on it, which was harder by an order he had not imagined.

He turned upcurrent, into the wading, away from the warm and the abundance and the green child a few steps downcurrent that he did not let himself look at, that he would not look at, that he would spend the rest of whatever he had not-looking at, and he reached for the thread.

It was still there. Thin, lived, loud. *—come back, you hear me, you don't get to do this, you don't get to leave me here doing the watch alone, there's a whole stupid world out here with a sign that says Only and a boy who needs you and me, Third, me, I'm still telling you about the sign, come back and let me finish—* He took hold of Tasha's voice with everything that was left of his will, and he waded, and the warm pulled, and Mathen — or the thing that was kind enough or cruel enough to wear him — called after, the last of it, the living man's exact cadence:

"*Go on,*" Mathen said. "Whatever's coming. You go on. And Third—" almost lost now, under the rope, under the wading "—tell it well. Laugh at the start."

Third stepped back into the world.

*  *  *

He came back wrong, and he knew he came back wrong, because the first thing he was aware of was Tasha's face very close to his and both her hands fisted in the vines of his arm and tears on her and her voice gone hoarse from how long she had been throwing it after him into a place she could not see.

"I had you," she was saying. "I had you, I had you the whole time."

"You did," he managed. The lived world was very loud and very cold and very full of unfinished things, and he had never in three hundred thousand spins been so grateful for any of them. "You did. I heard the sign." The bellows dragged, ragged, the one he allowed himself. "Tasha. It was worse than I told you it would be. I told you it would be hard. I did not know — I could not have known until I was in it — that knowing would not help. That I would want it *with the knowing.* That the wanting does not care what I understand." The coal of his eye found hers, and there was naked fear in it, the certainty gone, the old sureness nowhere. "When it comes — the real offering, at the cliff, with all of them and not just the one — I am not certain my will is the thing that decides it. I think it may be yours. I think the rope may be the whole of what stands between that thing and the door. I am sorry. That is a terrible thing to hang on a person. I would carry it myself if there were any way at all to carry it myself."

"I know," Tasha said. She didn't let go of his arm. "I've got the rope. I'm not putting it down." A breath. "What did it offer."

He looked, for a moment, the way she had learned meant he was choosing the true thing over the easy one.

"Him," Third said. "And all of them behind him. Kept. Warm. Forever." The ember sank to almost nothing. "And the worst of it, Tasha — the worst of it is that I could not tell, at the end, whether the Mathen who told me to go on was Mathen being kind, or the thing being clever. I will never be able to tell. It has taken even that. I went in to grieve my friend and I came out unable to be sure I have ever grieved anyone, or only ever been fished by something patient wearing the faces of my own dead."

The fire had burned to coals. Out past it, unwatched, the great book turned a page. And the three of them sat in the cold lived world that was loud and unfinished and *true,* and none of them slept, and Third did not go into the grey again, though I think — I came to think, after — that the notgoing was its own slow kind of starving, and that he did it for us, and that it was one more thing he never once asked us to thank him for.

&nbsp;

<p align="center">◆ &nbsp; ◆ &nbsp; ◆</p>



# Thirty One

Grief in a party of three is a different animal from grief alone. Alone, you can let it have all of you; there's no one watching to be frightened by how much. With two others, you have to keep enough of yourself back to be a person they can stand near, and the keeping-back is its own exhaustion, on top of the grief, which does not take kindly to being kept back and finds other ways out.

We were very bad at it, the day after. We were bad at it because the person who would have been good at it, who would have known exactly the right thing to say and the right moment to say nothing and the right way to put a bowl down by your hand so that you ate without deciding to — that person was ash now, and his absence was loudest precisely in the places he used to fill. The silences he'd have broken sat there unbroken. The food I made was just food, fuel, grey in the mouth, and I understood for the first time that Mathen cooking well had never really been about the cooking; it had been about giving us a thing to look forward to three times a day on a road that otherwise offered nothing to look forward to at all. He had been feeding us hope in the shape of stew. I couldn't do it. I didn't have the recipe.

Roan came apart quietly, which is the way the strong ones do it. He did his watches and ate what was put in front of him and answered when spoken to, and somewhere behind his eyes a door had shut, and I watched him standing at the rim of the field that second evening looking out at nothing with the specific stillness of a young man doing arithmetic he doesn't want the answer to. I knew the arithmetic, because I was doing it too, and it wasn't hard to write out his version. *We're three. Mathen was worth any two of us in a fight and the thing took him in a breath, before he could lift the blade he was the best with. We cannot win this. Whatever Tasha and the Forged know that I don't, it did not save Mathen, and it will not save me.* And then the line his sums had that mine didn't: *I am young — too few spins behind me to have learned how to spend the rest of them well — and I do not want to be a pile of ash on a rock at the edge of the world.* So I went and stood beside him, and I gave him the thing nobody had given me, because I'd had no one to give it: the door, held open.

*  *  *

"You can go, Roan."

He didn't look at me. "Don't."

"I mean it. I'm not — this isn't a test, it's not me checking are-you-brave. You've nothing left to prove to me; I watched you stand still on that field with a Grinding Teeth cloud an arm's length off your face, I know what you've got." I kept my voice level, which took some doing. "But this is going to get worse, and it's not your fight. You came out because a Forged needed a head brought back and it turned into something nobody could have signed up for. Mathen would skin me if I let you die here without telling you, out loud, that you are allowed to walk away. You go home. You guard a gate. You get old. Nobody — not me, not Third, not him, wherever he is — nobody thinks one thing less of you. That's true and I need you to have it."

He was quiet a long while. The Scar's dark edge was coming up over the field. Out in it a far slab went over with a crash, and neither of us flinched, which tells you something about what a tenday at the Graveyard does to a person.

"Is it worse than you're saying," he asked. "What's coming."

"Yes."

"Can you tell me how?"

I thought about it. About what Third had laid on me on the slab — the door, the offer, the dead held warm, the cruellest job in the world. It was not mine to hand to Roan, and it was too heavy to hand to anyone you were also telling to feel free to leave. "No," I said. "Not all of it. Some of it's Third's to tell or not. But the short of it is: there's a thing coming that wants something only Third can give it, and it's going to offer him the world to get it, and my job — the reason I can't take the door I'm offering you — is to make sure he doesn't give it. That's the whole of what I am here to do now. Stand between Third and the thing he's going to want more than anything. So no. I don't get to leave. But you do."

Roan turned and looked at me then, and the door behind his eyes was open again, just a crack, the arithmetic abandoned.

"Mathen didn't leave," he said. "He could've, loads of times. He stayed because — " Roan's jaw worked. "He stayed because of you two. Because you don't leave the people. He told me that once, not in those words, he never said anything in those words, but that's what it was. You tend the people. That's the — that's the whole job, he said. Everything else is just where you happen to be standing while you do it." He wiped his face with the heel of his hand, rough, done with the coming-apart. "I'm not going. Not because I'm brave. I worked out on that field that I'm brave enough, which turns out to be a smaller and worse thing than I thought it'd be. I'm staying because you two are the people, now, and I'm not going to be the one who leaves them. That's it. That's the whole of my reason and it's not a grand one and I don't care."

And there it was — Mathen, sitting up in him, wearing his face, saying his things in not-his-words. I'd buried a man yesterday and here he was today, transplanted whole into a frightened brave boy who hadn't even noticed it happen. I didn't tell Roan. You don't tell someone they've started carrying your dead; they find it heavy enough without knowing what it is.

"Okay," I said. "Then you're staying. And Roan—" I made myself say it, because Mathen would have, because somebody had to be the one who said the warm thing now and the job had apparently fallen to me. "He'd be unbearable about this. He'd have a whole speech. He'd make you cry and then make you laugh and you'd hate him for it."

Roan laughed, once, wet and surprised, exactly the way Mathen had laughed at the end, and I had to look away at the field for a moment.

"Yeah," he said. "He would've."

*  *  *

Third I found where he always was now, at the very lip of the rim, as close to the cliff as he let himself get, watching the thing he would not walk into anymore.

He had not gone into the grey since that one time after Mathen. I knew what it was costing him — that the grey had been the one place he could set the weight down, visit the warm parts, and now he wouldn't, both because it would take him and because he could no longer trust what he found there to be his dead and not their wearer. He was carrying all of it awake now, all the time, with no relief, the way the rest of us carry our griefs, except he had three hundred thousand spins of them stacked up and no end coming to set them down at. I had started to understand that this — the not-going, the carrying-awake — was its own slow dying, and that he'd chosen it for us, and that the choosing was using him up.

I could see the using-up. When I came alongside him the seam down his chest caught the last of the light, and through it, faint, I could see the two small lights he kept in there, and the dim one was dimmer. Not gone. But lower than the geysers, lower than yesterday, a wick guttering toward a wick-end.

"It's lower," I said. "The light."

"Yes." He didn't pretend not to know which I meant. "The grey pulls harder the longer I stand near it, and standing near it is the whole of my purpose, so. It draws on me. I had perhaps twenty spins in the dim one when we reached the geysers. I have rather fewer now." The coal of his eye stayed on the cliff. "It does not matter, Tasha. Truly. Whatever happens at that cliff happens long before the light does. I mention it only because you asked, and because you have decided to know the true things now, and that is one of them. I am running down. We are all of us running down. Mine is merely visible."

"Don't," I said, and didn't have the rest of the sentence, so I just put my hand on his forearm, the place that had become mine, where Mathen's hand had been, where I would put it again when it mattered most.

"He is close," Third said. "The man. The hand of the thing. I cannot look into the grey to count the days, but I do not need to. It pours toward the cliff faster every watch. The field is changing more — you'll have noticed, the cracks come closer together now. Everything is being drawn in. He is coming to the last peg, and we are sitting on it, and it will be soon. A day. Perhaps two." The eye came round to me, and the fear was in it, and under the fear, the thing I had come to trust more than I trusted his strength: the decision, held. "You remember what you promised me."

"I remember."

"You will not believe the version of me that begs."

"I'll believe this one," I said. "The one telling me not to. I've got it written down in here—" I tapped my own skull "—where the begging can't get at it. I'm ready, Third. I've been ready since the slab. Being ready is most of what I do now."

"Yes," he said. "You are very good at it. Better than I am, and I have had longer to practise." Which was the closest Third came to a compliment, and I took it, and we stood the last of the light together looking at the cliff that was drinking the world.

*  *  *

That night the three of us sat the watch all together, no pairs, because what was the point of pairs now, and because none of us wanted to be the one not in the firelight when it came. Roan sharpened things that didn't need sharpening. I told them, badly, the story of the inn and the burglar Third scared off by warning it which way not to break in, and Third corrected three of my details and Roan almost smiled and it was nothing at all like Mathen doing it and it was the best we had and it was, I decided, enough. You do the thing the dead taught you, badly, in your own clumsy hands, because the alternative is to let the thing die with them, and we had buried enough.

Out past the fire the great book turned its pages faster than it used to. The grey poured toward the cliff, carrying its warm and terrible cargo, and somewhere out in the dark a man with six cold lights on his back walked the last of his straight line toward us, toward the last pin in the world and the door that was the only thing left that could open it.

We were three. We were tired. One of us was running down where you could see it and all of us were running down where you couldn't. We had a plan thinner than river ice and a job crueller than any of us had agreed to when we set out, and not one of us was leaving.

I had followed a monster out of an inn because I was bored, and I had found, at the cold edge of the world, the only thing I have ever been certain was worth the whole terrible trip: three of us at a fire, with the end coming, choosing to stay.

Let it come. We were ready. We were as ready as the unready ever get.

*  *  *


# Thirty Two

We knew it was the day because the field stopped pretending.

For a tenday the open book had played its patient game — moved only when we looked away, settled its great canting pages a little nearer or a little further each time our backs were turned, so that you could almost believe you'd misremembered, almost believe stone behaved. That morning it stopped bothering. We woke to a field rearranged in plain sight, not while we slept but while we watched: a slab the size of a barn sliding its base across bare rock with a sound like a slow tooth, grinding itself a hand's breadth spireward and stopping, as if it had remembered it no longer needed to be shy. The grey was running so hard toward the cliff by then that even I could feel it, and I have no gift for the grey at all — a tug, low in the chest, the exact place you'd press if you were trying not to be sick, except the pull was outward, toward the pale wall, as though the morning itself were draining into it.

Third stood at the rim with his back to our dead fire and looked at the cliff the way you look at a debt you have always known you would have to pay.

"He is on the field," he said. "He came in the dark, while we kept the watch facing the wrong way again." A dry sound, not quite the bellows. "We are very bad at facing the right way. It is, I think, the most humin thing about all of us." The eye came round. "It is today. Do you still mean to come, or have you the sense the night might have given you?"

"Don't," I said. "You know I'm coming. Roan?"

"Coming," Roan said, and checked the lashings on his spear for the fourth time that hour, which was how Roan said *I am terrified and I am coming anyway,* and which I had learned not to comment on.

"Then we go to it," Third said, "while we can still choose the ground. He will make for the spine. The spine is the last of it — the last pin, the place the whole shape is hung from. If he reaches it unopposed he has everything he needs and we have a long walk home with nothing to show for it but our lives, which he will not let us keep regardless." He took up his glaive, the Boltcaster, and his Falkas, and he did not put on the cloak. He had no more use for looking like a man.

*  *  *

I have crossed bad country. I grew up a walk from a black swamp; I had been over the Gnawing Fields with their floating teeth and their pale plinths; I had stood a tenday at the rim of this place watching it crawl. None of it taught me what it was to walk *into* the open book, out among the pages, with the rule of the place — *do not look away* — turned from a curiosity into the only law that mattered.

Because you cannot watch everything. That is the whole cruelty of it. You have a front and the field has no manners about which direction it uses. We crossed in a tight knot, the three of us, each facing a third of the world, turning our heads in a slow shared sweep we fell into without discussing, like oarsmen finding a stroke — and still, every time the sweep came round, something was *different.* A slab that had leaned left now leaned right. A gap we'd meant to walk through had closed to a seam. A flat page we'd marked as our path had tilted in the breath our eyes were elsewhere and become a wall, and we had to find the path again from nothing, in a place that rearranged its own evidence the instant we trusted it.

Roan said, once, very low, "It's like being watched by something that stops the moment you turn." And none of us answered, because he was exactly right and there was nothing to add to it except more fear.

The pages got bigger as we went in. At the rim they had been barn-sized; here they were the size of town walls, of hillsides, vast flat sheets of pale stone canted at angles that hurt to stand under, each one a leaf of a book the size of a country laid open and reading itself in the dark. And between them, in the low places, the grey had come up out of the ground. I had never seen it from the lived side — Third's country, not mine — but there it was, pooled in the troughs between the great stones like ground-fog that had forgotten to be white, a faint moving greyness with the wrongest light in it, all of it sliding, all of it pouring, the same direction we were walking. Toward the wall.

*  *  *

The wall took its toll on Third by lengths.

I'd promised to watch him and I watched him, and what I watched was a slow leaning. Not of his body — his body crossed that field like it crossed everything, sure-hooved and exact — but of *him,* the him inside the make of him, tilting toward the cliff the way the slabs tilted, the way the grey poured. By the time the pages were hillside-sized he was walking at a slight cant against nothing, leaning upcurrent into a wind only he could feel, and the seam down his chest showed both lights working hard, the dim one guttering with the effort, and his answers had gone short.

"Third."

"I am here."

"Say the thing. The whole thing. Out loud, for me."

He knew what I was asking. We had made it a drill on the slab two nights back — that if the pull got into him I was to make him *talk,* because the part of him that talked to me was the part that was still walking to the cliff to stop a thing, and not the part that wanted to lie down in the warm and be known.

"I am Third," he said, and the avalanche of it steadied as he said it, the way a man's hands steady on a rope he has remembered he is holding. "I am going to the spine to refuse him. I will not be made willing. You will not believe the version of me that begs. There is a girl named Tasha walking on my left who is going to be the whole of my will if my own gives out, and a guardsman on my right who has chosen the people over the sense, and they are mine, and they are *here,* and that is more true than anything that wall is going to offer me." A pause. The cant of him eased, a degree. "Thank you. Ask me again when I go quiet."

"I will. I'll ask you the whole way."

"I know you will. It is why I let you come."

*  *  *

We saw him when the field gave us a long straight aisle between two leaning pages — a corridor of bare rock running spireward, and at the far end of it, small with distance, a figure walking.

He walked the way the grey-faced traveller had described back at the inn, and worse for the seeing of it: dead straight, unhurried, putting one foot exactly in front of the other along a line only he could see, as though the field's crawling did not apply to him, as though the slabs declined to rearrange in front of *that.* And riding his back, even at that distance, even in the day, six small cold lights — bottled lightning gone to dying embers in their jars — six of them where Third had two, and one of Third's already failing. He did not look at us. He had not, I understood, looked at anything in a very long time that was not the thing he was walking toward. He went building to building through a dead town and never turned his head; he would cross a field that ate the unwary and never break his line; and he was going to reach the spine at the same time we did, from the other arm of the open book, the two of us converging on the hinge of the world like the covers of the book closing.

"There," Third said, unnecessarily. The eye had gone very bright and very steady. "The hand of the thing under everything."

And then he went still — stiller than the moment asked for, a stillness with a different grain to it, the same stillness that had come over him in the inn the day the grey-faced traveller spoke, as though the shape of that far walker had reached across all the bare rock between us and pressed on something in him he had no intention of letting me see. Whatever it was, he didn't say it. His head stayed level on the distant figure, and his answer, when I asked with a look, was only the bright steady eye and nothing behind it I was allowed to read.

I filed it. Of course I filed it; it is the curse Mathen used to tease me for, and I had no Mathen now to tease me, so I filed it alone and said nothing, and we walked the last of the aisle toward the place the whole shape was hung from, where the grey stood up out of the ground in a pale drowning wall against the pale dead wall of stone, and where a thing was waiting that had a place in it shaped exactly like Third, and a girl from an inn was going to have to be a rope.

The spine rose in front of us, and resolved, the way the cliff always resolved on approach — gradually, grudgingly, a little more true with every step until you could not unsee how big it was. And at its foot, in the last of the bare rock before the stone and the grey, there was room enough to stand and say the things that had to be said before the other thing arrived to say his.

"Here," Third said. "We say what we must here. Quickly. He is close, and once he is closer there will be no more saying, only doing."

I looked at Roan, who did not know yet all of what he had walked into.

It was, I realised, my turn to hold a door open for someone — and this time, the cruel kind, the kind that only opens inward.

*  *  *


# Thirty Three

There is a particular cruelty in knowing exactly how a thing is going to land before you do it, because Third had done it to me, on a slab two nights gone, and I had felt the floor of the world go out from under me one fact at a time, and now I was going to do the same to Roan with the cold man a thousand paces off and closing, and there was no kind way and no slow way and no time for either.

So I did it the way Tondi would have set a broken arm. Fast, and straight, and without asking him to hold still first, because asking only makes them tense.

"Roan. Listen to me and don't say anything till I'm done, because we don't have the breath. You know some of this. Here's the rest."

He looked at me. The spear had stopped its endless re-checking. That was how I knew he'd already understood that the talking part was over and some other part had started.

"The thing under the world wants back in. It can't come on its own — it's too big, it's in pieces, half of it's in the grey. It needs a door. Third is the door. Not because he's willing — because of what he's *made* of, what woke him, the way he walks in both places at once. It can't force the door; that's the one mercy in the whole rotten business. It can only get him to *open* it. From the inside. On purpose." I kept my voice flat, the way you keep a hand flat for a frightened animal. "So it's going to offer him a price. And the price is everyone he's ever lost. All of them. Whole, and warm, and back, forever — Liwon, the swordsman, the woman who was sick, the woman in the snow. Mathen."

That one I watched land. I'm sorry to say I had to. I needed to know he'd heard it.

"Mathen's in there now, Roan. In the warm. The thing has him. And it will hold him up at the end like the last coin in a purse and tell Third *all he has to do is open the door and he gets them all back, Mathen included,* and Third is going to want to. Not might. *Going to.* I've seen what it does to him just standing near it; I watched it nearly take him with my own voice the only thing holding the other end. He is going to stand at that wall and want, with everything three hundred thousand spins has piled up in him, to say yes. And if he says yes, the thing comes through, and there is no more world. Not our lives — the *world.* Everyone's. Everywhere. That's the size of it. That's what we walked out here to stand in front of."

I made myself stop. I let him have the breath I'd told him to save.

*  *  *

He did not bolt. I will love him for that as long as I have left to love anything. The size of it went through him — I watched it go through him, the way a swallow of something too strong goes through you, all the way down, and his face did several things and settled, at the end, on the one I'd hoped for and not dared expect: not the slack of a man who's quit, but the stillness of a man who's decided to be useful with whatever he's got left.

"What do I do," he said. Not *can we win.* Not *are you sure.* What do I do. Mathen, I thought. Mathen all the way down.

"You keep me on him." I turned him by the shoulder so he was facing Third, who stood a little apart, giving us this, watching the cold man come. "That's the whole of your job and it's the worst one and I'm sorry it's yours. Whatever happens, I am the rope. As long as my voice is on Third and my hand is on Third, there's a chance. The thing knows that now. So it's going to try to take me off him. Maybe through the cold man. Maybe—" I made myself say it "—maybe through Third. If it turns him, if it gets him moving, he is stronger than the three of us stacked up and he will come through me to get to that wall and he will not know he's doing it."

"And I stop him." Flat. Already accepting the impossible arithmetic of it.

"You don't *stop* him. You can't. None of us can. You're not meant to." Third's words, from the drill; I gave them to Roan now because they were the truest thing I had. "You're meant to cost it time. You put your body between me and whatever's coming and you make it spend seconds getting through you. Ten breaths. That's the job. Buy me ten breaths to finish refusing, and you'll have done more than any hero in any of Mathen's stories, and most of them only had to die for a *town.*"

He took that in. Then, the boy in him surfacing one last time before whatever he was about to become: "Will it hurt him? If I have to. Will I hurt Third."

And there it was — the thing that told me Mathen had built better than he knew. Roan, told he might have to throw his body in front of a three-hundred-thousand-spin war-construct to buy a tavern girl ten breaths to save the world, and the question he stopped on was whether *the construct* would be hurt by it.

"No," I said, which was a lie, and a small one, and the kind Mathen would have approved of. "You won't be able to hurt him. Just slow him. That's all."

*  *  *

Third came to us then, because the cold man was close enough now that there was no more *here* to be said in, only *now.* He looked at Roan for a long moment with the bright unblinking coal of his eye, and Roan looked back and did not flinch, which still, after everything, was rare.

"I brought you to this," Third said. "You were going to guard a gate and grow old. I am sorry. It is the truest apology I have, and it is not enough, and I am giving it to you anyway because you should have it before, and not after, in case there is no after for the giving."

"You didn't bring me," Roan said. "I came. There's a difference. Mathen taught me that one too."

Something moved in the make of Third — not the bellows, nothing so loud, just a settling, the sound a house makes accepting a weight. "Yes," he said. "There is a difference. Thank you for the difference."

And then he turned to me, and his whole attention came round and landed, and I knew from the quality of it that whatever the three of us had just been was finished, and that the next thing he had to say he had to say to me alone, and that I was not going to like it, and that I was going to have to carry it anyway, because that, in the end, was the difference I had chosen too.

*  *  *


# Thirty Four

The first thing he did was set down the glaive.

He did it carefully, the way you set down something that has a history of being mishandled. He leaned it against the foot of the spine, upright, the wide pale blade he had carried since the day he was made — since before he was anyone, when he was only the thing the Titan had wrapped a purpose around and called finished — and he stepped back from it and left it standing there against the pale stone like a tool returned to a shed. He kept the Falkas. The two curved blades he'd spent thirty spins of the Scar learning to dance with, the weapons that were his choosing and not his making, those he checked at his hips with the small unconscious touch of a man making sure of his hands.

I understood it without being told, which is a thing that happens between people who have walked far enough together. He was not going to meet the end of the world as the instrument someone had built. He was going to meet it as the thing that instrument had decided, against every line of its making, to become. The glaive could stand there and watch. It had never been his anyway.

"Tasha," he said. "The last of it. The part I have not told you."

"I know. I saw it on your face. Get it said; he's nearly here."

He was. Down the converging aisle the cold man had grown from a far mark to a near one, close enough now that I could see the lights riding his back had a faint and constant tremor, like something breathing. We had a hundred breaths, perhaps. Perhaps fewer.

*  *  *

"You are the rope," Third said. "That is true and it remains true and it is the first thing. As long as you hold me, I have a chance to hold myself. But you are going to need to know the second thing, and the second thing is worse, and I have not let myself say it until now because saying it makes it real and I wanted, selfishly, a few more spins of it not being real." The eye was very steady on me. "If the rope fails — if it gets so far into me that I begin to open, and you cannot pull me back — then holding me is no longer the job. Ending me is the job."

I had known, somewhere, that this was coming. Knowing did not help. It never does; he'd taught me that himself, coming back out of the grey with Mathen's face still on him.

"No," I said.

"Tasha—"

"I heard you. I said no. Tell me the rest of it anyway, because I'm going to need it, but you should know my answer first so you understand what it costs me to change it."

Something in the make of him that was not quite the bellows let go, a small mechanical sigh of a thing that had been braced for a fight and been handed honesty instead. "Thank you," he said. "That is exactly why it is you."

And then he reached around to his back, with that wrong boneless ease that still put a flutter in my chest after all this time, and he brought out the Boltcaster.

Dorn's Boltcaster. I knew it the moment I saw it because he'd shown it to me once, early, at a fire, and told me whose it had been and how he had died — a tendril through the chest from below, before Third had finished standing up — and told me he had never once loaded it, having carried it a very long time and never fired it, because it was not a weapon to him. It was a person. It was the last weight of a man he had failed to save by the width of a single breath.

He held it a moment longer before he gave it over — and then, with the same wrong boneless economy, he brought a single magefire shot up from somewhere on him, a little vial of caged lightning, and he loaded it. I watched him do it and felt the floor go out from under me a second time, because at the fire, the night he told us about Dorn, he had said it plain to the whole table: *I do not carry it to use.* He had carried it unloaded across more road than I could imagine, kept it the way you keep a grave tidy, and now, here, he was thumbing a shot into it with his own two-fingered hands.

"I have never loaded it," he said, as if he'd heard the thought. "Not once. Loading it would have made it a weapon again, and it stopped being a weapon the day it became Dorn. I am loading it now, because this is the one thing left in the world he would forgive me for aiming it at." Then he put it in my hands. It was heavier than it looked, and warm where his grip had been.

"There is a seam down the centre of my chest. You have seen the lights through it. Those lights are the whole of what keeps the door a door — they are what the wall wants, the same as the cold man carries, the same make, the same dying glow. If you put one of Dorn's shots into that seam, into the lights, the door stops being a door. Permanently. There is no repairing it; I have repaired everything I am, a hundred times over, and I could not repair that. It would be the one wound I have ever taken that I could not stand up from."

"You're asking me to kill you."

"I am asking you to unmake a door. That the door is also me is the cruelty of it, and I will not pretend it is anything else, because you have earned better than pretending."

*  *  *

"How do I know," I said. My voice was doing something I did not give it permission to do. "How do I know when. You already told me you'll beg. You already made me promise not to believe the begging. Now you're telling me to — and the begging version is going to beg me not to, isn't it. It's going to look at me with your eye and beg me to put it down. How do I tell the difference. How do I know when there's no more you to save and it's only the door wearing you."

"Because the begging means I am still there." He said it gently, the way you'd explain a hard thing to a child you loved. "While I beg, hold me. The begging is the part of me that still wants you to win fighting the part of me that wants to lose. As long as I am begging you, in either direction, I am still a fight you can win, and you must not use the Boltcaster, no matter how it sounds, no matter what I promise or threaten — you hold." A pause. The lights in his chest guttered, the dim one nearly out. "You will know it is time when I go quiet. Not the begging-quiet. The other one. When I stop arguing with you, when I stop arguing with myself, when I go still and turn to the wall and reach for it and have nothing left to say to you at all — that silence is not me resting, Tasha. That silence is the door swinging open. That is the only moment. You will have a breath, perhaps two, between the silence and the world ending, and in that breath you put Dorn's shot through the seam and you do not hesitate, because the hesitation is the exact size of the gap the thing needs."

I looked down at the Boltcaster in my hands. The whole stupid weight of it. The dead man in it. The friend who'd carried it unloaded and unfired across more road than I could hold in my head, because using it was unthinkable, handing it to me so that I could fire it into him.

"Why me," I said. "Roan would— no. Don't say it kindly. Say it true. Why is this mine."

*  *  *

"Because Roan would do it too soon," Third said. "He loves me as a boy loves the thing he's decided to be brave for, and that love would flinch at the begging and end me to spare me the struggle, and the struggle is the whole of the chance. And the cold man would do it too late, or not at all, because to him I am a mechanism and he would want to see if the mechanism could be made to work after all." The eye held mine. "You are the only one who will do it at exactly the right moment, which is to say the last possible one, which is to say only when there is truly nothing left — because you do not want to do it. Because you will hate every breath between the silence and the shot. I am not choosing the person most able to kill me, Tasha. I am choosing the person least willing. That unwillingness is the most precise instrument in the world. It is the only thing I trust to wait long enough, and still not wait too long."

He looked, for a moment, at the cold man coming, and then back at me, and he said the thing under the thing, the thing he had been walking toward saying since the slab field, since the geysers, since the inn:

"I am afraid I am going to want it. The opening. The price. I have been alone inside this make of mine for three hundred thousand spins and it is going to offer me everyone, all at once, warm, and I am afraid — I, who am not built to be afraid of much — that I am going to want it more than I have ever wanted anything, more than I will want the world. So I am giving you the thing that means my wanting it will not be enough. I am putting the size of my own longing into your hands and I am asking you to be bigger than it. That is the whole of what I have ever asked anyone. I am sorry it is so large."

I did not say *I can't.* I had already used my no, and he had thanked me for it, and we both understood it had been the last one I was allowed.

I said, "Then you'd better beg loud, you old fool. Because I am going to need to hear you in there right up until the last breath, and if you go quiet on me one second before you have to, I will never forgive you, in this world or whatever's left of it."

"I will be as loud as I am able," Third said. "It is a promise. It may be the last one I am able to keep as myself."

And behind him, at the foot of the spine, where the pale grey stood up out of the ground against the pale dead stone, the cold man stopped walking.

We were out of breaths. He was here.

*  *  *


# Thirty Five

Up close the cold of him was a real thing, a weather. It came off him the way heat comes off a stove, except backwards, except wrong — a steady draught of cave-air that found the sweat on you and made you regret having a body at all. Roan, three paces to my left with his spear low, flinched at it before he caught himself. I didn't catch myself in time and shivered like a wet dog. Third did not feel it, or did not show it, which by then was the same thing.

I had braced, I think, for a monster. For something that looked like what it did. But the cold man just looked old — old past any use the word usually gets put to, old the way the cliff was old, a person who had been a person so long ago that the being-a-person had worn off him like paint off a fence. His face was all still there, eyes and mouth and the rest, and there was nothing behind any of it but patience. That was the worst part. Not menace. Patience. The patience of a thing that has already won and is only waiting for the rest of you to notice. And riding his stooped back, close enough now that I could see them clear, the six lights: bottled lightning long dead in their jars, the same blue-white as the two in Third's chest, except his were guttering and these six only sat there, steady and spent and somehow worse for it, like eyes that have stopped expecting anything.

He stopped a few paces from Third, and the two of them stood there, and I understood — watching the way they held themselves, both of them somehow a half-step out of the world, both leaning on a wind no one else could feel — that I was looking at two of a kind. Not the same. But kin, the way a key and a lock are kin: made to fit, made by the same hand for the same wound.

*  *  *

"You are awake," the cold man said.

His voice was nothing like Third's. Where Third was an avalanche, low and felt in the bones, this was the thin dry whisper of air pulled through a long-dead place, a sound with no warmth in it to lose. He said it the way you'd say a thing you had wanted for so long that getting it had stopped being a joy and become only a fact. There was no triumph in it. There was something far worse — something almost like tenderness.

"I felt them make you," he went on, when Third said nothing. "All of you. A long time ago, when the hand that made you struck you out — I felt each blow of it, here—" and he did not point to his chest, he pointed down, at the rock, at the cliff, at whatever was under it "—because I am fixed to the thing they struck you against. I have felt every one of your kind woken since, the way you feel a door open somewhere in a house you cannot leave. Most never woke. The ones that did were too far, or too broken, or died too soon." The patient eyes rested on Third. "I have waited a very long time for one of you to wake near enough, and whole enough, and last long enough to walk to me. And here you are. Do you know what you are?"

"I am Third," said Third. The avalanche held. "I know what I have made myself."

"That is not the question I asked." Not unkind. A teacher correcting a beloved pupil. "I asked if you know what you *are.* You do not. You have wondered — I can see that you have wondered, the whole long walk of your life. Why you can step beside the world when no other made thing can. Why the grey lets you pass and pulls at you both at once. Why, of all the things the Titans struck out, *you* woke." The cold deepened, or seemed to. "I will tell you, because I want you to understand what you are giving, when you give it. There is no joy in taking a thing from someone who does not know its worth."

I had the Boltcaster under my cloak, loaded, Dorn's whole grave of a weight against my ribs, and my hand on Third's forearm, and my voice already low in my throat saying the small steadying nothings I'd sworn to say — *here, I'm here, you're Third, I've got you* — and I felt Third lean, the smallest degree, toward the cold man's voice. Not toward the lure. There was no lure yet. Toward the *answer.* After three hundred thousand spins, somebody was finally going to tell him why.

*  *  *

"Under us," the cold man said, "is the Anvil. It is not a stone. It is the thing the Titans set everything else against when they were striking the world into shape — the surface they beat creation on. It is in the grey, and it is larger than this cliff, larger than the country, and it cannot be moved; I have had an age to try, and I am fixed to it, and one does not move the thing one is nailed to." For the first time something that was not patience crossed him — a very old, very tired frustration, worn down to almost nothing. "Everything you have felt these last spins, the pulling, the grey running one way like water down a drain — that is the Anvil. That is me, drawing the world toward the place the world began, because that is the only thing a fixed thing can do. Pull. I have pulled for longer than your kind has had names." The eyes came up. "But pulling is not opening. And I need it *opened.*"

"Then open it," Third said. "You walk in the grey. I have watched you do it. Step across, here, where it is thin, and reach your Anvil, and leave us be."

"I can go to it." A sound came out of him that might once, very long ago, have been a laugh. "I have gone to it more times than there are days in your life. But I am a passenger, made thing. I cross the wall; I do not breach it. I can carry one body sideways into the grey — myself, a thing I collect, no more — the way a man swims a river. The river is not opened by his crossing; it closes behind him as he goes. What is wanted is not a swimmer. What is wanted is a *door* — the wall held wide, and held, long enough for what is on the far side to come *through,* into here, into Anka, in its own vastness and not one body at a time." The patient eyes, the terrible tenderness. "A wall is not opened from without. It is opened from within, by the part of it that is alive. You are the only living part of the wall, made thing. They forged you against the Anvil; the making wove you into the seam of the two worlds; and then the last of them, dying, woke you with its own essence — the very essence that now wants through. You are not a soldier who happens to walk in the grey. You are the grey, and you are Anka, in one skin, with one will. You are the only seam in all the world that can be asked to part. That is what you are. That is what I have waited for. I did not make you, and I do not know your name, and neither of those things has ever mattered. I only ever needed one of you to be *alive,* and *here,* and able to say yes."

*  *  *

The silence after that had weight, like the air before a stony.

I felt the whole of it land on Third — felt it through my hand on his arm, the way you feel a tree take an axe-blow through the ground. Everything he had ever wondered, answered, in a dead man's whisper, at the end of the world: not a fluke, not a mercy, not a question the Titans forgot to answer. A purpose. He had spent his entire long life believing he had *escaped* his making — refused the glaive, refused the killing, made himself into something his makers never intended — and here was a thing older than his makers telling him, gently, that the escape itself had been the design. That he had been woken to be a door, and everything he'd become since, every kindness, every refusal, every companion buried, was just the door learning to want things, so that one day it could be offered the one thing that would make it open.

I want to tell you he answered like a hero. He didn't. He went very still, and the lights in his chest stuttered, and for a moment — one moment, the worst of my life to that point — I thought I had already lost him, that the silence I'd been warned to watch for had come this early, before the cold man had even named a price.

"Third," I said. Out loud. Not a steadying nothing. His name, hard, the way you'd throw a rope. "You're Third. You made that name yourself; nobody struck it into you. Say it."

A long breath of bellows, ragged, the first he'd spent in days.

"I am Third," he said. To me. Only to me. "I made myself. Whatever I was struck for." The eye came back to the cold man, and the avalanche came back into the voice, and I could have wept. "You have told me what I am. You are mistaken about one thing only. A door can be a door, and still choose to stay shut. That is also a thing a living seam can do."

The cold man did not look troubled. He looked, if anything, fonder.

"Yes," he said. "It can. That is why I have not come to threaten you, or to break you, neither of which would serve. I have come to *offer* you something. You will want to hear it. Everyone, in the end, wants to hear it." He tilted his worn face, and the six dead lights shivered on his back, and the grey stood up off the ground behind him like a held breath. "Shall I tell you what is waiting, on the other side of the wall you are?"

And Scar help me, I felt Third want to say yes.

*  *  *


# Thirty Six

He did not describe it. I think that is what I had braced for — a speech, a temptation laid out in words I could hear and hate and argue with. Instead the cold man simply opened his hand, palm up, toward the wall of standing grey behind him, and the grey thinned where he gestured the way frost thins on a window when you put your thumb to it, and Third made a sound.

I have heard Third make a great many sounds. The bellows-wheeze, the avalanche, the dry not-laugh. I had never heard him make this one. It was small, and it came from somewhere the bellows didn't reach, and it was the sound a person makes when they see something they had decided, for the sake of going on living, to stop letting themselves want.

I couldn't see it. That is the thing I have to keep telling people, the thing that makes them look at me like I failed him: I could not see into the grey. Whatever opened in that thinning, whatever stood in it warm and reaching, it was shown to Third and not to me. I fought the whole rest of it blind. I had a hand on his arm and his name in my mouth and a dead man's Boltcaster against my ribs, and I was holding him against a thing I could not see, by the one thread I had, which was that he had asked me to.

"There," said the cold man, soft as falling dust. "You know her."

*  *  *

"Liwon," Third said. Not to me. Not to the cold man. To the thinning in the air, to whatever stood in it. The avalanche had gone all to gravel. "Liwon."

"As she was," the cold man said. "Before the sundering took her. Whole. Small, and afraid of the dark, and certain, the way the very young are certain, that you would never let anything happen to her. She has been kept exactly so. The grey does not let go of what it holds; it only waits to be asked for it back." A pause, weightless, terrible. "You remember what was said to you, the night she died. A seed must die, and be buried, before it can blossom into something beautiful. You thought that a cruelty. It was a promise. This is the blossoming. The seed was planted three hundred thousand spins ago and I am offering you the flower. All of it. Her, and the swordsman with the squat Boltcaster, and the woman the illness took, and the one who taught you to want quiet, and — most lately, still warm, barely cold — the one who taught you to make your losses into stories. Mathen. All of them. Kept. Returned. The moment you open."

I felt Third lean. Not his body. The thing inside the body, the way it had leaned crossing the field, except the field had been a breeze against this. He leaned the way a drowning man leans toward air.

"You can't," I said — to him, low, the rope. "You're Third. You're here. I've got you. Whatever it's showing you, I've got you."

"It is Liwon, Tasha." He said it like a man explaining why he has to let go of the ledge. "She is holding up her arms. She does that — she did that — when she was frightened and wanted to be carried. She is doing it now. She is asking me to carry her. I have not been asked to carry anything in a very long time."

*  *  *

I want to be honest about how close it was, because the people who weren't there tell it like it was never really in doubt, like Third was always going to refuse, and that is an insult to what it cost him. It was in doubt. It was in doubt for longer than I will ever admit to anyone but this page. He stood at the foot of the spine with his dead daughter holding up her arms in a place I couldn't see, and every part of three hundred thousand spins of being alone reached back toward her, and the lights in his chest guttered low and lower, and his hand under mine had gone slack, and I talked, and talked, and felt the words going into him and falling through, like dropping stones down a well too deep to hear them land.

"Ask me what you are paying," Third said suddenly, to the cold man, and I heard him reaching — reaching for something, a handhold, a counter. "You always tell them the prize. You are too patient to tell them the cost. What is the cost."

"The world," the cold man said, without the smallest hesitation, and that was somehow worse than a lie would have been. "You know this. Everyone, everywhere, all that lives. I will not insult you by softening it. That is the price, and I am telling you plainly, because you are the first of your kind worth telling plainly, and because—" the worn face did something that might once have been gentleness "—because it does not matter. You will reach for her anyway. They always reach. I have watched a great many smaller souls than yours weigh the whole living world against one held face, and the world has never once been heavy enough. It is not a flaw in you. It is the shape of love. I am not your enemy, made thing. I am only the one who understands what you are for."
