"Tell me your name," Third said — and I understood, dimly, that he was doing something, that asking was a way of staying, of keeping his feet, of making the thing in front of him a someone he could refuse rather than a force he could only fall into.

The cold man considered. "Sougad," he said. "It is the one thing I will give you for nothing. Everything else in the world has a price now; I have seen to it. But a name is cheap, and you have been honest with me, and I find—" the dust-dry voice paused over the unfamiliar shape of it "—I find I would like one person, before the end, to have known me well enough to use it. Sougad. There. Now refuse a thing with a name, made thing, if you can. It is harder. I know. I have a name too, and it is why I am still fixed to that Anvil and have not let myself fall into the grey and end, all these ages. A name is a small hook, but it holds."

*  *  *

"Liwon was not a seed."

Third said it slowly, and I felt the first thing in a long time that was not slack — a tension coming back into the arm under my hand, a man finding, in the dark, the edge of something solid.

"You say it as if it were a kindness. The seed, the blossom. But she was not a seed. A seed is a thing that is for something else, that has not happened yet, that is only worth what it becomes. Liwon was not for anything. She was small, and afraid of the dark, and entirely herself, already, finished, real. She was not waiting to become beautiful. She was beautiful, and then she was killed, and the killing was real, and the losing was real, and they were the last true things left of her." The gravel was firming back toward stone. "If I take her back as your flower, I agree with you that she was only ever a seed. I let you make her into raw material for a thing that comes later. I unmake the one true thing I have — that she lived, and was hers, and not yours, and not the world's, and not a price. A woman in the snow taught me that, once. I did not understand it until just now, with my daughter holding up her arms."

And for a moment — one bright unbearable moment — I thought that was it. I thought he'd done it. I felt him straighten, felt the armour hold, felt the rope go slack in my hand because he didn't need it, he was standing on his own—

And then Sougad, gently, did the cruelest thing in this whole cruel book. He did not argue.

"I know," he said. "You are entirely right. She was herself, and the loss was true, and taking her back dishonours all of it. I am not going to pretend otherwise; you have earned better." The grey thinned a little further. "Knowing that changes nothing. You are going to reach for her anyway, because being right has never once stopped anyone from being lonely. I told you. It does not matter if she is real. It does not matter if it is true. I do not need you to believe me. I need you to *want* her. And you do. You are right, and you want her, and the wanting is the door, and the door is opening, and I am sorry — I find, to my surprise, that I am actually sorry — but I have waited so very long."

And Third stopped talking.

Not the begging-quiet. I knew the difference; he'd carved the difference into me two nights gone. This was the other one. This was the going-still. His hand slid out from under mine. His head turned, slow, toward the wall of grey, toward the small reaching shape only he could see, and he took one step toward it, and he had nothing left to say to me at all.

I had a breath. Perhaps two.

And my hand, Scar forgive me, would not go to the Boltcaster. It went tighter around his empty arm instead, because he was my friend and he was walking away from me into the arms of his dead and the thing I had sworn to do in this exact moment was the one thing my whole body refused to do.

"ROAN," I screamed. It was not the plan. It was the opposite of the plan. It was the only thing in me.

*  *  *


# Thirty Seven

Roan had been three paces back and to my right, where I'd put him, where I'd told him to wait for the worst and not before. He couldn't see into the grey any more than I could; he didn't know about the seed or the blossom or the door. All he had was what I'd given him at the foot of the spine — keep her on him, and when it goes wrong, cost it time. He'd been standing in the cold with his spear and his terror the whole while the cold man talked, waiting for a wrongness he could recognise.

My scream was the wrongness. He didn't ask what for. That, in the end, was the whole of Roan: he didn't ask what for.

He came past me in a lunge that knocked my shoulder half around and did the one thing none of us had planned because none of us had been able to say it aloud — he threw himself not at the cold man, not at the grey, but at *Third.* At his friend. At the wooden back already turning toward the wall, the hooves already lifting for that first long stride into the place where the dead were holding up their arms. Roan got one arm across the seam of Third's chest, over the guttering lights, and his other hand to his own wrist, and locked, and set his heels, and hauled — a boy trying to arm-wrestle a landslide back up its hill.

It did nothing, in the matter of strength. Third took the step anyway, dragging the both of them, Roan's heels furrowing the bare rock toward the standing grey.

So the cold man did the other thing. The thing that told me, finally and completely, what he was.

*  *  *

Sougad sighed. Actually sighed — the dust-dry ghost of a man put to a small tedious chore — and he said one word.

I did not know it. It was Titanspeech, the old inversion-tongue, the one the greybeards say carries no vowels of its own and borrows whatever ones you lend it, and though I could not read it I felt the shape of it go past me like a draught under a door: a word built backwards, *take* turned cleanly inside out, the old, old word for casting a thing forever out of your keeping. He did not point. He did not so much as lift a hand. He said the word, and the space between Roan and Third simply declined to go on holding him — and Roan was torn off Third's back as though the world had tilted under him alone, flung up and back and down, spear and limbs clattering across the bare stone, and he came to rest a dozen paces off in a heap that did not move.

He did not get up. I could not let myself look long enough to learn whether he *would* — whether the chest rose, whether there was breath left in him at all — because I had a door to hold and only the one pair of eyes to hold it with. I have had to live, since, with not knowing in that moment. It is its own small permanent wound.

And under the horror, cold and clear, I understood the thing that mattered: *he could have done that at any time.* To any of us. To Third. One word, no hand raised, and the strongest thing on that rock thrown down like a swatted fly. He had not done it to Third. He would never do it to Third — could not, dared not — because a door flung and broken is no door at all, because the single lesson of all his ages fixed to that Anvil was that the seam cannot be forced, only *asked.* He would unmake a guardsman with a breath and not so much as raise his voice at the made thing he needed. Everything true about him was right there, in the gap between what he was willing to break and what he was not.

*  *  *

And Third saw it.

That is the whole of it. That is what Sougad, in all his patience, in all his ages, had never once had to reckon with — because he had only ever watched souls reach toward what they had lost, and had never watched one get *held onto* by what it still had, and so he did not understand, even now, what he had just done.

One hoof in the grey. His dead daughter an arm's reach into a place I would never see, holding up her arms. Every part of three hundred thousand spins reaching for the warm. And Third turned his head — the smallest degree, a thing that should not have been possible against that pull — and he *saw the boy.* Saw Roan thrown down and unmoving on the cold rock for the single crime of having tried to keep him.

The dead were asking to be carried. And here, flung bleeding on the stone, was the price of the carrying — counted out in front of him, in a living boy, by the very hand that wanted him to say yes. Sougad, who needed Third willing, had reached for the one tool he understood and shown Third precisely what his yes was worth.

"That's it," I said, and I do not know where the steadiness came from; I think it came from Roan. "That's your yes, Third. That. On the rock. He came because you tend the people — Mathen's whole law, the only thing he had to leave us — you tend the people, and Roan tended *you,* and that is what your yes does to the ones who tend you. Walk into that wall. Go on. And he doesn't let go of you in his heart even now, so the grey takes him too, and you make another one. You make another Liwon. You make *him* one of the little arms reaching, forever. Is that the carrying you missed? Is it?"

*  *  *

And Third — one hoof lifted, the grey an arm's reach, the dead reaching, the boy bleeding — stopped.

He did not turn around. He was not back; the silence still held the most of him, and I could see the wall's pull on him like a current on a wading man. But the lifted hoof did not complete its step into the grey. It came down. On the lived side. On the cold honest rock.

And into the stolen breath where that step should have finished, Third said the first word he had spoken since the going-still closed over him — not to Sougad, not to the dead, not even to me, but low, over his shoulder, toward the heap of a boy a dozen paces off on the stone:

"...Roan."

It was not the refusal. The refusal was still to come, and it would cost him the worst thing he ever paid for anything, and I would have to watch him pay it. But it was the first crack of light under a door I had given up for shut, and I put my shoulder to that crack and shoved with everything I had left in me.

*  *  *


# Thirty Eight

I have been asked to describe what happened next more times than I have been asked anything else in my life, and I have never once done it well, because the truest answer is the one nobody wants: I don't know. I couldn't see it. The most important thing that has ever happened in the world happened a half-step sideways from me, in a place my eyes were not made to go, and all I had was the back of a wooden man and the things he said out loud and the slow terrible arithmetic of watching someone decide.

So this is not what happened. This is what it looked like, from the rock, from the wrong side of the wall, holding a rope I couldn't see the far end of.

It looked like a man saying goodbye to his daughter.

*  *  *

"Liwon," he said. The hoof was down. The other had not lifted again. But he had not turned from the grey either, and his voice when it came was not the avalanche and not the gravel — it was small, and careful, the voice you'd use on a child you were not going to be able to keep from crying. "Liwon. Look at me. No — keep your arms down a moment. I know. I know you're frightened. I know it's dark in there. Listen to me."

I could not hear an answer. There was no answer to hear; she was in the place I couldn't go. But I watched Third listen to one, watched his head tilt the exact degree it must have tilted over a sling three hundred thousand spins ago, and I have never in my life felt so much like an intruder on something holy and so unable to leave.

"I cannot come and get you," Third said. "I know I said I would always come. I know. I have thought about that promise every day you have been gone, and I am breaking it now, on purpose, with my eyes open, and I need you to hear me say that it is a promise I am choosing to break and not one I forgot." A pause. The lights in his chest had stopped guttering; they had gone very steady, very low, the way a flame goes steady right before. "Because the you in there is not the last true thing about you. The last true thing about you is that you were *mine,* and you were *real,* and you were taken, and it was wrong, and it has stayed wrong every day since, and that wrongness is the only honest grave you have. If I take the warm copy this thing is holding up, I have to agree with it that you were only ever a seed for it to grow me a flower from. And you were not a seed, Liwon. You were a girl. You were my girl. And I would rather keep the empty place where you were, true, forever, than fill it with something that calls your whole short real life a *down payment.*"

*  *  *

I felt it through the rope before I understood it. The arm under my hand — slack since the going-still took him — came back. Not pulling toward the wall. Not pulling anywhere. Just *his* again, weight in it, a man inhabiting his own limb.

"You taught me this," Third said, and I knew, this time, it was not to Liwon, and not to me; it was to a woman who had died old in a bed a long way from here, who had lost everything and gone on anyway. "You said going on was the only way to call the loss what it was. I did not understand you. I thought you meant *endure.* You meant *refuse.* You meant that to take the bargain is to let them tell you it was never really a loss at all — and that the loss is the last of the love you get to keep. I am keeping it. I am keeping all of it. Thank you. I am sorry it took me until the end of the world."

And then, to the grey, to the small reaching shape in it, the worst sentence I have ever heard a person say, said gently, said like tucking someone in:

"Goodbye, Liwon. I love you. Stay where it's warm. I am not coming."

*  *  *

I will tell you what I saw, and you can believe what you like about what it means.

I saw the standing wall of grey go *out* — not torn, not slammed, nothing violent. It went out the way a held breath goes out. It had stood up off the ground like something about to speak and now it simply sat back down into the rock and the troughs and the ordinary low places, thinning, paling, becoming again the faint wrong fog I'd watched all the way across the field, and then less than that, and then gone, and the bare honest rock of the world lay there under the Scar's grey light with nothing standing up out of it at all.

And I felt Third lose her.

I had braced for triumph. There wasn't any. What came through the rope, through the arm under my hand, was the thing I had no name for until I'd felt it — the second losing. He had buried her once, a child, three hundred thousand spins ago, with no choice in it. Now he had been handed her back, whole, warm, asking to be carried, and he had given her up *again,* with every choice in the world, and chosen the empty place on purpose. The first time the world took her from him. This time he handed her back to the dark with his own two-fingered hands, so that the world could keep being a world.

He made a sound then. Once. I won't try to spell it. It was not the bellows and it was not a word. It was the sound the make of him made around a grief it had chosen, and I hope I never hear anything like it again, and I will hear it for the rest of my life.

*  *  *

Sougad did not rage.

I think, if he had raged, I could have hated him cleanly, and it would have been easier. Instead the worn old face simply... settled, the way water settles when you stop disturbing it, back into the patience it had worn coming across the field — except the patience had nothing left to be patient *toward,* now, and so it was just a face, very old, looking at a wall that had closed and a door that had chosen, with the six dead lights guttering on his stooped back.

"You understand what you have done," he said. Not a threat. Barely a question. "I cannot be killed. Not by you, not by anything that lives on this side. I will go back to the Anvil, because there is nowhere else a thing like me can go, and I will be fixed to it as I have been fixed to it since before your makers had names, and I will pull, because that is the only thing a fixed thing can do. And one day, made thing — long after the girl beside you is dust, long after the boy on the rock is a name no one says — another door will wake. They always do. I am very patient. I have just been taught how patient I am going to have to be."

"Yes," Third said. He had turned, finally, away from the place she'd been. The single light of his eye found the cold man, and there was no triumph in it either, only a kind of terrible kinship. "You will wait. And I will spend whatever I have left making certain the next one who wakes is found by someone who loves them, before it is found by you. That is the whole of the war, I think. That is the only war there ever was. You have patience. I will leave behind people."

Sougad looked at him a long moment. Then he stepped sideways, the half-step out of the world that I had watched Third make a dozen times, and the cold went with him, all at once, like a door closing on the cold — and the rock where he'd stood was only rock, and the Scar wheeled its slow light over a world that had, against everything, not ended.

I became aware that I was still holding Third's arm. That my other hand had, at some point I never felt, finally found the Boltcaster under my cloak, and was gripping it white-knuckled, and had never drawn it.

And a dozen paces off, on the cold stone, the heap that was Roan made a small, wet, outraged groan — the single most beautiful sound in the entire language of the living — and tried, and failed, and tried again, to sit up.

*  *  *


# Thirty Nine

The world did not feel saved. I want to put that down plainly, because the stories will say we saved it and the word will do what words do, it will round everything off smooth, and there was nothing smooth about that rock in the grey light with the cold man gone and the three of us still on it, breathing, which is a different and much smaller thing than *saved.* The world felt the way a room feels after someone has been very ill in it and the fever has finally broken: not triumphant. Just quiet, and wrung out, and aware of how close the other thing had come.

I went to Roan first, because Roan was the one making noise, and noise meant alive.

He'd got himself half-propped on one elbow by the time I reached him, and he was swearing — a low steady creative stream of it, the kind you only manage when the hurt is real but not the worst kind, the kind that's furious to still be here rather than grateful — and when he saw my face he stopped and tried to rearrange his own into something braver, and failed, and I loved him for the failing.

"Don't move," I said, getting my hands on him. "Let me see."

"It threw me," he said, as if I hadn't watched. "It didn't even — Tasha, it didn't even *touch* me. It said a *word.*" His outrage at this was total and somehow the most healing thing in the world. A man does not waste breath being indignant about the rules of his injury unless he intends to live to complain about it properly later.

His leg was wrong below the knee — not bone-through-skin wrong, but wrong, already swelling, the kind of wrong that would make the walk out a long ugly business. Two of his ribs grated under my hands in a way that made us both hiss. There was blood drying at the corner of his mouth and a deeper greyish cast to his skin that I didn't like and didn't have a name for, the toll of having been flung so near a wall he was never meant to touch. But his eyes tracked. His swearing scanned. His hand, when I gripped it, gripped back hard enough to grind my knuckles.

"You're going to be unbearable about this," I told him, which was the truest comfort I had. "You threw yourself at a Forged and got cast off by a magic word and lived. You'll dine out on it for the rest of your life."

"Mathen," Roan said, and his voice cracked clean in half on it, "would've gotten a *tenday* of drinks."

"A hundred spins," I said. "Easy."

And we both looked away from each other for a moment, because that was as close as either of us could go to him just then, and it was enough, and it was the first time we'd been able to go even that close since the Grask.

*  *  *

Third had not moved.

He stood where he'd turned to face Sougad, a little apart, and he was looking at the place the grey had been — the ordinary rock, the ordinary low troughs, nothing standing up out of any of it — and I knew, the way I'd learned to know him, that he was not looking at the rock. He was looking at where she had been. At the empty place he had chosen to keep empty. The lights in his chest had gone back to their small steady burning, the dim one no worse than before, no better; the make of him was intact; he had taken no wound that he could repair or that anyone could see.

He had taken the other kind.

I left Roan propped and bound as well as I could manage and went and stood beside Third, not touching him, just there, the way he'd stood beside me a hundred times in the dark and let his being-there be the whole of what he offered. For a long while he said nothing. The Scar wheeled. Somewhere far off across the field a slab went over with a crash, because the place was still the place; the world had gone back to being only ordinarily terrible.

"I had her," he said at last. Not to me. To the empty rock. "For the length of a goodbye, I had her back, exactly as she was, and she was holding up her arms. And I gave her to the dark with my own hands so that he—" the eye moved, a fraction, toward Roan "—could keep being a boy who complains about magic words. I want you to understand that I do not feel I did a great thing, Tasha. I feel I did a murder. I murdered the chance to have my daughter, and I did it on purpose, and the only thing I can say for myself is that I would do it again, which is not the same as being glad, and I am so tired of people thinking those are the same."

"I don't think they're the same," I said.

"No," he said. "You don't. It is why I could bear for you to be the one who saw it." A pause. "Thank you for not telling me it was worth it. Everyone is going to tell me it was worth it."

"It wasn't worth it," I said. "It was just necessary. Those aren't the same either."

The single light of his eye came round to me, and held, and something in the make of him eased a fraction — not healed, nothing was healed, but eased, the way a weight shifts to a part of you better made to carry it.

"No," he said softly. "They are not. You have gotten very good at the true things, Tasha. I am sorry it was my company that taught you."

*  *  *

He saw the Boltcaster then. It was still in my hand. I had not been able to make myself put it away, and I realised, looking down at it, that my fingers had cramped around it, that I had been holding it that hard for that long, and that the safety of it — the loaded, ready, never-drawn weight of it — was the only thing my body had let itself do with the thing it could not do.

"You did not use it," Third said.

"No."

"When I went quiet. When I went to the wall and reached. That was the moment. That was the only moment, the one I drilled into you, and you had it, and you did not use it." He said it without accusation, the way you'd note the weather. "You could not. Could you."

"No." It came out of me in a rush, the confession I'd been dreading, the failure under the relief. "I couldn't. I had it aimed in my head a hundred times those two nights and when it was real my hand just — it wouldn't. It went tighter on your arm instead. I broke the plan, Third. The one thing you built the whole of it on, the failsafe, the precise instrument, *only when there's truly nothing left* — and when there was truly nothing left I screamed for a hurt boy instead of doing the one thing you asked me for. If Roan hadn't— if Sougad hadn't spent him in front of you— the world ends, because I couldn't open my hand."

Third was quiet a moment. Then he did a thing I had never seen him do and never saw him do again: he reached out, slow, with that wrong boneless ease, and he closed his own wooden hand around mine, around the Boltcaster, gently, over the cramp, and held it.

"I chose you," he said, "because you were the one least willing to do it. I told you that. I thought I was choosing the most precise blade — the hand that would wait until the last possible breath because it hated the cutting. I was wrong about what I was choosing. I was not choosing a blade at all. I built a failsafe because I needed to believe there was a bottom under me before I could step out over the drop — and the failsafe I actually built, without knowing it, was a person whose hand would not open on me no matter what. Who would scream for help instead of for an ending. Tasha. The reason you could not use it is the same reason it worked. You did not let go of me. That was always the only thing that was going to hold. Not the threat of the shot. The fact of the grip. I am only sorry I made you carry the shot at all, and I am taking it back now, and you will never have to decide that again."

He took Dorn's Boltcaster out of my cramped hand, as gently as he'd given it, and somewhere in the taking it became a memento again instead of a weapon, and he put it away at his back, and that was the end of the failsafe, which had never once been the thing that saved us.

*  *  *

We did not stay long. You don't, in that country; the place had spared us its worst but it had not become safe, and a slab the size of a barn settling half a field off is a poor lullaby. There was the matter of Roan, who could not walk, and the matter of how far we were from anywhere that wasn't trying to kill us, and the matter of the long impossible distance between a cliff at the cold edge of the world and any roof at all.

Third lifted Roan as easily as he'd once lifted an infant, one arm, settled, and Roan made a noise of protest that turned into a noise of pain that turned into the quiet of a man saving his strength, and we turned our backs on the spine.

I looked back once. The glaive was still standing where Third had leaned it, upright against the pale stone, the wide pale blade he'd carried since before he was anyone.

"You're leaving it," I said.

"It was never mine," said Third, with the boy on his arm. "Let it stand there. Let the next fool who climbs all this way looking for treasure find a door-warden's glaive and no door, and no warden, and let them make of it whatever story keeps them warm on the walk home. Mathen would like that, I think. A thing left behind on purpose, to be wondered at. He always said the wondering was the point."

And we walked out into the open book, the three of us, one of us carried and one of us carrying and one of us holding a rope that had no far end anymore and not yet knowing how to put it down — out across the crawling field toward a horizon that was a very long way away, with no notion in the world of how we were going to cross it, and behind us a glaive stood guard over nothing at all, exactly as it had been made to do, and was, at last, free of us.

# Forty

The water was the first thing it had ever stopped for that was not a threat.

It did not have a word for what it was doing. It was crouched at the edge of a brook in a blue-green wood, and the water ran over stones and made a sound, and the sound was not the sound of anything coming to kill it or anything it had been made to kill, and so the thoughts in its head had nothing to say about the water and were, for a moment, only quiet. It put two fingers into the cold of it. It watched the running thing break around its fingers and close again downstream, unbothered, as though the hand had never been there. It did not know why this was worth doing. It did it for a while anyway.

That was when the green one stood up out of the reeds with the bow already drawn.

The thoughts came back at once, fluent and calm, the way they always were. *A Virek. Armed. One step left takes the line of the arrow; one swing of the glaive takes the bow and the arm that holds it in the same arc. It is small. It will be slow.* It had heard this kind of thing its whole short existence and had never once had cause to doubt it. Its body began, very slightly, to do what the thoughts described. The weight came onto the left hoof.

Then it looked at the bow.

It did not mean to. The thoughts had not asked it to. There was no use in looking at the bow — the bow was a problem already solved, a thing to be broken on the way to the thing that held it. But it looked, and what it saw stopped the weight where it was.

The bow had been carved down its whole length. The carving spiralled out from the grip in both directions, fine past following, as though the wood were not cut but coaxed, as though someone had spent a great many days persuading it to become this. There was red worked deep into the bindings, new and bright, not yet gone to the dull of age. It was not a parody of anything. The glaive in its own hands was a parody — a Virek shape, a Virek weapon, made by a Titan as a joke against the Virek, made for the one purpose of ending them and nothing else, and it had never once thought of the glaive as anything but the most efficient version of itself. But the bow was not a joke and not efficient. The bow was a thing a frightened young person had made slowly, with their whole care, knowing it would mostly hang on a wall, and had carved anyway, every length of it, where no enemy would ever look closely enough to see.

Something happened that it had no name for, because it had never happened before.

The thoughts stopped.

Not quieted. Not argued down — it did not yet know it could argue them down; that would come later, with practice, the way the fire would come, the way the warmth would. They simply stopped, the way a voice stops in a room when something walks in that it does not know how to speak about. For the length of a held breath there was nothing in its head telling it where the line of the arrow was, or how the swing should fall, or which part of the small green thing would come apart first. There was only the bow, and the care in the bow, and the strange new pressure of finding a made thing beautiful and not knowing what to do with the finding.

Into that silence — the first silence it had ever been given — the green one spoke.

"What are you doing?"

And it heard its own voice answer, a sound like a slope giving way, a sound that startled the thing that made it:

"I... do not know."

*I.* The word came up out of the new silence as though it had been waiting under the floor of every order it had ever followed. It turned the word over. It did not break against anything. There was room for it now, in the quiet the bow had made, and once there was room the word simply walked in and stood there, and the thoughts did not come back to push it out.

"What am I?" it asked the green one, and meant it, and the asking was the second thing it had ever done that no one had told it to do.

The green one's arms were shaking. The bow stayed up, but the young face above it had stopped being only afraid and started being something else, something that leaned forward despite itself.

"Blank," the green one said. "You're what we call a Blank."

It looked at the bow a moment longer — the spiral, the red, the whole patient length of it — and then it opened its hands and let the glaive fall forward into the reeds, because the glaive was the efficient version of itself and it had just discovered, in the silence a stranger's bow had made, that it did not want to be the efficient version of itself anymore.

It did not know what it wanted to be instead. It would spend the whole length of the world finding out. But it knew, crouched there with the water closing over the place its hand had been, that the wanting itself was new, and was its own, and had come in through a door a frightened child had carved without meaning to, every length of it, where no enemy would ever look.

And then it bent and took the glaive back up out of the reeds. Not because the wanting had changed — but the world was full of things that had made no discoveries that day, and an unarmed thing alone in it would not live long enough to find out what it wanted to be instead. The wet wood was the same weight it had always been and sat in its hands the same way, and nothing about it had changed except that it was now a thing being carried, and no longer a thing being been. It would carry it for the whole length of the world. It would set it down at last on a hill at the edge of everything, against a pale stone, and walk away and not look back. The setting down began here, in the reeds, in the silence a bow had made. It simply took three hundred thousand spins to finish. Behind it, as it turned downstream, it heard the green one say, very low, to no one:

"A Blank just used 'I'."

It did not look back. It had not yet learned that there were things worth looking back at. That, too, would come.

# Forty One

The stories will tell you we walked out of the Giant's Graveyard. They will make it sound like a single motion, a thing with a shape — *and then they walked out* — the way stories tidy a distance into a sentence. It was not a sentence. It was most of a day and into the dark and most of another, across a field that had gone back to merely trying to kill us in the ordinary ways, with Roan on Third's arm making the small involuntary sounds of a man whose ribs disagreed with being carried and being still equally, and the cliff at our backs never seeming to get any further away no matter how long we put it behind us. That is what walking out is. It is the part the stories cut.

Third said almost nothing the whole of it. I had learned to read him by then, well enough to know he was not sulking and not broken past speech — he was a long way back, somewhere behind his own eye, somewhere I'd been given the shape of but not the rooms. Once, when the Scar came round and the light caught the side of his ruined patient face, I saw the coal of his eye go soft in a way I had only ever seen it go on the watch, the way it went when he was off a little beside the world with someone he'd lost — except he wasn't beside the world now. He was just walking, and remembering, the ordinary way the rest of us do it. I let him. You learn, eventually, which silences are yours to fill and which are a person keeping company with their own dead, and the second kind you leave alone and match your steps to, and that is most of what love turns out to be when you strip the songs off it.

We came over a long low rise of broken pages on the second evening, and there it was, sitting on the air the way a fat gull sits on water, fans going *clatter-clatter-clatter* against the dusk.

The Porter.

I knew it for a skybarge before I knew anything else about it — everyone knows the clatter, the way you know rain coming — but I had never seen one close, and I had certainly never seen one that had a *porch*. Because it did. Lanterns strung along a rail. A board by the ramp with the day's pot chalked on it. A barge built like a building, like somebody's stubborn warm idea of an inn that had simply declined to stay on the ground with the others. Down both flanks of the hull, set in cradles of dark banded hexwood, ran a row of bottled magefire — fat vessels of that caged blue-white light, the same as lived in Third's chest but the size of ale-kegs, throwing a cold glow down onto the rock and holding the whole impossible weight of the place up on nothing I could see. At the stern a single great push-fan stood as wide as a mill-wheel, and as I watched it slowed, and slowed, and came round to a halt — the barge easing to a stop at the rim of the rise and coming no closer, hung there on its cold lights with the lanterns swinging.

"It won't come over," Third said — the first full thing he'd said in a day. "The spine is still shedding — letting go of itself a page at a time, the way you've watched it do. No barge-master takes a hull in under falling rock, and Waj is too good a one to try. They hold the edge. No further." He shifted Roan higher on his arm. "We walk the last of it. As we walked in."

So we did. And a figure leaned over the rail as we came up the last stretch of stone, and cupped their hands, and called down in a voice built for being heard across a full common-room:

"We saw the devastation. In a line. Right across the bad country, like something had been *editing* it." A pause, and even at that distance I could hear the dry shape of the next part coming. "And we thought — well. We thought there was a fair chance you'd be involved." Another pause. "Again."

"Waj," Third said, in the tone he kept for the very few. "You came back to the rim."

"I keep a tab, ser Forged. You're on the wrong side of it, still, since the business with the Ravagers and my ramp." They were already cranking the ramp down, this Waj — a broad, unhurried person with an apron over travelling leathers and the particular calm of someone who has watched a great deal of trouble walk up to their door and decided, long ago, to simply feed most of it. "I don't forget who covered my retreat walking *backward*. I think you'll find we're square after a ride and a few hot meals. Don't tell me what happened back there. I run an inn. I'd rather not know what I'm ferrying, it puts me off the cooking."

Getting Roan up the ramp was its own small war, and Waj took his weight off Third at the top without being asked and without making it a moment, the way good innkeepers move you out of a doorway — and then we were aboard, on a floor that shifted, very slightly, underfoot, the whole warm strung-lantern length of it, the pot on the stove and the bedding rolled in the corners and the smell of somewhere that fed people for a living.

I stood on it and felt it move and I laughed. I couldn't help it. It came up out of me ugly and sudden and I had to put my hand over my mouth.

Because here is the thing the stories also leave out. I left my inn — a good inn, a still inn, an inn that did not go anywhere and never would — because I was bored, because I wanted a floor that took me somewhere, because there had to be more than wiping the same six tables under the same unmoving roof for the rest of a life. I had said almost those words, once, closing up alone. And now I was standing on a floor that moved, in an inn that came to *you*, that went wherever the trouble and the trade were — I had got, down to the grain of it, the exact thing I'd wanted — and I'd had to walk out of a place where the dead crawl, carrying what we were carrying, to get it. I laughed because the alternative had my hand over my mouth for a different reason, and I have never been one to let a room see which it was.

We rode through the dark. Waj fed us and asked nothing. Roan slept the sleep of the finally-safe-enough. And late, when the lanterns were down to embers and the fans had settled to a drone you stopped hearing, Third sat with his back to the warm flue and I sat near him, and I said, because somebody had to and it had been sitting in me the whole walk:

"Tell me the good part again. The tree story. I want to hear it on a floor that moves."

He was quiet a moment. Then the avalanche turned over, gentle, and he told it — the little one who would not sleep, the singing leaves, the bird, the swaying, the frightened thing learning it could be still. He told it better than he had at the fire. A story wears smooth where you hold it, he'd said once; this one had been held a great deal, lately, and it had gone smooth in the good way — the way the bow had, worn by being carried and not just kept.

And at the edge of the failing light, where the dark started past the last lantern, there was — I will say it plainly now, because I have stopped being afraid of the things I see — there was the suggestion of someone small, sitting with their knees drawn up, listening to a story they already knew and loved anyway. The lanternlight went a little way into them, the way it goes into smoke. I did not look away this time. I let them be there. And when the story ended they were gone, the gentle way, no worse than before, and Third set the last word down soft into the dark the way you'd set down a cup you didn't want to wake anyone with.

"He'll be back," Third said, to me, or to the dark, "for as long as it gets told. That is the whole of what he is, now. A thing that is here while it is told." The coal of his eye came round to me. "It is not nothing, Tasha. I spent a long time thinking it was nothing — a thing you could only *almost* keep was the same as a thing you'd lost. It is not the same. I gave up the chance to have my—" and there he stopped, the way he always stopped, and then, because we were on a floor that moved and the worst was behind us, he went on, quietly, the first time he ever said it to me whole: "—to have my daughter. At the cliff. He offered her back and I said no, because the price was the world, and saying no was the hardest thing I have ever done, and I would do it again, and it has left a hole in me the exact size and shape of her that I am going to carry for whatever is left of a very long life." The eye held steady. "But she is not *gone*. That is what the bow taught me, in your mouth, on the road. The boy is gone past all finding and his bow is on a wall being handsome and in someone's way. Unrecognisable is not the same as gone. She is in every story I will not stop telling, and in a green thing somewhere with her eyes that will never know my name, and in the hole itself, which is hers, which I would not trade for a full and easy chest. I lost her. I did not lose *her.* Do you see. I am only now beginning to."

I saw. I didn't say so, because some seeing is bigger than the saying of it, and he knew anyway.

So that is the end of it, or the end of the part I was there for. We rode out of the bad country on an inn that flew. Roan mended, mostly, and limps on the other leg now, which he says makes him symmetrical and which Mathen would have got a hundred spins of drinks out of, and we say so, often, because that is how you keep a man who liked drinks. Third went on, of course. There is always another peg, another patient thing, another door that might wake; and there is, he says, the matter of leaving behind people, which is the only argument he has found that beats *I am very patient*, and which he intends to keep making with his whole long life. He left the glaive on the hill. He kept the Boltcaster. He keeps the hole.

And me — I am telling you this. That is the part I have left to do, and I have come to think it is not a small one. Mathen said a story is how you get more than one person to carry the weight, so it doesn't all rest on the one. I didn't understand him then. I was wiping tables. I understand him now, on a floor that moves, with the lanterns down and someone small at the edge of them for as long as I keep the telling going: I am carrying. That is what this is. Every name I say keeps a door open the width of a finger and holds it there.

I followed a monster out of an inn because I was bored, and nothing was ever going to change, and that was the thing about quiet towns.

I was wrong about the quiet towns. I have never in my life been so glad to be wrong about anything.
